# The custody band

The programme map splits the 135 nodes closest to the alleged programme into seven bands, and this file is the custody band: 32 nodes, taken era by era from the earliest to the most recent. Each entry gives what the node is, why the board places it here, why it holds the tier it holds, what it has denied, its documented and testimonial claims with their sources, and the links that tie it to the rest of the board.

Names in full, for this file:

- **AARO**: All-domain Anomaly Resolution Office

# THROUGH 1969: Estimate through Blue Book

## Project Blue Book

Project Blue Book is a program on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1952 to 1969; axes C1 A2 D2; 27 connections; in the custody band.

In one line: Public-facing USAF UFO program (1952-1969).

### What it is

Project Blue Book was the United States Air Force's public-facing unidentified flying object investigation, headquartered at Wright-Patterson Air Force Base and running from March 1952 until Secretary of the Air Force Robert C. Seamans, Jr. announced its termination on 17 December 1969. It succeeded Projects SIGN and GRUDGE and logged 12,618 sightings, of which 701 were closed as "Unidentified." Its textual records were retired to the USAF Archives and subsequently to the National Archives, where they are open as 94 rolls of 35mm microfilm, publication T-1206, within Record Group 341.

### Why it is implicated

The Air Force's own termination paperwork states in plain language that Blue Book was not the channel through which serious UAP reporting flowed. The 20 October 1969 staff document signed by Brig. Gen. C. H. Bolender records that "reports of unidentified flying objects which could affect national security are made in accordance with JANAP 146 or Air Force Manual 55-11, and are not part of the Blue Book system," and in the same paragraph that "there is still, however, no evidence that Project Blue Book reports have served any intelligence function." That is a signed admission of a two-channel architecture: a public channel with a press office and a phone number, and a separate operational reporting channel whose corpus has never been inventoried, indexed or transferred to any archive. The same document shows the Air Force weighing where to store the Blue Book files specifically to manage researcher access, with the Secretary of the Air Force Office of Information agreeing to "rigidly limit the right of access" and to "screen researcher's notes." Blue Book therefore sits on this board twice: as the mechanism that absorbed and normalised public interest for seventeen years, and as a custody object whose public copy was deliberately shaped before release.

### The longer account

Project Blue Book is the most examined document set in the history of the subject and the least useful, and both facts follow from the same cause: it was never the file where the answer would be.

The Air Force ran three named UFO efforts in sequence. Project SIGN began in 1948, Project GRUDGE followed in 1949, and Blue Book was stood up in March 1952 under Air Force Regulation 80-17, which required base commanders to provide a UFO investigative capability and Air Force Systems Command to continue the project. The Bolender document of 20 October 1969 describes the resulting establishment with some precision: "Project Blue Book has two officers, one NCO and one civilian assigned on a full-time basis," plus Dr. J. Allen Hynek as a scientific consultant until 1 July 1969. Four people, one part-time academic, and a stated mission to determine whether UFOs threatened national security and whether they exhibited advanced technology. For seventeen years this was the entire visible federal apparatus.

The Bolender document is the load-bearing artifact. It was written as the staff package recommending termination, was released to researcher Robert Todd in January 1979, and reads as an internal cost-benefit memorandum rather than a scientific finding. Paragraph 4 contains the sentence that reorganises everything around it: after noting that the 1953 Robertson Panel had already concluded UFOs posed no direct physical threat, the document observes that "in spite of this finding, the Air Force continued to maintain a special reporting system," then states that national-security-relevant reports move under JANAP 146 or Air Force Manual 55-11 "and are not part of the Blue Book system." JANAP 146 is the joint communications instruction governing CIRVIS reporting of vital intelligence sightings; AFM 55-11 governs operational reporting. Neither corpus has ever been assembled, declassified or transferred as a body. Whatever came up those channels between 1952 and 1969 is not in T-1206 and never was.

The same document then explains, in operational terms, why Blue Book was kept alive after its own premise was disclaimed. Paragraph 8 recommends that if Blue Book is terminated the records "should be transferred to an appropriate archive and preserved for historical value and to prevent charges that the Air Force is concealing facts." The reason given for preservation is reputational management, stated openly. Paragraph 9 goes further. Because witnesses had been promised confidentiality, "access to these records and the publications must be carefully reviewed and controlled," and the Secretary of the Air Force Office of Information judged that "an Air Force controlled archive away from the Washington, D.C. area is the most appropriate storage facility," because ready availability in Washington "would result in an inordinate workload in clearing investigators, providing documentation, and reviewing and clearing manuscripts." Air University agreed to take the files on three conditions: that SAFOI answer all queries, that SAFOI "rigidly limit the right of access to recognized, legitimate researchers," and that SAFOI "screen researcher's notes to insure that 'confidentiality' is maintained." The Air Force decided, in writing, where to put the archive on the basis of how hard it would be for the public to reach.

The public announcement two months later, Department of Defense release No. 1077-69 of 17 December 1969, carries none of that. It quotes Seamans that continuation "cannot be justified either on the ground of national security or in the interest of science," cites the Condon report and the National Academy of Sciences review, and closes with the three conclusions that have been repeated ever since: no reported UFO ever indicated a threat to national security, no "unidentified" case represented technology beyond present-day scientific knowledge, and no "unidentified" case indicated extraterrestrial vehicles. Read against Bolender, the first conclusion is a statement about a filing system rather than about the sky, because the reports that could affect national security were routed elsewhere by regulation.

Custody is the second reason Blue Book is on this map. The National Archives holds approximately 2 cubic feet of project and administrative files, 37 cubic feet of case files arranged chronologically, and 3 cubic feet of records relating to the Air Force Office of Special Investigations, arranged by OSI district and overseas command. NARA states that the textual records are available "excluding names of people involved in the sightings." The public Blue Book is therefore a redacted copy of the Air Force's own Blue Book, and the confidentiality rationale from Bolender paragraph 9 is the reason. NARA also states flatly that it "has been unable to locate any documentation among the Project BLUE BOOK records that discuss the 1947 incident in Roswell, New Mexico." The General Accounting Office found the same when it reviewed Blue Book Special Report No. 14 at Archives II during its 1994-95 Roswell audit: the analysis of sightings from 1946 to 1953 "did not mention the crash or the recovery of an airborne object near Roswell in July 1947." The single most famous American recovery event does not appear in the official American UFO file. That is either evidence the event did not happen or evidence the file was not where such events were recorded, and Bolender tells you which architecture the Air Force actually built.

What the record does not show is equally specific. The Bolender document carries sixteen attachments listed on a separate page. Several are known and were released separately; several have never surfaced. There is no public inventory of JANAP 146 CIRVIS traffic. There is no public accounting of the OSI material beyond the three cubic feet NARA holds. And the January 1985 Wright-Patterson fact sheet that NARA reproduces contains the categorical sentence "There are not now nor ever have been, any extraterrestrial visitors or equipment on Wright-Patterson Air Force Base" - a dated, attributable, institutional denial about a specific installation, which is exactly the kind of statement this board catalogues rather than weighs.

### What it has denied

The Air Force's position has been fixed since 17 December 1969 and restated verbatim for decades: no reported UFO ever indicated a threat to national security, none represented technology beyond present-day scientific knowledge, and none indicated extraterrestrial vehicles (DoD release No. 1077-69). The January 1985 Wright-Patterson fact sheet adds the installation-specific denial that there are not now and never have been "any extraterrestrial visitors or equipment on Wright-Patterson Air Force Base." Both are dated, attributable and institutional, and both are silent on the JANAP 146 and AFM 55-11 channel the Air Force's own termination paperwork describes.

Roles the board assigns it: concealment, custody, oversight, disclosure.

### Open questions on this node

- No inventory, index or transfer of the JANAP 146 (CIRVIS) and AFM 55-11 UFO reporting corpus for 1952-1969 has ever been made public. Where does that traffic physically reside?
- Several of the sixteen attachments to the 20 October 1969 Bolender staff package have never been released. What is in Atch 8, cited for the claim that Blue Book served no intelligence function, and Atch 10, cited for the JANAP 146 routing?
- Does an unredacted Blue Book master copy containing witness names still exist in Air Force custody, and under what disposition authority?
- The exact date, transfer authority and accession record for the movement of the files from Wright-Patterson to the USAF Archives at Maxwell and then to NARA is not stated on NARA's own page.
- The text and successive amendments of AFR 200-2 (1953) and its provisions on public release of unidentified cases were not verified in primary form for this dossier and remain a research target.
- Whether AFOSI retained a parallel UFO case series beyond the three cubic feet at NARA, and whether any of it was destroyed.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 16 of 1125, score 113). 31 connections, 11 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 5 official denials recorded against it. Axes C1/A2/D2.

## The MITRE Corporation

The MITRE Corporation is a institution on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1958; axes C3 A3 D2; 26 connections; in the custody band.

In one line: Not-for-profit operator of six FFRDCs; dual headquarters McLean, VA and Bedford, MA; founded 21 July 1958.

### What it is

The MITRE Corporation is a private not-for-profit spun out of MIT Lincoln Laboratory in 1958 that operates exclusively as a manager of federally funded research and development centers. It runs six FFRDCs on behalf of the Department of Defense, the Federal Aviation Administration, Treasury and the IRS, the Department of Homeland Security, the Department of Health and Human Services, and NIST. It is headquartered in McLean, Virginia, and also provides the administrative home for JASON, the government's standing outside scientific advisory panel.

### Why it is implicated

MITRE is on this map because of the legal mechanism it embodies, not because a witness has named it as a custodian of material. An FFRDC is granted, by regulation, access to government and contractor data 'beyond that which is common to the normal contractual relationship' - including sensitive and proprietary data, installations, equipment and real property - and its sponsoring agreement gives the sponsor's Executive Agent approval authority over the classification, publication and distribution of its output. That combination produces a private organisation holding federal program records under a disclosure obligation that runs to a sponsoring agency rather than to Congress or to the public, and whose corporate files are not agency records for FOIA purposes. The DHS sponsoring agreement goes further: MITRE's own staff sign non-disclosure agreements that expressly preserve the right to report substantial violations of law to Congress, but subcontractors and consultants are required to sign the same NDA with that whistleblower carve-back removed. On 22 May 2026 Rep. Eric Burlison put this exact structure in writing, telling MITRE that FFRDCs 'cannot become private vaults for federal records' and demanding a search back to 1 January 1930.

### The longer account

MITRE occupies a category that exists nowhere else in the federal enterprise. It manufactures nothing, competes for nothing, and sells nothing. It exists to hold institutional memory and technical judgement on behalf of government sponsors, and the Federal Acquisition Regulation builds that role out of a single privilege: an FFRDC 'has access, beyond that which is common to the normal contractual relationship, to Government and supplier data, including sensitive and proprietary data, and to employees and installations equipment and real property.' That sentence, FAR 35.017(a)(2), is the load-bearing beam of this dossier. Everything a prime contractor knows about its own black programmes, and everything the government knows about the prime, can lawfully sit inside the FFRDC that sits between them.

MITRE currently operates six FFRDCs: the National Security Engineering Center for DoD; the Center for Advanced Aviation System Development for the FAA; the Center for Enterprise Modernization for Treasury and the IRS, co-sponsored by Veterans Affairs, the Social Security Administration and Commerce; the Homeland Security Systems Engineering and Development Institute for DHS; the Health FFRDC for HHS; and the National Cybersecurity FFRDC for NIST. Its own fact sheet notes there are only forty-two FFRDCs in total and that operating six of them is 'a high honor.' The relevant point for this board is not prestige but coverage: one nonprofit sits inside the defence establishment, the intelligence-adjacent national security enterprise, and the civil aviation regulator that owns the primary radar picture of American airspace.

The governing document that shows how this works in practice is the FY2009 sponsoring agreement between DHS and MITRE for HS SEDI, signed by MITRE's president and by the Acting Under Secretary for Science and Technology on 13 March 2009. It is worth reading as a specification for exactly the kind of custody problem this board is mapping. Section 3 states that SEDI's work 'will often require privileged access to information, including sensitive Government information and industry proprietary data not normally available to non-Government organizations.' Section 5(h) obliges the sponsors to 'provide access to classified and sensitive data, facilities, plans and related information, including proprietary data.' Section 6(c)(9) records the routine practice: 'Ordinarily, sponsors release classified, privileged, no-contractor, and other sensitive material directly to SEDI.' Section 5(a) states plainly that 'The SEDI will work on many classified and highly sensitive projects.'

Then the agreement closes the loop on disclosure. Section 6(b)(9) gives the DHS Executive Agent authority to review and approve 'the classification, publication, and distribution of SEDI publications.' Section 9(g) defines 'Privileged Information' so broadly that it captures every deliverable, note, lab record, test result and analysis MITRE produces for the sponsor, and forbids disclosure except as required by law or 'as expressly authorized in writing by the DHS Executive Agent.' Section 9(g)(2) then carves back the whistleblower statutes, stating that 'Signing this Agreement does not bar disclosures to Congress or to an authorized official of an executive agency or the Department of Justice that are essential to reporting a substantial violation of law.' That is the protective clause. And Section 5(j)(iv) requires MITRE to make every subcontractor and consultant sign a non-disclosure agreement containing the Section 9(g) terms 'with the exception of Section 9(g)(2).' The people at the outer edge of the work - the ones brought in for specialised expertise MITRE does not hold in-house - are contractually bound by the secrecy terms without the clause that preserves their right to tell Congress.

This is the structural argument, and on 22 May 2026 a member of Congress made it out loud. Rep. Eric Burlison, of the House Oversight Committee's Task Force on the Declassification of Federal Secrets, sent a ten-page package of legislative interrogatories and production requests to MITRE's president Mark Peters and its general counsel Christopher Land. The relevant time period is 'January 1, 1930, to the present.' The definitions section reaches every MITRE-operated FFRDC, every subcontractor, and any record 'described, coded, compartmented, or euphemized as involving UAP, UFO, USO, transmedium craft, advanced aerospace threat, technology of unknown origin, non-human intelligence, legacy crash retrieval, crash retrieval, reverse engineering, recovered vehicle, recovered material, biological material, or equivalent terms.'

The interrogatories are targeted at exactly the mechanism described above. Section III demands identification of every Special Access Program, Controlled Access Program, Alternative Compensatory Control Measure, Managed-Need-To-Know codeword and DOE SIGMA category MITRE participated in or supported in connection with UAP records, and asks whether each 'was reported to the appropriate congressional committees.' It asks MITRE to identify any classification guide, NDA, access roster or sponsor communication that instructed personnel to avoid using the terms UAP, UFO, crash retrieval or reverse engineering in records. Section VIII asks for every NDA or indoctrination instrument 'beyond standard government-secrecy obligations that restricted MITRE personnel from reporting UAP-related matters to Congress.' Section IX asks MITRE to identify any UAP-related work involving Lockheed Martin, Northrop Grumman, Raytheon, Boeing, General Dynamics, SAIC, Leidos, Battelle, The Aerospace Corporation, Booz Allen Hamilton, BAE Systems or Bigelow Aerospace. Section X asks whether responsive funding was routed through a cover programme 'whose public description differed materially from the responsive work performed.' The letter warns that concealment or destruction may implicate 18 U.S.C. sections 1001, 1505, 1512, 1519 and 2071.

MITRE's reply, given to DefenseScoop on 27 May 2026, was a single sentence from a spokesperson: 'If any relevant material is found, we will coordinate with the federal agencies responsible for the work to determine how to best provide any assets.' That answer is not evasive so much as accurate. It restates the architecture the letter was written to expose. An FFRDC's disclosure obligation runs to its sponsor. Congress asks MITRE; MITRE asks the sponsor; the sponsor is the same authority that classified the work. What the record does not contain, as of this writing, is the certification of search methodology, the records-location index, the preservation hold, or any production - all of which the letter demanded on 30- and 45-day clocks.

### What it has denied

Roles the board assigns it: concealment, custody, oversight.

## National Aeronautics and Space Administration

National Aeronautics and Space Administration is a institution on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1958-07-29; axes C0 A1 D3; 18 connections; in the custody band.

In one line: Civil space agency; host of the 2023 UAP Independent Study Team.

### What it is

The National Aeronautics and Space Administration, the U.S. civil space agency. Since 2022 it has been a formal participant in the government's UAP effort through a commissioned Independent Study Team, whose final report it published on 14 September 2023 alongside the appointment of a Director of UAP Research. Since 2026 it has been a contributing agency to the Presidential Unsealing and Reporting System for UAP Encounters, supplying Apollo- and Gemini-era imagery and astronaut accounts.

### Why it is implicated

NASA's role in the concealment architecture is not custody; it is certification. It is the civilian, scientifically credentialed institution that repeatedly states there is no evidence of extraterrestrial origin while holding, by its own design choices, no access to the compartmented material where the question would actually be settled, its Independent Study Team used unclassified data only, and its charter expressly excluded review of past incidents. The sharper fact is a custody claim. In 2025 NASA's Office of Communications told a records researcher that it has no UAP records qualifying for transfer to the National Archives under the FY2024 NDAA because "any agency-related records associated with UAP, came from, and are owned by, the U.S. Department of Defense." That single sentence removes NASA's UAP holdings from the statutory records regime by attributing ownership to the department least likely to release them. Ten months later NASA's own Administrator publicly identified NASA-provided Apollo 12 and Apollo 17 lunar-surface imagery as "some of the most interesting data that NASA has provided as part of the UAP disclosure." Both statements cannot sit comfortably together: an agency with nothing to transfer, holding the most interesting data.

### The longer account

NASA entered the modern UAP file formally in 2022, when it commissioned an outside Independent Study Team to advise on how the agency might contribute. The team's final report was published on 14 September 2023 under NASA release 23-106, chaired by David Spergel, president of the Simons Foundation, with sixteen members. Its overall recommendation was that NASA play a prominent role in the whole-of-government effort by using its open-source resources, technological expertise, data analysis techniques, federal and commercial partnerships, and Earth-observing assets to curate a better dataset for understanding future UAP. The report explicitly is not a review or assessment of previous UAP incidents. That boundary is the point: NASA's charter for the study excluded the historical record, which is where the legacy program question lives.

Spergel described the methodological constraint as a virtue: "Using unclassified data was essential for our team's fact-finding, open-communication collaboration, and for upholding scientific rigor to produce this report for NASA." The team reported that there are currently a limited number of high-quality observations of UAP, which make it impossible to draw firm scientific conclusions about their nature. The same architecture reappears three years later in the UAP Science Advisory Council chaired by Avi Loeb, unclassified data only, chartered to recommend how to get better data, structurally incapable of reaching a custody finding. NASA built the template.

With the report NASA named Mark McInerney its Director of UAP Research. McInerney had previously served as NASA's liaison to the Department of Defense "covering limited UAP activities for the agency", meaning the agency's UAP function had already existed as a DoD liaison billet before it was given a public title. Administrator Bill Nelson framed the role in terms of instrumentation and analytics: the director would apply "artificial intelligence and machine learning to search the skies for anomalies," and "NASA will do this work transparently for the benefit of humanity." McInerney's career runs through NASA's Goddard Space Flight Center, NOAA and the National Hurricane Center since 1996, a NOAA lineage he shares with Tim Gallaudet, the retired admiral now on the 2026 UAP Science Advisory Council.

Then came the records question, and with it the most consequential NASA statement in the public record. The FY2024 NDAA, sections 1841-1842, required federal agencies to identify and transfer UAP records to a new collection at the National Archives, with digital transfers due by 30 September 2025. Record Group 615 was created to hold them. Over the course of 2025 the FAA, the Nuclear Regulatory Commission, the National Security Agency, ODNI and the Office of the Secretary of Defense transferred records into the online catalog. NASA did not. Asked why, NASA's Office of Communications replied: "Per the requirements in Section 1841-1842 of the Fiscal Year 2024 National Defense Authorization Act for handling records related to unidentified anomalous phenomena (UAP), NASA does not have any records that meet the requirements to submit records to the National Archives. Any agency-related records associated with UAP, came from, and are owned by, the U.S. Department of Defense." That is not a denial of possession. It is an assertion of foreign ownership over material NASA holds, a jurisdictional argument that keeps agency UAP records outside a statutory public-release regime by routing their custody back into the Department of War.

The 2026 disclosure wave then produced the contradiction. On 27 May 2026, NASA Administrator Jared Isaacman told Fox News Digital that the newly declassified files show "real unexplained phenomena" but no recovered craft: "What's being surfaced isn't crashed ships or alien bodies, but real unexplained phenomena," and "There's nothing I'm aware of in terms of alien bodies or spaceships." Note the construction, personal awareness, from a political appointee without read-in to the compartments where such material would be held. In the same interview Isaacman said agencies "really didn't take this quite as seriously in the past until President Trump put out the tweet, basically giving an order to government agencies and going out and saying, look, this time you better go through the files, you better start searching your databases and bring things up", a sitting NASA Administrator stating that federal agencies did not conduct records searches for UAP material until a presidential post compelled them.

And on NASA's own contribution: "I would say that some of the most interesting data that NASA has provided as part of the UAP disclosure was taken on the surface of the moon from Apollo 12 and 17. You can't be a moon landing denier and also believe that those photos captured unexplained phenomenon." Fox News reported that the PURSUE material includes astronaut and NASA-related accounts from the Apollo and Gemini eras describing strange lights and unidentified objects observed in space, alongside 2023 infrared footage of an F-16 engaging a diamond-shaped object over Lake Huron and reports from military operations in Iran, Syria, Iraq and Greece. Set against the 2025 Office of Communications statement, NASA has no qualifying records because DoD owns them, the position is that NASA's most interesting UAP data was released through the Department of War's website rather than deposited in the National Archives collection Congress created for exactly that purpose. One route is statutory, permanent and indexed; the other is a rolling, discretionary web publication controlled by the Secretary of War. NASA's material went down the second.

A loose thread worth pulling: on the war.gov PURSUE page, the Release 05 slideshow of 7 August 2026 carries an image described as "A photograph of an unidentified object in low-Earth orbit, taken from Space Shuttle Columbia in 1996," while the underlying file is named DOW-UAP-D098_Film-Analysis-of-Unidentified-Objects_1953.jpg. A 1996 Columbia photograph filed under a 1953 film analysis is either a metadata error or an indexing artifact, and either way it is a checkable discrepancy in the government's own release. The Columbia 1996 imagery in question is almost certainly from STS-75, the tethered satellite mission whose downlink footage has been argued over for thirty years, meaning the 2026 disclosure has quietly placed a long-contested NASA video into an official unresolved-case release.

### What it has denied

NASA's denial operates in three layers, each with an escape hatch. The 2023 Independent Study Team found no evidence of extraterrestrial origin while working exclusively from unclassified data and explicitly excluding past incidents from its charter. The 2025 Office of Communications statement denies possession of transferable records by asserting DoD ownership rather than absence, a custody argument, not a factual one. And Administrator Isaacman's 2026 formulation, 'There's nothing I'm aware of in terms of alien bodies or spaceships,' limits the denial to the personal knowledge of an appointee with no read-in to the compartments in question. Each is technically defensible and none touches the underlying question.

Roles the board assigns it: research, concealment, disclosure, custody.

### Open questions on this node

- What UAP-related records does NASA physically hold that it characterises as DoD-owned, and who at NASA made that ownership determination?
- Was NASA's ownership position coordinated with the Department of Defense or the National Archives, and is there written correspondence recording it?
- Is Mark McInerney still serving as Director of UAP Research under Administrator Isaacman, and has the role been budgeted or staffed?
- Why does PURSUE Release 05 file DOW-UAP-D098 carry a 1953 film-analysis filename while being described as a 1996 Space Shuttle Columbia photograph, and is the underlying imagery from STS-75?
- Where are the original Apollo 12 and Apollo 17 lunar-surface negatives and the associated frame indices, and were any frames withheld from the PURSUE release?
- Did the NASA Independent Study Team request classified access and, if so, who denied it?
- Which NASA Gemini- and Apollo-era astronaut accounts entered PURSUE, through what channel, and were the astronauts or their estates consulted?

## Battelle Memorial Institute

Battelle Memorial Institute is a institution on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1929; axes C3 A2 D1; 14 connections; in the custody band.

In one line: Private nonprofit applied science institute, Columbus, Ohio, est. 1929; operator or co-operator of PNNL, ORNL, INL, NREL, Brookhaven and NBACC.

### What it is

Battelle Memorial Institute is a Columbus, Ohio applied-science nonprofit founded in 1929 that describes itself as the world's largest independent nonprofit applied science organization. From late 1951 it held the classified Air Technical Intelligence Center contract code-named Project STORK, out of which came Project Blue Book Special Report No. 14 (5 May 1955), the largest statistical study of UFO reports ever performed for the U.S. government. Today Battelle manages or co-manages eight U.S. national laboratories, including Los Alamos, Oak Ridge, Idaho, Pacific Northwest and Savannah River.

### Why it is implicated

Battelle is the single institution that sits on both sides of the mid-century split: it produced the numbers that the public debunking campaign was built on, and it is the contract operator of the laboratory system where exotic-materials characterization would actually be performed. The Durant Report's own list of documentary evidence placed before the CIA's Robertson Panel names, as item 3, "Progress Reports of Project STORK (code name for Battelle Memorial Institute contract work supporting ATIC)", meaning a private contractor's classified analysis was the evidentiary substrate for the panel that recommended a national "training and debunking" program. Battelle's own analysis found roughly 22 percent of about 3,200 cases were unknowns, and that the higher the case quality the more likely it was to be unknown; the Air Force publicized the study in October 1955 as proof that flying saucers did not exist and cited a 3 percent unknown rate. Five days before the Robertson Panel convened, a Battelle memorandum dated 9 January 1953, the document Jacques Vallee found in J. Allen Hynek's files in 1967 and calls the "Pentacle memorandum", is reported to have proposed that Battelle and ATIC agree in advance on what could and could not be said to the CIA panel. Battelle is therefore not a neutral statistical vendor; it is the earliest documented instance of the pattern this board tracks, a cleared contractor mediating between what the government knew and what a public-facing scientific body was permitted to see.

### The longer account

The relationship began with an admission of institutional inadequacy. Captain Edward J. Ruppelt, running the Air Force's UFO desk at the Air Technical Intelligence Center at Wright-Patterson, concluded at the end of 1951 that the Air Force lacked the analytic capacity to do anything scientific with the report backlog. He went to Battelle Memorial Institute in Columbus, roughly a hundred and twenty miles away, and the resulting classified support contract was code-named Project STORK. Battelle built a standardized observer data sheet, encoded about thirty characteristics of each sighting onto IBM punched cards, and ran the first machine-assisted statistical analysis of the UFO corpus. It is important to hold the shape of this arrangement in view: the government's public UFO investigation had, from 1952 onward, a classified contractor layer underneath it that the public did not know existed and that Blue Book's own critics could not see.

The Robertson Panel is where that layer surfaces in the documentary record. F.C. Durant's 16 February 1953 memorandum to the CIA's Assistant Director for Scientific Intelligence records that on Thursday morning, 15 January 1953, "The Project STORK support at Battelle Memorial Institute, Columbus, was described by Dr. Hynek." Tab B of the same document lists the STORK progress reports as evidence item 3, sitting alongside the Project GRUDGE and BLUE BOOK status reports, the Holloman Air Force Base sightings summary, Project TWINKLE, and Project POUNCE. The five-scientist panel that would recommend stripping UFOs "of the special status they have been given" was reading Battelle's work product.

The timing of the Pentacle memorandum is the part of this that will not settle. Vallee has stated for decades that in 1967, while organizing Hynek's files, he found a memorandum stamped SECRET dated 9 January 1953, five days before the Robertson Panel's first session, written by Battelle's Howard C. Cross and addressed to Miles Goll at Wright-Patterson, and that it proposed agreement between Project Stork and ATIC on what could and could not be discussed in Washington on 14-16 January. Vallee's papers are now at Rice University's Woodson Research Center as MS 0672, and the finding aid lists a file titled "11. The Robertson Panel and the 'Pentacle Memorandum', January 1953" in Series III. The collection is restricted: Series I and II open on 24 January 2028, Series III case files on 1 January 2031. The Department of Defense has never released the memorandum. Treat the document's existence as archivally attested and its full text as an open question, not a claim.

Special Report No. 14, dated 5 May 1955, is the payoff. Battelle's analysts sorted about 3,200 cases into knowns, unknowns and insufficient information, graded each on a four-point quality scale, and required unanimity among four analysts before a case could be called unknown, an unusually stringent test that biases toward suppressing the unknown count. About 22 percent came back unknown. The quality gradient ran the wrong way for the debunking thesis: roughly 35 percent of excellent-quality cases were unknowns versus about 18 percent of the poorest. Battelle's own summary nevertheless declared it "highly improbable" that any of the reports represented technological developments outside present-day knowledge. When the Air Force released the study in October 1955 under Secretary Donald Quarles, the public framing was that the report scientifically disproved flying saucers, with an unknown rate cited as 3 percent. The report itself was not distributed; the press release was. The cleanest surviving copy of the 312-page report sits in CIA's declassified holdings as CIA-RDP81R00560R000100060001-5, which is itself worth noting, since the document is nominally an Air Force product.

What makes Battelle load-bearing for a recovery-and-exploitation thesis rather than merely for a history of statistical malpractice is what the institute became. Battelle's own site states it manages eight national laboratories for the Departments of Energy and Homeland Security under the government-owned, contractor-operated model, and partners in managing Canadian Nuclear Laboratories. It is the sole LLC member for Pacific Northwest National Laboratory, Idaho National Laboratory (via Battelle Energy Alliance), the National Biodefense Analysis and Countermeasures Center, and Savannah River National Laboratory; an LLC member of UT-Battelle for Oak Ridge; of Brookhaven Science Associates with Stony Brook; and of Triad National Security with the Texas A&M System and the University of California for Los Alamos, which Battelle describes as executing work for the Department of Energy, the Department of War and the Department of Homeland Security. Battelle's stated core competencies are advanced materials, biology and chemistry, and its national security portfolio includes an advanced functional materials line.

The analytic point is a continuity of institutional posture, not a proven chain of custody. The organization that in 1953 was negotiating with ATIC over what the CIA's scientists would be allowed to hear is the same organization that now holds the operating contracts for the laboratories where anomalous materials would plausibly be characterized. No public contract, appropriation line, or DD Form 254 has been produced connecting Battelle or any Battelle-operated laboratory to UAP materials exploitation. That absence is the research target, and Battelle's carve-out-eligible SAP work at those laboratories is exactly the space in which such an absence would be expected.

### What it has denied

Battelle has issued no public statement about Project STORK, the Pentacle memorandum, or any materials-analysis role, and its corporate history pages do not mention the ATIC contract at all. The denial in this case is by omission: the institute's public account of its own past treats 1952-1955 as if the largest statistical study of UFO reports ever performed for the U.S. government was not its work.

Roles the board assigns it: research, concealment, custody.

### Open questions on this node

- The full text of the 9 January 1953 Battelle memorandum has never been released by DoD or Battelle; the only known copy sits in a Rice University collection restricted until 2031. What does the complete document say about what ATIC withheld from the CIA panel?
- What was the contract number, contracting activity and dollar value of Project STORK, and how long did it run after Special Report No. 14 was delivered?
- Battelle reportedly reacted with hostility when Hynek returned in 1967 to ask about the memorandum; no contemporaneous record of that exchange has surfaced.
- Did Battelle perform materials characterization on any recovered debris, at Columbus or at any Battelle-operated national laboratory? No contract, appropriation line, DD Form 254 or facility clearance record establishing this has been produced.
- Which Battelle-operated facilities hold SAP carve-out designations under 32 CFR 117.6, and does DCSA retain or lack industrial security cognizance over them?
- Why does the cleanest surviving copy of an Air Force report reside in CIA's document holdings rather than in the Air Force's own accession to NARA?
- Battelle's own corporate history omits the ATIC work entirely, was that omission a contract requirement, a classification consequence, or an editorial choice?

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 36 of 1125, score 90). 15 connections, 6 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 1 official denial recorded against it. Axes C3/A2/D1.

- **Tier 1 documented, allegation shaped wording as stored.** The federal literature on nitinol, one report of it written by Battelle itself, does not support a Battelle 1948-49 lineage for the alloy. NASA SP-5110, prepared for NASA by Battelle Memorial Institute in 1972, opens: 'Some 10 years ago, Buehler and Wiley of the U.S. Naval Ordnance Laboratory developed a series of engineering alloys that possess a unique mechanical (shape) memory. The generic name of the series of alloys is 55-Nitinol, where Nitinol stands for Nickel Titanium Naval Ordnance Laboratory.' The earliest Naval Ordnance Laboratory nitinol report cited anywhere in its reference list is NOLTR 61-75 of 3 August 1961; US Patent 3,174,851 was filed on 1 December 1961; and NASA CR-1433 of September 1969 records Battelle's own role as a subcontractor to Goodyear Aerospace which 'processed the basic Nitinol rod'. The allegation that Wright-Patterson-sponsored Battelle reports on nickel-titanium alloy from 1948-49 are missing from Air Force and Battelle archives is kept on this board as the allegation it is, and is unsupported by the federal literature opened here. ## Força Aérea Brasileira / Ministério da Aeronáutica

Força Aérea Brasileira / Ministério da Aeronáutica is a institution on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1941; axes C2 A2 D3; 10 connections; in the custody band.

In one line: Brazilian Air Force; ran Operação Prato and the 1986 intercepts; transfers UAP records to the Arquivo Nacional under Portaria 551/GC3 of 9 August 2010.

### What it is

The Forca Aerea Brasileira (FAB), the air arm of the Comando da Aeronautica, formerly the Ministerio da Aeronautica, which existed as a cabinet ministry from 1941 until the Ministry of Defence absorbed it in 1999. It is the Brazilian state organ that holds the air-defence radar picture, controls civil and military air traffic through CINDACTA and COMDABRA/COMAE, and receives pilot and controller reports of unidentified aerial objects. Since 2010 it has operated under a published administrative rule requiring it to register those reports and forward them to a civilian national archive.

### Why it is implicated

The FAB is the board's control case. It is a mid-sized air force inside a country that spent 1964 to 1985 under a military dictatorship, which means it had every institutional reason to behave like the US Air Force did after 1947, and for a period it did: SIOANI in 1969, Operacao Prato in 1977 under a named captain, and an air-defence scramble in 1986 that it initially treated as a security event. What it then did is what makes it load-bearing. It did not build a permanent denial apparatus around the subject; it built a filing pipeline out of it. Portaria do Comando da Aeronautica no 551/GC3 of 9 August 2010 does not classify unidentified aerial phenomena reports, it routes them to the Arquivo Nacional for public consultation, and the FAB says on the record that everything it holds from 1952 to 2025 has gone there. Every structural argument the US posture rests on (national security, sources and methods, the danger of public panic, the need to protect radar performance data) was equally available to a Cold War military government in Brasilia, and Brasilia declined to use it.

### The longer account

The Brazilian Air Force arrives on this board not as a suspected participant in a legacy recovery programme but as the measuring stick against which the US posture is read. Its value is that it controls for almost every variable the US concealment argument depends on. Brazil had an air force with a genuine air-defence mission, a real radar network, supersonic interceptors, a military intelligence service (the SNI), and, for twenty-one years, a military government answerable to nobody. If the argument for permanent secrecy about anomalous aerial objects were driven by the intrinsic sensitivity of the subject, the FAB should look like the most closed institution on the map. Instead it is the most open, and the openness is administrative rather than rhetorical, which is what makes it hard to dismiss.

The institutional history runs in three phases. The first is investigative. In 1969 the FAB stood up the Sistema de Investigacao de Objetos Aereos Nao Identificados, SIOANI, inside the 4a Zona Aerea in Sao Paulo, the command later renamed IV COMAR. SIOANI ran for roughly three years before ceasing operations quietly in 1972, and left behind a documentary residue that is now in the Arquivo Nacional. The second and best known phase is Operacao Prato, the field operation the FAB mounted in Para in 1977 and 1978 under Capitao Uyrange Hollanda, which put uniformed personnel into riverine villages with cameras and orders to observe. The third is the 19 May 1986 night, when CINDACTA and civil radar produced returns the FAB could not reconcile, five fighters were launched, and the Minister of Aeronautics stood in front of the press four days later and said the Air Force had no technical explanation.

What happened after 1986 is the part that matters most for comparison. The FAB did not sustain a public debunking programme. It did not issue a terminal report closing the file the way Project Blue Book was closed in 1969 with the Condon Committee as its warrant. Its posture, as characterised in the Brazilian Senate's own record, was discretion without denial: reticent when unasked, non-denying when asked. Then, in stages between 2007 and 2010, it converted the material into an archival stream. From 2007 the Aeronautica began sending its ufological papers to the Arquivo Nacional in Brasilia. In April 2009 CENDOC, the Air Force's documentation and history centre, sent across the first tranche relating to the Para operation. In August 2010 the Comando da Aeronautica published Portaria no 551/GC3 in the Diario Oficial da Uniao, formalising the pipeline: reports of aerial phenomena received by the aerospace operations command are received, registered, catalogued and forwarded to the Arquivo Nacional, where they are made available for consultation.

That rule is the single most consequential artefact in this dossier, because it inverts the default. Under 551/GC3 the resting state of a Brazilian military UAP report is public. There is no analytic gate, no adjudication of credibility before release, no requirement that the object be resolved before the paperwork moves. A LATAM captain reporting lights over Guarulhos in September 2025 and a Londrina tower controller reporting an intermittent radar-and-visual target in November 2025 both end up as scanned forms a citizen can pull down. In August 2026 the FAB restated the position in a written note to CNN Brasil: all documents, videos, photographs and reports available within the Comando da Aeronautica concerning unidentified aerial phenomena for the period 1952 to 2025 have already been transferred to the Arquivo Nacional, where they are in the public domain.

The limits are real and should be stated. The transfer was one service only. The Senate record is explicit that from 2007 it was the Aeronautica, and not the Army and not the Navy, that sent its papers to the Arquivo Nacional, and a 2013 approach to the Ministry of Defence by Brazilian researchers was aimed specifically at prying loose the Army and Navy holdings. So Brazil is not a case of total disclosure. It is a case of one service unilaterally choosing custody-with-publication while its sister services did not, which is itself an argument that the choice was institutional rather than physically compelled by the nature of the material. Nothing in the FAB record establishes retrieval of a craft or reverse-engineering activity, and nothing here should be read as claiming otherwise. What it establishes is narrower and sharper: a military that scrambled interceptors against unresolved radar contacts, said so at a podium, and then put the file in a civilian archive did not find that any of those three acts destabilised the state. The US argument that it cannot do the first two, let alone the third, is therefore not a claim about the subject matter. It is a claim about the US institution.

### What it has denied

The FAB does not deny; it defers to the archive. Its standing public answer, restated in a written note in August 2026, is that everything it holds from 1952 to 2025 is already in the Arquivo Nacional and in the public domain, which converts every substantive question into a records query rather than a security refusal. That posture is itself the artefact: an air force that treats the subject as a filing matter has decided the subject is not a secret.

Roles the board assigns it: research, custody, disclosure, concealment.

### Open questions on this node

- The full text of Portaria no 551/GC3 of 9 August 2010 as published in the Diario Oficial da Uniao has not been retrieved directly; the date and content are attested by the FAB's own note and by the Senate record, not yet by the DOU page itself.
- The Senate record spells the 2010 FAB commander as 'Junichi Saito'; the commander of the Aeronautica in that period is conventionally rendered Juniti Saito. The discrepancy has not been resolved against a primary FAB or DOU source.
- The FAB says it has transferred everything through 2025, but the Arquivo Nacional's own description of the fund gives the most recent occurrence as 2016. Whether this reflects a lag in accessioning, a separate accrual series, or a genuine gap is unresolved.
- No document has been located establishing why the Aeronautica transferred and the Exercito and Marinha did not, or what the Army and Navy holdings contain.
- Nothing in the FAB record located here speaks to material recovery, custody of hardware, or reverse-engineering. Absence of such records in an otherwise open service is itself a datum but is not evidence either way.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 105 of 1125, score 61). 11 connections, 4 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C2/A2/D3.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** Air Defence Command finding, 2 June 1986: the phenomena 'são sólidos e refletem de certa forma inteligência'. Source: Relatório de Ocorrências, Arquivo Nacional BR_DFANBSB_ARX_0_0_0249
- **Tier 1 documented.** DOU RECORD FOUND (by citation, not by page image). A Chamber of Deputies information requirement cites the underlying process 'citado no Diário Oficial da União de 10 de agosto de 2010, Seção I - página 101', and states that 'no dia 10 de agosto do ano passado a Força Aérea admitira e abrira oficialmente parte do seu esquema de procedimentos quanto ao assunto dos OVNIs, por meio da publicação no DOU da Portaria 551/GC3'. Publication is therefore attested by a primary parliamentary document independent of the FAB's own note: DOU of 10 August 2010, Seção 1, p. 101. The in.gov.br page image itself could not be retrieved. Source: Câmara dos Deputados, RIC 679/2011, item 1.3 and justificação, 22 June 2011 (https://www.camara.leg.br/proposicoesWeb/prop_mostrarintegra?codteor=891762&filename=RIC+679%2F2011)
- **Tier 1 documented.** Full operative text of Portaria nº 551/GC3, de 9 de agosto de 2010, transcribed in a federal court judgment. Legal basis: 'inciso XIV do art. 23 da Estrutura Regimental do Comando da Aeronáutica, aprovada pelo Decreto nº 6.834, de 30 de abril de 2009', and Processo nº 67000.001974/2010-61. Art. 1º: 'As atividades do Comando da Aeronáutica (COMAER) relativas ao assunto "objetos voadores não identificados" (OVNI) restringem-se ao registro de ocorrências e ao seu trâmite para o Arquivo Nacional.' Art. 2º assigns COMDABRA to receive and catalogue. Art. 3º assigns CENDOC to copy, bind, archive and periodically forward originals to the Arquivo Nacional. Art. 6º: 'Revoga-se a Nota Nº C-002/MIN/ADM, de 13 de abril de 1978 e o Aviso Nº S-001/MIN, de 28 de fevereiro de 1989.' Source: Justiça Federal, Seção Judiciária de Santa Catarina, Recurso Cível n. 5002417-48.2022.4.04.7200/SC, RELVOTO signed 27/2/2023 (https://www.migalhas.com.br/arquivos/2026/1/7F5E4CC12D7AE7_terceiraacao.pdf)
- **Tier 1 documented.** SPELLING RESOLVED - 'Juniti Saito' is correct; 'Junichi Saito' is an error originating in the Senate requerimento. The Câmara dos Deputados portal reproduces the Air Force Commander's own Ordem do Dia for Aviator's Day, signed in his own signature block: 'Tenente-Brigadeiro-do-Ar JUNITI SAITO / Comandante da Aeronáutica' (24/10/2012). The variant 'Junichi Saito' appears in the justificação of Senado RQS 193/2022, which also miscalls the Portaria a 'decreto'. Source: Câmara dos Deputados, CREDN, 'Pronunciamento do Comandante da Aeronáutica, Tenente-Brigadeiro-do-Ar Juniti Saito', 24/10/2012 (https://www2.camara.leg.br/atividade-legislativa/comissoes/comissoes-permanentes/credn/noticias/pronunciamento-do-comandante-da-aeronautica-tenente-brigadeiro-do-ar-juniti-saito); contrasted with Senado Federal RQS 193/2022
- **Tier 1 documented.** The Arquivo Nacional states that its Fundo Objeto Voador Nao Identificado (OVNI), reference code BR DFANBSB ARX, was produced by the Comando da Aeronautica do Brasil and is held in the Arquivo Nacional's custody. Source: Arquivo Nacional, 'Conheca o fundo sobre OVNIs do Arquivo Nacional', https://www.gov.br/arquivonacional/pt-br/canais_atendimento/imprensa/noticias/conheca-o-fundo-sobre-ovnis-do-arquivo-nacional
- **Tier 1 documented.** The Brazilian Senate's own record states that from 2007 the Aeronautica, but not the Army and not the Navy, began sending its research papers on the subject to the Arquivo Nacional in Brasilia. Source: Senado Federal, Requerimento no 193, de 2022 (RQS 193/2022), justificacao, presented 16 March 2022, https://legis.senado.leg.br/sdleg-getter/documento?dm=9089831&disposition=inline
- **Tier 1 documented.** The Senate record asserts that Brazil was the first nation to officially admit that OVNIs exist, in a 1954 session at the Escola Superior de Guerra in Rio de Janeiro at which the then Aeronautica captain Joao Adil de Oliveira addressed military officers, civilians and press. Source: Senado Federal, RQS 193/2022, justificacao, https://legis.senado.leg.br/sdleg-getter/documento?dm=9089831&disposition=inline ; Agencia Senado, 24 June 2022, https://www12.senado.leg.br/noticias/materias/2022/06/24/em-homenagem-no-plenario-ufologos-cobram-liberacao-de-documentos-sobre-ovnis
- **Tier 1 documented.** The Senate record characterises the Aeronautica's historic posture as discreet but non-denying: 'A Aeronautica sempre foi discreta quanto a estes e a outros fatos, mas nunca os negou quando inquirida.' Source: Senado Federal, RQS 193/2022, justificacao, https://legis.senado.leg.br/sdleg-getter/documento?dm=9089831&disposition=inline
- **Tier 1 documented.** The Senate record names Operacao Prato (1977), the creation of SIOANI in 1969 at the IV Comando Aereo Regional in Sao Paulo, and the 1986 'Noite Oficial dos UFOs no Brasil' as official Brazilian government UFO research programmes conducted through the Aeronautica. Source: Senado Federal, RQS 193/2022, justificacao, https://legis.senado.leg.br/sdleg-getter/documento?dm=9089831&disposition=inline
- **Tier 1 documented.** The Senate record states that in April 2013, at the invitation of then Foreign Minister Celso Amorim, Brazilian researchers were received at the Ministry of Defence before the commanders of the armed forces to press for full opening of the ufological documents, specifically those of the Army and the Navy. Source: Senado Federal, RQS 193/2022, justificacao, https://legis.senado.leg.br/sdleg-getter/documento?dm=9089831&disposition=inline
- **Tier 1 documented.** The Senate record states that the FAB's own web portal has made available a link dedicated exclusively to the subject of unidentified flying objects. Source: Senado Federal, RQS 193/2022, justificacao, https://legis.senado.leg.br/sdleg-getter/documento?dm=9089831&disposition=inline
- **Tier 1 documented.** The same Senate record states that in August 2010 the then commander of the FAB, identified in the document as brigadeiro Junichi Saito, determined by an instrument published in the Diario Oficial da Uniao that all OVNI cases registered across the force's installations be concentrated at COMDABRA for onward transmission to the Arquivo Nacional. Source: Senado Federal, RQS 193/2022, justificacao, https://legis.senado.leg.br/sdleg-getter/documento?dm=9089831&disposition=inline
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** A LATAM captain reported to Brazilian authorities on 1 September 2025 near Guarulhos (SP) seeing two to three strong balls of light, white, red and yellow, moving irregularly, and reported associated dry turbulence; the report form is in the Arquivo Nacional release. Source: CNN Brasil, 12 August 2026, reproducing the Arquivo Nacional form, https://www.cnnbrasil.com.br/ciencia/pilotos-e-controladores-relataram-18-casos-de-ovnis-no-brasil-em-2025/
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** Air Force note to BBC Brasil: all available UFO material has been sent to the Arquivo Nacional and the force 'não dispõe de profissionais especializados para realizar investigações científicas ou emitir parecer a respeito deste tipo de fenômeno aéreo'. Source: BBC Brasil, 14 January 2022
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** FAB documents released through the Arquivo Nacional record at least 18 unidentified-object cases reported in Brazil during 2025 by pilots, air traffic controllers and other aviation-sector personnel, with Parana (5 cases, concentrated at Londrina) and Ceara (4) leading. Source: CNN Brasil, 'Pilotos e controladores relataram 18 casos de OVNIs no Brasil em 2025', 12 August 2026, https://www.cnnbrasil.com.br/ciencia/pilotos-e-controladores-relataram-18-casos-de-ovnis-no-brasil-em-2025/
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** FAB's current stated coverage runs to 2025, contradicting the Arquivo Nacional's 2018 description. Official Air Force note: 'A Força Aérea Brasileira (FAB) informa que todos os documentos, vídeos, fotografias, relatos, entre outros, disponíveis, no âmbito do Comando da Aeronáutica, sobre fenômenos aéreos não identificados, no período de 1952 a 2025, já foram transferidos para o Arquivo Nacional, onde são de domínio público.' The same note cites Portaria 551/GC3 and names COMAE (not COMDABRA) as the receiving command, reflecting the post-2017 reorganisation. Source: Nota da Força Aérea Brasileira, quoted verbatim in CNN Brasil, 12 August 2026 (https://www.cnnbrasil.com.br/ciencia/pilotos-e-controladores-relataram-18-casos-de-ovnis-no-brasil-em-2025/)
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** On 10 August 2010 the Air Force created rules for pilots in case of contact with UFOs. Source: EFE/Terra, 'FAB cria normas para pilotos em caso de contato com ovnis', 10 August 2010
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** Portaria do Comando da Aeronautica no 551/GC3, dated 9 August 2010, governs the forwarding of reports of aerial phenomena received by the Comando de Operacoes Aeroespaciais (COMAE) to the Arquivo Nacional; under it the Comando da Aeronautica receives, registers, catalogues and forwards the occurrences to that body, where they are made available for consultation. Source: Nota da Forca Aerea reproduced verbatim in CNN Brasil, 12 August 2026, https://www.cnnbrasil.com.br/ciencia/pilotos-e-controladores-relataram-18-casos-de-ovnis-no-brasil-em-2025/
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** SIOANI, the Sistema de Investigacao de Objetos Aereos Nao Identificados, was created in 1969 within the FAB's 4a Zona Aerea in Sao Paulo (later IV COMAR) and ceased operations in 1972. Source: Revista Questao de Ciencia, 'A ditadura a caca de extraterrestres', 30 March 2024, https://www.revistaquestaodeciencia.com.br/apocalipse-now/2024/03/30/ditadura-caca-de-extraterrestres ; SIOANI bulletin scans, Internet Archive 'Brazilian UFO Files', https://archive.org/stream/BrazilianUFOFiles/1969%20Boletim%20SIOANI%20Conteu%CC%81do%201969_BOLETIM_SIOANI_CONTEUDO_djvu.txt
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** The Forca Aerea Brasileira stated in an official written note in August 2026 that all documents, videos, photographs and reports held within the Comando da Aeronautica on unidentified aerial phenomena for the period 1952 to 2025 have already been transferred to the Arquivo Nacional and are in the public domain. Source: Nota da Forca Aerea reproduced verbatim in CNN Brasil, 'Pilotos e controladores relataram 18 casos de OVNIs no Brasil em 2025', 12 August 2026 (updated 14 August 2026), https://www.cnnbrasil.com.br/ciencia/pilotos-e-controladores-relataram-18-casos-de-ovnis-no-brasil-em-2025/

### Its strongest connections

- Força Aérea Brasileira / Ministério da Aeronáutica runs out to **AARO FY2025 Consolidated Annual Report on UAP** (document, board tier A), a institutional link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: COMPARISON: AARO's Foreign Partner Engagement section names no Latin American partner at all. COMPARISON EDGE, and a sourced negative. Section V.A of AARO's FY2025 Consolidated Annual Report is two sentences in full: 'During the reporting period, AARO continued to engage with international partners to exchange information and best practices for UAP reporting, analysis, and response. AARO's deputy director presented a mission briefing to the Defense Geospatial Intelligence conference in London, United Kingdom.' No foreign office, no foreign air force and no foreign archive is named anywhere in the document except that one London conference. Brazil, the FAB and the Arquivo Nacional, the largest declassified military UAP holding in the Americas, do not appear. The task asked what the section actually names: it names one conference in London and nothing else. Sources: All-domain Anomaly Resolution Office, 'Fiscal Year 2025 Consolidated Annual Report on Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena', document 26-P-0880, section V.A 'Foreign Partner Engagement', https://www.aaro.mil/Portals/136/PDFs/FY25%20UAP%20Annual%20Report/AARO_FY2025_Consolidated_Annual_Report_on_UAP.pdf; Relatório de Ocorrências (Arquivo Nacional), http://imagem.sian.an.gov.br/acervo/derivadas/BR_DFANBSB_ARX/0/0/0249/BR_DFANBSB_ARX_0_0_0249_d0001de0001.pdf, dated 1986-06-02.
- Força Aérea Brasileira / Ministério da Aeronáutica runs out to **Operação Prato (Operation Saucer)** (program, board tier A), a programmatic link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: The Brazilian Air Force mounted Operação Prato as its own field operation. Operação Prato was run by the Força Aérea Brasileira out of the Belém air base in Pará, with the SNI, after mayors in the Colares area formally asked for help. The Brazilian Senate's own Requerimento 193/2022 lists it among the official Aeronáutica UAP programmes: 'Desde 1954, o Governo Brasileiro, por meio de sua Aeronáutica, já criou vários programas oficiais de pesquisas dos OVNIs, como em 1977, quando ocorreu a chamada Operação Prato'. Bob Pratt's file records that the operation was staffed by 'Captain Uyrangê Hollanda and six sergeants' and was shut down after four months by Brigadier Protázio Lopes de Oliveira, the base commander in Belém. This is the base institutional fact the whole Brazilian cluster hangs from and it was previously unwired. [CORRECTED 14 Aug 2026: RQS 193/2022 is a request for a COMMEMORATIVE SPECIAL SESSION under Art. 199 RISF, approved 16 Mar 2022, held 24 Jun 2022 - not a records-seeking or compelling instrument. Its durable value is the content of its justificacao, which names Operacao Prato, the 1986 night, SIOANI and the archive reference BR DFANBSB ARX. These edges assert what the document NAMES, which is verifiable in its text and unaffected by its procedural nature, so the tier is unchanged. - Força Aérea Brasileira / Ministério da Aeronáutica runs out to **Arquivo Nacional (Brazil) - UFO/UAP collection** (institution, board tier A), a programmatic link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: Portaria 551/GC3 (9 Aug 2010): COMDABRA/COMAE catalogues, CENDOC forwards originals periodically. Art. 3 mandates periodic transfer of originals, which is why the fund has no terminal year. Published DOU 10 Aug 2010, Seção 1, p. 101. Revoked Nota C-002/MIN/ADM (13 Apr 1978) and Aviso S-001/MIN (28 Feb 1989). Successive accessions are documented at least to a 'terceiro recolhimento' covering 1981, with CENDOC's bulk remittance to COREG scheduled to close 31 Aug 2010; FAB states coverage now runs 1952-2025. Sources: https://www.migalhas.com.br/arquivos/2026/1/7F5E4CC12D7AE7_terceiraacao.pdf, https://www.migalhas.com.br/arquivos/2026/1/7F5E4CC12D7AE7_terceiraacao.pdf; https://www.camara.leg.br/proposicoesWeb/prop_mostrarintegra?codteor=891762&filename=RIC+679%2F2011, https://www.camara.leg.br/proposicoesWeb/prop_mostrarintegra?codteor=891762&filename=RIC+679%2F2011; https://www.cnnbrasil.com.br/ciencia/pilotos-e-controladores-relataram-18-casos-de-ovnis-no-brasil-em-2025/, https://www.cnnbrasil.com.br/ciencia/pilotos-e-controladores-relataram-18-casos-de-ovnis-no-brasil-em-2025/; Relatório de Ocorrências (Arquivo Nacional), http://imagem.sian.an.gov.br/acervo/derivadas/BR_DFANBSB_ARX/0/0/0249/BR_DFANBSB_ARX_0_0_0249_d0001de0001.pdf, dated 1986-06-02; BBC Brasil, https://www.bbc.com/portuguese/brasil-59953278, dated 2022-01-14.
- Força Aérea Brasileira / Ministério da Aeronáutica runs out to **Noite Oficial dos OVNIs - the Brazilian 'Night of the UFOs', 19 May 1986** (event, board tier A), a programmatic link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: The Air Force scrambled the interceptors on the Night of the UFOs. The 19 May 1986 engagement was executed by the FAB: fighters launched from Santa Cruz and Anápolis against radar plots held by the CINDACTA network. RQS 193/2022 describes it in the Senate's own words as the night 'quando 21 OVNIs esféricos de 100 metros de diâmetro cada foram perseguidos durante horas por nossos caças a jato'. The event node previously connected only to the minister and to the report, not to the service that flew the sorties. - Força Aérea Brasileira / Ministério da Aeronáutica runs out to **Brigadeiro do Ar José Pessoa Cavalcanti de Albuquerque** (person, board tier B), a institutional link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: Pessoa Cavalcanti signed for the FAB's own Air Defence Command. José Pessoa Cavalcanti de Albuquerque signed the Relatório de Ocorrências of 2 June 1986 as acting commander of COMDA/NuCOMDABRA, a command of the Brazilian Air Force. The finding that the objects were solid and behaved intelligently is therefore a FAB internal finding rendered up its own chain of command under classification, not an outside assessment. - Força Aérea Brasileira / Ministério da Aeronáutica comes in from **Senado Federal do Brasil** (institution, board tier C), a legislative link at Tier 1 documented. It also records that the Army and Navy did not transfer their holdings, a named, sourced gap in the Brazilian openness story rather than a clean win. - Força Aérea Brasileira / Ministério da Aeronáutica runs out to **Capitão Uyrangê Bolívar Soares Nogueira de Hollanda Lima** (person, board tier C), a institutional link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: Hollanda was a serving FAB officer, not a civilian investigator. Uyrangê Hollanda commanded Operação Prato as a captain of the Brazilian Air Force and retired as a lieutenant colonel. Pratt's file: 'In 1997, long after he had retired as a lieutenant colonel, Hollanda decided he was no longer obligated to remain silent about Operação Prato and publicly discussed Operação Prato.' The institutional tie matters because his later account contradicts the service's official negative finding, the contradiction is internal to the FAB, not between the FAB and outsiders. ## Atomic Energy Commission

Atomic Energy Commission is a institution on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1946-08-01 to 1974-10-11; axes C2 A3 D1; 9 connections; in the custody band.

In one line: Civilian atomic authority, 1946-1974; statutory custodian of fissionable material and atomic weapons.

### What it is

The civilian atomic authority of the United States from 1 August 1946 to 11 October 1974. It was created by section 2(a) of the Atomic Energy Act of 1946 as a five-member commission appointed by the President with the advice and consent of the Senate, and it took statutory title under section 9(a) to everything the Manhattan Engineer District had held: all fissionable material, all atomic weapons and parts, and such other property in the custody or control of the District as the President might determine. It ran the born-secret information regime, it seated a Division of Military Application under a serving officer of the armed forces from the beginning, and it was abolished by the Energy Reorganization Act of 1974, its functions vesting in ERDA and passing from there to the Department of Energy.

### Why it is implicated

Three reasons, all statutory rather than testimonial. First, it is the only body in American law that has ever held a general custody title of the kind a recovery account requires, and the language of section 9(a) is broad enough to reach property that has nothing to do with fissionable material. Second, it ran the only classification regime in the United States where secrecy attaches by operation of statute rather than by an official's decision, so that a category of information is born classified and stays that way until the Commission affirmatively releases it. Third, it is the landlord: at CIA's request in 1955 it absorbed Groom Lake into its Nevada real estate, and the strip known by its map designation as Area 51 was added to the Nevada Test Site with the President's approval. The Commission's authority is the reason the most secure aviation site in the country sits inside an atomic reservation.

### The longer account

The Commission is on this board for what its statute permits, not for what any witness says it did. Section 9(a) transferred to it 'All fissionable material; all atomic weapons and parts thereof' and, in the same list, 'Such other property owned by or in the custody or control of the Manhattan Engineer District or other Government agencies as the President may determine.' That second clause is a custody instrument with no subject-matter limit in it. Any account that has recovered material moving into federal custody in the late 1940s has to explain which authority received it, and this is the authority whose founding statute was drafted to receive whatever the President said it should receive.

The classification regime is the second half of the same argument. Restricted data under the 1946 Act is 'all data concerning the manufacture or utilization of atomic weapons, the production of fissionable material, or the use of fissionable material in the production of power, but shall not include any data which the Commission from time to time determines may be published without adversely affecting the common defense and security.' The default runs the opposite way from every executive-order system on this board: information is secret because of what it is about, and it becomes public only by an affirmative act. A Division of Military Application, statutorily directed by a member of the armed forces, sat inside this civilian body from the first day. Civilian control with a uniformed division and a self-executing secrecy rule is a specific architecture, and it is the one the board keeps pointing at.

The geography closes the case for placement. CIA's own overhead reconnaissance history records that after the April 1955 overflight, Bissell found Groom Lake was not part of the proving ground, that he and Miller asked the Commission to add it, and that Chairman Lewis Strauss agreed. That is how the site got its jurisdiction. What the board declines to do is take the next step. Four guests across four Michels lead maps place the Commission at a custody point for recovered material. Nothing in either statute, and nothing in the National Archives description of Record Group 326, says anything of the kind. The node carries the statute, and the testimony stays off it.

### What it has denied

The Commission is not recorded on this board as having denied anything, and no denial link touches this node in either direction. Its disclosure letter is the lowest on the map for a reason that is architectural rather than behavioural: a body operating a born-secret regime does not need to deny, because the statute withholds by default and release requires an affirmative act the Commission alone can take. The board's discipline on this node runs the same way. Guest testimony placing it at a custody point for recovered material is not carried as a claim, because the statute and the archival record say nothing to support it.

Roles the board assigns it: custody, concealment, oversight.

### Open questions on this node

- Nothing in the 1946 Act, the 1954 Act or the National Archives description of Record Group 326 speaks to recovered non-nuclear material. The statute permits a custody the record does not evidence, and the board holds only the permission.
- Four guests across four Michels lead maps place the Commission at a custody point for recovered material. Those assertions are named in the node's own record and deliberately not carried as claims; no document has been opened that would raise or refute them.
- The Commission's own series inside Record Group 326 have not been examined. The board cites the record group description, not the records.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 129 of 1125, score 55). 9 connections, 3 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C2/A3/D1.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** A Division of Military Application under a serving officer was in the Commission from the start: the 1946 Act establishes it alongside Research, Production and Engineering and provides that 'The Director of the Division of Military Application shall be a member of the armed forces.' The 1954 Act carries it forward at section 25 with the Director required to be 'an active member of the Armed Forces.' Source: Atomic Energy Act of 1946, sec. 2(a)(4)(B), https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/STATUTE-60/pdf/STATUTE-60-Pg755.pdf ; Atomic Energy Act of 1954, Public Law 83-703, 68 Stat. 919, sec. 25, https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/STATUTE-68/pdf/STATUTE-68-Pg919.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** Classification runs by category rather than by decision. The 1946 Act defines restricted data as 'all data concerning the manufacture or utilization of atomic weapons, the production of fissionable material, or the use of fissionable material in the production of power, but shall not include any data which the Commission from time to time determines may be published without adversely affecting the common defense and security.' Source: Atomic Energy Act of 1946, Public Law 79-585, 60 Stat. 755, sec. 10(b)(1), https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/STATUTE-60/pdf/STATUTE-60-Pg755.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** The Commission absorbed Groom Lake into its Nevada real estate at CIA's request in 1955. The Agency's own overhead reconnaissance history records that after the April 1955 overflight Bissell found Groom Lake was not part of the proving ground, that he and Miller asked the Commission to add it, that Chairman Lewis Strauss agreed, and that Eisenhower approved the addition of the strip known by its map designation as Area 51 to the Nevada Test Site. Source: Pedlow and Welzenbach, The Central Intelligence Agency and Overhead Reconnaissance, 2013 release, chapter 2, https://archive.org/download/cia-readingroom-document-0000190094/0000190094_djvu.txt
- **Tier 1 documented.** The Commission gains its first named chairman on this board. The CIA's own declassified chronology records under January 1961: '16 JAN. Dr. Glenn T. Seaborg named AEC chairman, succeeding John A. McCone (confirmed by Senate 24 Feb).' McCone went from the Commission to the Directorship of Central Intelligence, and two years later Seaborg was writing to him there on Commission letterhead. No end to the chairmanship is established by any record opened for this pass. Source: CIA Historical Staff Chronology 1946-65, Volume II 1956-65, June 1970, CIA-RDP85B00803R000200050003-5, declassified in part 8 July 2013, https://archive.org/details/cia-readingroom-document-cia-rdp85b00803r000200050003-5
- **Tier 1 documented.** The Commission took statutory custody of the Manhattan Engineer District's holdings. Section 9(a) directs the transfer to the Commission of 'All fissionable material; all atomic weapons and parts thereof' and of 'Such other property owned by or in the custody or control of the Manhattan Engineer District or other Government agencies as the President may determine.' Source: Atomic Energy Act of 1946, Public Law 79-585, 60 Stat. 755, sec. 9(a), https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/STATUTE-60/pdf/STATUTE-60-Pg755.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** The Commission was abolished by statute: 'The Atomic Energy Commission is hereby abolished.' Its functions vested in the Administrator of the Energy Research and Development Administration, and the Act took effect 120 days after enactment or on an earlier date the President might prescribe. Source: Energy Reorganization Act of 1974, Public Law 93-438, 88 Stat. 1233, secs. 104(a) and 312(a), approved 11 October 1974, https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/STATUTE-88/pdf/STATUTE-88-Pg1233.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** The Commission was created by statute: 'There is hereby established an Atomic Energy Commission (herein called the Commission), which shall be composed of five members.' Members are appointed by the President by and with the advice and consent of the Senate. Source: Atomic Energy Act of 1946, Public Law 79-585, 60 Stat. 755, sec. 2(a), approved 1 August 1946, https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/STATUTE-60/pdf/STATUTE-60-Pg755.pdf
