## Lockheed Martin Advanced Development Programs (Skunk Works)

Lockheed Martin Advanced Development Programs (Skunk Works) is a institution on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 1943; axes C3 A3 D0; 9 connections; in the custody band.

In one line: Unacknowledged-program aeronautics organisation, Burbank then Palmdale.

### What it is

Lockheed Martin Advanced Development Programs, universally known as Skunk Works: the unacknowledged-program aeronautics organisation founded by Clarence 'Kelly' Johnson in 1943 at Burbank, California, and now resident at Air Force Plant 42, Palmdale, a government-owned contractor-operated facility. Ben Rich directed it from 1975 to 1991. Its programme list is the working definition of the black aircraft: the U-2, the A-12 and SR-71, the F-117, Sea Shadow, DarkStar and the RQ-170 Sentinel.

### Why it is implicated

Skunk Works is the operational proof of the board's central structural claim: that a private corporation can hold, fly and conceal hardware from everyone without a need to know, including senior officers in the chain of command, for years at a time. AARO's own Historical Record Report uses Skunk Works aircraft to explain a class of UAP reports, and in doing so concedes the point -- the organisation routinely fields flying objects whose existence the government denies while they are flying. As of the 1986 GAO pass it also carries a documented custody failure: the companion report to GAO's testimony of 24 July 1986 names the Lockheed/Burbank plant as the site where special-access document control deteriorated, and says the deterioration might not have happened had the Defense Investigative Service been permitted to inspect the contract as it inspected Lockheed's others.

### The longer account

The reason Skunk Works belongs at programme level on this board is not that anyone has documented anomalous material inside it. It is that Skunk Works settles an argument the board otherwise has to make by inference. The standing objection to every legacy-programme account is that a secret of this size could not be kept -- too many people, too many decades, too many contractors. Skunk Works is the counter-example the United States government publishes about itself: the RQ-170 flew operationally for years before Lockheed acknowledged it existed, and the acknowledgment came after a loss on foreign soil rather than on a schedule anyone chose. The concealment worked until physics interrupted it.

What the 1986 GAO material adds is the failure mode. Concealment of that quality is not free, and the price is paid in accountability: 1,460 discrepancies in a 40,000-item special-access inventory at one company, 11 still unresolved, discovered because a congressional subcommittee asked rather than because the security system surfaced it. GAO's own explanation of why nobody caught it earlier is the sentence this board keeps returning to -- special access contracts are 'carved out of the Defense Investigative Service's periodic inspection program', by a 1965 decision, and by 1983 there were probably several thousand of them.

The organisation therefore sits on this board twice over: as the demonstration that unacknowledged hardware can be held indefinitely, and as the documented case where holding it meant nobody could say where the paper went.

### What it has denied

Skunk Works does not deny; it declines to confirm, and the record shows the decline holding for years at a time and then ending on someone else's timetable. The RQ-170 is the canonical instance: operational, photographed, and unacknowledged until it was lost abroad. The 1986 GAO exchange is the same posture at the paperwork layer -- GAO was granted access on condition that it not name the programme, the component or the service, so the public record of the failure exists with the subject of the failure redacted out of it. The board treats that as a documented behaviour rather than an inference, because in both cases the concealment and its terms are described in a government document.

Roles the board assigns it: concealment, custody, research.

### Open questions on this node

- GAO/NSIAD-88-2BR, 'Information Security: Actions Taken To Improve Lockheed's Special Access Document Accountability', is the follow-up that would say whether anything changed after 1986. It appeared in a search result only and has not been opened; nothing on this board is cited to it.
- The 1986 testimony and its companion report never name the special access programme itself, by agreement with DoD. Whether that programme has since been acknowledged, and under what name, is not established from any document read here.
- Ben Rich's post-retirement remarks about technologies 'locked up in black projects' remain the most quoted and least documented artefact in this field. No primary recording or contemporaneous transcript has been located, and the board carries no Tier 1 or Tier 2 claim resting on them.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 190 of 1125, score 46). 9 connections, 3 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C3/A3/D0.

- **Tier 1 documented.** GAO/NSIAD-86-191 of 7 August 1986 names 'the Lockheed/Burbank plant' as the location of the special access contract whose document accountability deteriorated, and states the deterioration 'may not have occurred if the Defense Investigative Service had been permitted to conduct semiannual inspections of the contract, as it does with other contracts at Lockheed.' Source: GAO/NSIAD-86-191, Information Security: Need for DOD Inspections of Special Access Contracts, B-223830, 7 August 1986, https://www.gao.gov/assets/nsiad-86-191.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** Lockheed Martin's Advanced Development Program organisation, also known as Skunk Works, developed the RQ-170 Sentinel and the DarkStar; AARO cites these programs as sources of UAP misidentification. Source: AARO, Historical Record Report Volume I, March 2024, Section on classified aeronautics
- **Tier 1 documented.** Lockheed Martin's Advanced Development Programs organisation developed the RQ-170 Sentinel and the DarkStar, and AARO cites Skunk Works aircraft as the explanation for a class of UAP reports. Source: GAO accession 130581, statement of Martin M. Ferber, 24 July 1986, footnote, https://www.gao.gov/assets/130581.pdf

## Sandia National Laboratories

Sandia National Laboratories is a institution on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1949; axes C3 A3 D1; 8 connections; in the custody band.

In one line: DOE national laboratory, Albuquerque, NM.

### What it is

Sandia National Laboratories, a Department of Energy federally funded research and development centre in Albuquerque, administered by National Technology and Engineering Solutions of Sandia, LLC since 1 May 2017. It is the largest FFRDC in the United States by funding, at approximately 4,584.0 million dollars in FY2024, and it sits adjacent to Kirtland Air Force Base, the installation that contains the Manzano Weapons Storage Area and Coyote Canyon.

### Why it is implicated

The geography does most of the work, and it puts Sandia on this board twice over. In 1948 and 1949 the green fireballs that triggered the Los Alamos conference and Project Twinkle were concentrated over the atomic weapons installations, Los Alamos and Sandia among them. In August 1980 the best-documented American military UFO incident of the Cold War occurred on the adjoining base, and a Sandia security guard is a reporting party in the resulting Air Force Office of Special Investigations file. Thirty years later, three Sandia staff members were among the five outside reviewers of the FY09 AAWSAP technical reports, which is a DOE weapons laboratory peer-reviewing the physics portfolio of a Defense Intelligence Agency programme. The laboratory also appears in the custody record of an earlier document: the distribution list of AFSWC TR-59-39 sends a copy to the President of Sandia Corporation, attention Document Control Division.

### The longer account

The 1980 file is the one to read. AFOSI Special Agent Richard C. Doty's complaint form of 9 September 1980, District File No. 8017D93-0/29, records that three members of the 1608 Security Police Squadron, SSgt Stephen Ferenz, ATC Martin W. Rist and AMN Anthony D. Frazier, observed a bright light near the Manzano restricted test range on the night of 8 August 1980 that made stop-and-go manoeuvres, landed in Coyote Canyon and departed vertically at high speed. The same file records a Sandia security guard observing a round disc-shaped object near an alarmed structure at about 00:20 on 9 August, his radio malfunctioning during the encounter.

What makes the file structurally interesting rather than merely anecdotal is that a contractor-operated national laboratory, an Air Force counterintelligence office and a nuclear weapons storage site all appear in one anomalous-object document. What makes it uncomfortable is what the same office and the same agent did next. Doty was the conduit for fabricated material supplied to Paul Bennewitz, the Albuquerque physicist whose company Thunder Scientific operated adjacent to Kirtland and who had licensed sensor technology from Sandia in 1966; the operation ended in Bennewitz's psychiatric collapse. The board grades that Tier 2 and attributes it to AFOSI, not to the laboratory.

The 2009 material is a different register and is worth keeping separate from the 1980 material. DIA memo U-429-09-DWO-IM of 30 October 2009 records the outside review of the AAWSAP deliverables, with three of the five reviewers from Sandia, and DIA recorded the reviews as positive. The board's reading of the timing is that DIA sought institutional cover for a portfolio it expected to have to defend, which it did five weeks later, and that reading is graded Tier 3 because it is an inference from sequence rather than anything the memo says.

### What it has denied

The board records no denial issued by Sandia and none directed at it. That absence should be read carefully rather than charitably: the laboratory appears in the 1980 file as a reporting party, not as a respondent, and nothing here shows it was ever asked to account for what its guard saw. The deception in this story belongs to the Air Force counterintelligence office on the adjoining base, and the board keeps the attribution where the documents put it. What Sandia has not done, on any record held here, is say anything at all about August 1980.

Roles the board assigns it: research, custody, concealment.

### Open questions on this node

- The Sandia security guard of 9 August 1980 is described in the AFOSI file but the board does not hold his name, and no Sandia-side incident record from that night has been located.
- The AEC-lineage custody link is the weakest thing attached to this node. It rests on a November 2025 video investigation citing Edgar Fouche and a former New Mexico state representative, and is graded Tier 2 on that basis. No primary document supports it here.
- The identities of the three Sandia reviewers of the FY09 AAWSAP deliverables are not in the DIA memo as held, and nothing on this board establishes which reports they reviewed.

## Air Technical Intelligence Center

Air Technical Intelligence Center is a institution on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1951; axes C3 A3 D0; 7 connections; in the custody band.

In one line: USAF technical intelligence centre, Wright-Patterson AFB.

### What it is

The Air Technical Intelligence Center, formed in 1951 out of Air Materiel Command's T-2 intelligence organisation at Wright Field and resident at Wright-Patterson Air Force Base, Ohio. It was the United States' centre for the exploitation of foreign aerospace technology, the shop that processed the Operation Paperclip haul and took captured airframes apart. Every official American UFO project of the legacy era was hosted here: SIGN from 1948, GRUDGE from 1949 and Blue Book from 1952. It issued Project Blue Book Special Report No. 14 on 5 May 1955 under Project 10073, Study No. 102-EL-55/2-79, and both editions of AFR 200-2 name it as the Air Force activity responsible for conducting analysis of information and evidence. It became the Foreign Technology Division in 1961 and later the National Air and Space Intelligence Center, which is still at the base.

### Why it is implicated

The implicating fact is the assignment, not an allegation. The United States did not send unidentified flying objects to a science agency, to an academic body or to an air-defence command; it sent them to the organisation whose standing function was to answer one question about recovered hardware, which is what it is and how it works. That placement is usually reported as an administrative detail and is the structural point of this whole domain. The board also carries a Tier 2 custody link on the same ground: Howard McCoy ran AMC intelligence from 1945 to 1951, the organisation that received and exploited recovered foreign materiel and that hosted Project SIGN, so any 1947 recovery would have been processed at this address. And ATIC's own 1947-1951 T-2 intelligence files have never been subject to a comprehensive declassification review.

### The longer account

Read the regulation rather than the folklore and the placement is explicit. AFR 200-2 of 26 August 1953, paragraph 4a, says that 'The Air Technical Intelligence Center (ATIC), Wright-Patterson Air Force Base, Ohio, has been designated the Air Force activity responsible for conducting analysis of information and evidence.' The 1954 edition keeps the same terminal position, taking reports from base level through the Air Defense Command and the 4602d Air Intelligence Service Squadron and ending at ATIC.

One correction this board has had to make to its own corpus belongs here, because the mistake is easy and it repeats. The claim that AFR 200-2 routed reports to ATIC rather than to Blue Book is a category error. Project Blue Book was a project inside ATIC at Wright-Patterson; naming ATIC is naming Blue Book's host, and the 1953 regulation does not mention Blue Book at all in either edition.

The second thing the record establishes is that ATIC's analytical work was not confined to reports of things other people saw. TR-AC-47, the joint report on Project Silver Bug of 15 February 1955, carries the Air Technical Intelligence Center imprint on its title page, and its own conclusion asks for direct liaison between WADC and ATIC personnel. That is the same organisation, in the same year it published the largest statistical study of UFO reports the United States has ever produced, working with the Air Force's development command on a circular-planform aircraft.

### What it has denied

ATIC is not recorded on this board issuing a denial of its own, and its D axis is 0. Its posture is structural rather than spoken: the regulation that named it the analysis authority is the same instrument that governed what could be said publicly, so the centre held the evidence and the parent service held the microphone. That division is worth stating plainly, because it means an absence of ATIC denials is not an absence of denial. The statements this board grades as directed denials in the legacy era are the Air Force's, and they are carried on the usaf node.

Roles the board assigns it: custody, research, concealment.

### Open questions on this node

- ATIC's 1947-1951 T-2 intelligence files have never been subject to a comprehensive declassification review. Nothing on this board establishes what is in them.
- The 1954 edition of AFR 200-2 is carried here from a transcription at cufon.org rather than from a government scan. The 1953 edition has a four-page image scan behind it; the 1954 one does not.
- The board holds no document establishing what ATIC did with any recovered materiel, only that it was the organisation designated to receive and analyse evidence. The custody link through McCoy is graded Tier 2 for that reason.

## EG&G (Edgerton, Germeshausen and Grier)

EG&G (Edgerton, Germeshausen and Grier) is a institution on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1947; axes C3 A3 D0; 7 connections; in the custody band.

In one line: Nuclear test instrumentation and classified site services; Groom Lake support.

### What it is

EG&G, Edgerton, Germeshausen and Grier, was founded in 1947 by three MIT-connected figures whose wartime work instrumented and photographed nuclear detonations, and became the Atomic Energy Commission's principal test-instrumentation contractor at the Nevada Test Site. Its Las Vegas-based Special Projects division provided engineering, technical, radar and logistical support to classified programs at the Air Force operating location near Groom Lake and operated the unmarked passenger airline known as Janet from a dedicated terminal at McCarran International Airport. The name and the contracts have since passed through the Carlyle Group, URS Corporation, AECOM and Amentum.

### Why it is implicated

EG&G is the connective tissue of the American black-site system: the same firm that instrumented nuclear tests ran the workforce airlift, the technical support and the site services that make an unacknowledged installation function. Its employees are the population at the center of the only litigation that has ever forced a court to decide what legal protection a denied facility enjoys. When Groom Lake workers sued in August 1994 alleging illegal open-air burning of hazardous waste, the Secretary of the Air Force filed a formal military and state secrets privilege claim covering categories that expressly included "security sensitive environmental data"; the President signed an annual RCRA exemption for "the United States Air Force's operating location near Groom Lake, Nevada"; and the Ninth Circuit affirmed dismissal, holding that seemingly innocuous information forming part of a classified mosaic may be withheld and that a court cannot order the government to disentangle it. That sequence, contractor workforce, denied facility, state secrets, presidential exemption, dismissal, is the complete legal template a legacy program would require, and it is fully documented in the Federal Register and the Federal Reporter.

### The longer account

Harold "Doc" Edgerton, Kenneth Germeshausen and Herbert Grier came out of MIT and out of the Manhattan Project's photographic and timing instrumentation work, and in 1947 incorporated the company that carried their initials. EG&G's core business for decades was the measurement of nuclear detonations for the Atomic Energy Commission, high-speed photography, timing and firing, diagnostics, making it one of very few private firms holding continuous clearances into the most sensitive American technical programs. From that base it grew into site services, security support, range operations and personnel logistics across the Nevada complex.

The most visible artifact is the airline. EG&G Special Projects operated the passenger service, universally known by its Janet callsigns, that moves the workforce from Las Vegas to the Air Force operating location at Groom Lake and to the Tonopah Test Range. A Nevada Department of Transportation diagram for McCarran dated May 1999 labels the building on the west side of the field the "EG&G Airlift Terminal." Reporting places the first Las Vegas-to-Groom flights in 1972 with a Douglas DC-6, and current operations at roughly twenty round trips per weekday carrying one to one and a half thousand workers on unmarked aircraft. This is what a routinized, industrial-scale commute to an unacknowledged facility looks like, and it has looked like that for over fifty years.

The legal history is the part that matters most for this board. In August 1994, Professor Jonathan Turley of the National Law Center at George Washington University filed two suits based on field research by the Project on Government Oversight: Frost v. Perry against the Secretary of Defense and Kasza v. Browner against the EPA Administrator, both in the District of Nevada, both under the citizen-suit provisions of the Resource Conservation and Recovery Act. Helen Frost was the widow of a former worker at the site; Walter Kasza was another. Six plaintiffs were permitted to proceed under pseudonyms because, in the district court's finding, "there is a substantial danger here that the Plaintiffs will be subject to retaliation by the government or possible physical attack and harassment from third parties in Nevada."

The government's response was not to deny the allegations. Once discovery began, the Air Force refused to furnish almost all requested information on privilege grounds, and Secretary of the Air Force Sheila Widnall invoked a formal claim of military and state secrets privilege over categories of national security information associated with the operating location near Groom Lake, "specifically including '[s]ecurity sensitive environmental data.'" The claim was supported by an unclassified declaration and a classified declaration reviewed in camera, and the court also reviewed in camera a classified declaration by Air Force Vice Chief of Staff General Thomas S. Moorman, Jr. The district court found the privilege properly invoked and held that it covered the requested information "even though her need for discovery was compelling." The Air Force then moved for summary judgment on the ground that the privilege barred any evidence tending to confirm or disprove that hazardous waste had ever been generated, stored or disposed of at the location, an essential element of every claim. Summary judgment was granted.

On the EPA side, the district court held that RCRA itself contains no exception for disclosure of classified information, but that the President may exempt a federal facility under 42 U.S.C. 6961(a), and gave the Administrator time to obtain one. On 29 September 1995 the President issued Presidential Determination No. 95-45, published at 60 Federal Register 52823, which reads in operative part: "I find that it is in the paramount interest of the United States to exempt the United States Air Force's operating location near Groom Lake, Nevada (the subject of litigation in Kasza v. Browner (D. Nev. CV-S-94-795-PMP) and Frost v. Perry (D. Nev. CV-S-94-714-PMP)) from any applicable requirement for the disclosure to unauthorized persons of classified information concerning that operating location." It exempted the location from "any Federal, State, interstate or local provision respecting control and abatement of solid waste or hazardous waste disposal that would require the disclosure of classified information concerning that operating location to any unauthorized person," and stated: "This exemption shall be effective for the full one-year statutory period." One year. It must be renewed, and it has been.

The Ninth Circuit decided the consolidated appeals on 8 January 1998. Kasza v. Browner, 133 F.3d 1159, cert. denied, 525 U.S. 967 (1998). The panel held that the state secrets privilege is absolute; that "if seemingly innocuous information is part of a classified mosaic, the state secrets privilege may be invoked to bar its disclosure and the court cannot order the government to disentangle this information from other classified information"; and that RCRA section 6001 does not preempt the privilege, "we discern no Congressional intent to replace the government's evidentiary privilege to withhold sensitive information in litigation by providing an exemption from RCRA's regulation of hazardous waste." The Supreme Court declined review.

What that sequence establishes is not that anything exotic was buried in the Groom Lake trenches. It establishes that a contractor-operated installation the government declines to name can be placed outside an ordinary environmental statute by a one-page presidential memorandum; that a cabinet secretary's declaration can foreclose discovery of the physical facts; that federal courts will accept a mosaic theory under which even innocuous data is protected; and that the workers who get sick have no remedy. Every element a concealed recovery and exploitation effort would need to survive litigation was demonstrated in public between 1994 and 1998.

The corporate lineage matters for tracking who holds the contracts today. EG&G's technical services business was acquired in August 1999 by a Carlyle Group entity and operated as EG&G Technical Services; URS Corporation acquired it in August 2002; URS was absorbed by AECOM in 2014; and the Janet operation is reported to have passed with AECOM's federal business to Amentum. The initials survive on the fuselages of nothing, but the function has never been interrupted.

### What it has denied

Neither EG&G nor its successors has ever publicly described the Groom Lake work. The government's posture in the Frost and Kasza litigation was not denial but non-cognizability: it did not deny that hazardous waste had been burned, it asserted that the existence or non-existence of such information is a state secret. Presidential Determination 95-45 preserves that posture explicitly, stating that nothing in it is intended to "imply that in the absence of such a Presidential exemption, the Resource Conservation and Recovery Act (RCRA) or any other provision of law permits or requires disclosure of classified information to unauthorized persons."

Roles the board assigns it: custody, concealment, research.

### Open questions on this node

- Whether the Presidential Determination has been reissued in every year since 1995 and under which presidents; the determination is expressly one-year and a complete Federal Register series has not been assembled here.
- Which corporate entity currently holds the Groom Lake support and airlift contracts, under what contract number, and at what value, none of which appears in FPDS or any public award database.
- Whether EG&G or its successors have ever held a waived DoD special access program contract, which would be recorded on a classified DD Form 254 provided to the cognizant security office under DoDI 5205.11 and not otherwise disclosed.
- What the classified Widnall and Moorman declarations actually say about activities at the location; they remain sealed.
- Whether Robert Lazar's claim of an EG&G-mediated hiring process for work at S-4 in 1988-89 can be corroborated by any employment, payroll or badge record; no such record has been produced.
- Whether any of the missing or deceased individuals tracked on this board were EG&G, URS, AECOM or Amentum employees, and whether their assignments touched the Nevada complex.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 146 of 1125, score 53). 7 connections, 1 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 1 official denial recorded against it. Axes C3/A3/D0.

## Los Alamos National Laboratory

Los Alamos National Laboratory is a institution on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 1943; axes C2 A3 D1; 6 connections; in the custody band.

In one line: DOE FFRDC administered by Triad National Security, LLC since 9 June 2018; Los Alamos, NM.

### What it is

Los Alamos National Laboratory, the Department of Energy federally funded research and development centre at Los Alamos, New Mexico, administered by Triad National Security, LLC since 9 June 2018 and receiving approximately $3,918.3 million in FY2024. It is the laboratory that built the first nuclear weapons, and on this board it appears in three unrelated capacities: as the host of a documented 1949 conference on the green fireballs, as an institution whose own archivist says it fields several UFO-related FOIA requests a year, and as the workplace named in the single most contested credential claim in the subject.

### Why it is implicated

It joined this map with the Lazar cluster of 18 September 2026, and it is the node where that cluster's central factual question actually lives. Bob Lazar's account requires a documented scientific career, and the one contemporaneous document that appears to supply it is a Los Alamos artefact: the 27 June 1982 front page of the Los Alamos Monitor, which reports on him as a laboratory-connected physicist, and a 1982 internal telephone directory that lists his name. Both are real, both were opened, and both are narrower than the use made of them. The laboratory is therefore not a bystander here. It is the place where the strongest evidence for the claimant and the strongest qualification of that evidence sit on the same pair of pages.

### The longer account

The board carries this laboratory for the ordinary reasons first. It hosted a conference on 16 February 1949 on the green fireballs, attended by Laboratory Director Norris Bradbury and Frederick Reines among others, which is documented by the laboratory itself rather than by the literature about it. Its senior archivist has said on the record that the institution receives several FOIA requests a year on unidentified aerial phenomena, which is a rare thing for an institution to say about itself and is the reason a `custody` reading of this node is defensible at all.

What brought it onto the top-level map is narrower and sharper. The Lazar account stands or falls on whether he was what he said he was, and the only contemporaneous documentary thread in his favour runs through here. On 27 June 1982 the Los Alamos Monitor put him on its front page. A 1982 internal telephone directory lists his name. Taken together they are routinely presented as proof of employment at the laboratory.

They are not that, and this node exists partly to say so precisely. The directory's own terms state that it covers outside contractors as well as staff, and a 1996 taped interview with a former colleague places him under Kirk-Mayer, a contractor, which is why `kirk-mayer` is now a node in its own right with an edge to this one. The board's rule, written as a claim on this node rather than left to a reader to work out, is that his connection is to be stated as named in a directory and never as employed by. That is a smaller claim than either his supporters or his critics usually make, and it is the one the documents support.

The laboratory has made no statement about him in anything opened here, and none is asserted. The edge from this node to `bob-lazar` is Tier 3 for exactly that reason.

Roles the board assigns it: research, custody, disclosure.

### Open questions on this node

- The 1982 Los Alamos telephone directory page itself, as an image. It is described and quoted in the secondary literature but was not opened as a scan, so its exact wording and the placement of his entry are not established here.
- Any laboratory or Kirk-Mayer personnel record for Bob Lazar. None was opened, and the board asserts no employment either way.
- Whether the laboratory has ever made any statement about him. Nothing opened here contains one.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 245 of 1125, score 42). 6 connections, 1 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C2/A3/D1.

# 1970-1999: Post-Blue Book

## DoD Special Access Program Central Office (DoD SAPCO)

DoD Special Access Program Central Office (DoD SAPCO) is a institution on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1994; axes C3 A3 D2; 18 connections; in the custody band.

In one line: Central management office for all DoD special access programs; reports to the Deputy Secretary of Defense.

### What it is

The DoD Special Access Program Central Office is the headquarters element that runs the Department of Defense's entire special access program enterprise. Its Director operates under the authority, direction and control of the Deputy Secretary of Defense, reports directly to the Deputy Secretary, serves as Executive Secretary of the Special Access Program Oversight Committee and Senior Review Group and Chair of the SAP Senior Working Group, and holds delegated TOP SECRET original classification authority for all DoD SAP information. It is also the Department's single point of contact for SAP matters with the Executive, Judicial and Legislative branches.

### Why it is implicated

If a legacy program exists inside the Department of Defense, DoD SAPCO is the office that knows where it sits in the architecture, who is read in, and what Congress has been told. Three provisions of the governing issuances make this office load-bearing rather than merely administrative. First, DoDD 5205.07 paragraph 3.1.g bars any DoD contractor entity, employee, representative or consultant from providing SAP material to any member of Congress or their staff without the Director's approval, a regulatory chokepoint sitting directly across the path of every contractor whistleblower. Second, the Director accredits congressional SAP facilities and "maintains records of DoD SAP information provided to Congress," which makes SAPCO both the conduit and the recordkeeper of its own conduct. Third, under DoDI 5205.11 the Director may approve the establishment and disestablishment of SAPs at tier 2 and below on his own authority with SSWG concurrence, meaning a single flag officer or SES can create and dissolve compartments. In March 2023 the then-Director, Major General David W. Abba, signed the memorandum that authorized AARO to receive sensitive U.S. Government information and effectively released NDA-bound personnel to speak to AARO, the single most consequential document in the government's handling of the whistleblower question, and it came from this office.

### The longer account

DoD Directive 5205.07, "Special Access Program Policy," was reissued on 12 September 2024 over the signature of Deputy Secretary of Defense Kathleen H. Hicks. It cancels the July 1, 2010 version and, importantly, incorporates and cancels the Deputy Secretary of Defense Memorandum of July 11, 2023, "Implementation Guidance for DoD Special Access Program Enterprise Reform." That reform memorandum was issued three days before the Schumer-Rounds UAP Disclosure Act was unveiled and eight days before the Senate added it to the NDAA. The companion issuance, DoD Instruction 5205.11, was reissued the same day.

The Directive creates four governance bodies and one office. The SAP Oversight Committee is chaired by the Deputy Secretary of Defense and includes the four Under Secretaries who serve as SAP oversight authorities, Research and Engineering, Acquisition and Sustainment, Policy, and Intelligence and Security, plus the Comptroller, the Vice Chairman of the Joint Chiefs, the DoD General Counsel, the Director of Cost Assessment and Program Evaluation, the DoD Chief Information Officer, the Under Secretaries of the Army, Navy and Air Force, the ASD for Special Operations and Low-Intensity Conflict, the Vice Chiefs of the Army and Air Force, the Assistant Commandant of the Marine Corps, and the Vice Chiefs of Naval and Space Operations. The Director of DoD SAPCO is its Executive Secretary. Below it sits the Senior Review Group, which the Directive pointedly says "has no designated individual as the Chair," and below that the SAP Senior Working Group, chaired by the Director of DoD SAPCO, whose voting members are the SAPCO directors of the four oversight-authority offices, the military departments, SOLIC, and the Joint Staff.

The Director's own portfolio in section 2.1 of the Directive is broader than the governance role. He is the "DoD-designated proponent" for SAP policy; the oversight authority for SAPs designated by the Deputy Secretary, "including those protecting capabilities and information shared with the DoD by foreign governments"; the cognizant authority for SAPs designated by the Deputy Secretary, for DoD components not supported by another SAPCO, for foreign-government-shared capabilities, and for "SAP studies, portfolios, and associated access management structures involving DoD SAPs"; and the DoD primary point of contact for SAP matters with all three branches of government, "including support to SAP congressional meetings, hearings, and responses to congressional inquiries." He is also Executive Secretary of the Special Technical Activities Oversight Committee established by a Deputy Secretary of Defense memorandum of 21 November 2020. He publishes and maintains security classification guides for all DoD SAP umbrellas and is delegated TOP SECRET original classification authority for all DoD SAP information for that purpose. He maintains the single authoritative repository of SAP personnel access and facility accreditation. He accredits congressional SAP facilities and maintains the records of what SAP information Congress received.

Paragraph 3.1.g of the Directive is the sentence to underline: "All personnel accessed to DoD SAPs will notify the Director, DoD SAPCO and OA SAPCO directors through their SAP CA SAPCO in a timely fashion when briefing or providing DoD SAP material to any member of Congress or their staff. No DoD contractor entity, contractor employee, contractor representative, or consultant will provide SAP material to any members of Congress or their staff without Director, DoD SAPCO approval." A contractor employee who believes a program is being illegally concealed from Congress must, under this rule, ask the office that administers that program for permission to tell Congress about it.

DoDI 5205.11 supplies the operating mechanics. Only the Secretary or Deputy Secretary may alter the tier-1 architecture; the Director of DoD SAPCO may approve establishment and disestablishment of SAPs at tier 2 and below. New CPI enters through a "SAP project" assigned a number in the TR umbrella, and if a decision is not reached in 210 days it transfers automatically toward termination absent a waiver from the Secretary or Deputy Secretary. Access Approval Authority for non-waived SAPs may be delegated to component SAPCO staff; "AAA for waived SAPs may be delegated to the SAPCO Deputy Director or alternate designee." DoD SAPCO coordinates on personnel access requests for judicial and other federal agency personnel for waived programs, that is, the office decides which judges and which agencies get read in. The SAPOC's mandatory annual review must assess, among other things, "All waived DoD SAPs to determine the need for continued waived status."

The office intersects the UAP record at three documented points. In March 2023 Major General David W. Abba, then Director of DoD SAPCO, signed a memorandum, released to The Black Vault in FOIA case 24-F-0266, stating that AARO was authorized to receive information relating to sensitive U.S. Government information and activities, the instrument on which AARO's repeated attempts to interview David Grusch rested. On 17 April 2024 Abba appeared alongside acting AARO director Tim Phillips in a classified UAP briefing to members of Congress. And in July 2024 former AARO director Sean Kirkpatrick described briefing and testifying to members of Congress "with the SAPCO director and the CAPCO director from ODNI," saying that they had gone through everything and that none of the alleged hidden programs were what witnesses claimed.

That last item is the denial, and it is a good one to catalogue precisely because of who is standing next to whom. The office that administers waived SAPs, the office that controls contractor communication with Congress, and the office that keeps the records of what Congress was told, went to Congress to say there is nothing there.

### What it has denied

DoD SAPCO does not speak publicly under its own name. Its position enters the record through others: the Abba memorandum authorizing AARO to receive sensitive information, and Kirkpatrick's account of the SAPCO director appearing alongside him before Congress to say that every alleged hidden program had been checked, was something else, and had already been reported.

Roles the board assigns it: concealment, oversight, custody.

### Open questions on this node

- Whether DoD SAPCO's records of 'DoD SAP information provided to Congress' have ever been examined by an independent body, and whether they would show what the twelve recipients of any waived-SAP notification actually received.
- The full text and classification markings of the March 2023 Abba memorandum, and whether it authorized disclosure to AARO of waived-SAP information or only of non-waived material.
- Whether any request has been made under DoDD 5205.07 para 3.1.g by a contractor employee seeking approval to take SAP material to Congress, and how such requests are adjudicated and logged.
- Who succeeded Abba as Director of DoD SAPCO and what that officer has told Congress about UAP.
- What the July 11, 2023 SAP Enterprise Reform memorandum actually directed, given that it has now been cancelled and absorbed into DoDD 5205.07 without separate publication.
- Which judicial personnel have been granted access to waived DoD SAPs, and in connection with which litigation.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 50 of 1125, score 78). 20 connections, 4 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 2 official denials recorded against it. Axes C3/A3/D2.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** AARO states it 'established a partnership with the Special Access Program Control Offices for the DoD, IC, and DHS to review programs identified in interviews by name or description' and that 'AARO investigators have been granted full access to all pertinent sensitive USG programs'. Source: AARO Historical Record Report Volume I, p. 34

Hicks; it reissues and cancels the July 1, 2010 directive and incorporates and cancels the Deputy Secretary of Defense Memorandum, "Implementation Guidance for DoD Special Access Program Enterprise Reform," July 11, 2023. - **Tier 1 documented.** DoDD 5205.07 (12 September 2024) establishes DoD SAPCO 'under the authority, direction, and control of the Deputy Secretary of Defense', with a Director who must be a general officer, flag officer or SES member reporting directly to DEPSECDEF. Source: DoD Directive 5205.07, 12 September 2024
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoDD 5205.07 paragraph 1.2.e provides that the Director, DoD SAPCO, "under the authority, direction, and control of the Deputy Secretary of Defense, will be a general officer, flag officer, or Senior Executive Service member or equivalent appointed to oversee day-to-day management of the SAP Enterprise. The Director, DoD SAPCO reports directly to the Deputy Secretary of Defense." Source: https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodd/520507p.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoDD 5205.07 paragraph 2.1.a(5) makes the Director the "DoD primary point of contact for SAP matters with agencies of the Executive Branch, the Judicial Branch, and the Legislative Branch, including support to SAP congressional meetings, hearings, and responses to congressional inquiries, unless otherwise specified by statute or regulation." Source: https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodd/520507p.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoDD 5205.07 paragraph 2.1.b provides: "The Director, DoD SAPCO has jurisdiction over the protection of information contained within DoD SAPs and is delegated TOP SECRET original classification authority for all DoD SAP information for purposes of issuing SAP security classification guides." Source: https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodd/520507p.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoDD 5205.07 paragraph 2.1.e requires the Director to accredit congressional SAP facilities and to "maintain records of DoD SAP information provided to Congress", making the same office the conduit for, and the recordkeeper of, what Congress is told. Source: https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodd/520507p.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoDD 5205.07 paragraph 3.1.g provides: "No DoD contractor entity, contractor employee, contractor representative, or consultant will provide SAP material to any members of Congress or their staff without Director, DoD SAPCO approval." Source: https://www.esd.whs.mil/Portals/54/Documents/DD/issuances/dodd/520507p.pdf
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoDD 5205.07 requires personnel to notify the Director, DoD SAPCO and the OA SAPCO directors when briefing Congress or congressional staff with SAP material, and bars contractors from providing such material without specific approval. Source: DoD Directive 5205.07, 12 September 2024
- **Tier 1 documented.** DoDI 5205.11 assigns DoD SAPCO the duty to notify the congressional defense committees of changes to CPI protected by DoD SAPs, and the intelligence committees for intelligence or intelligence-related CPI, and to report proposed classification changes or declassifications with a description, the reasons, and notice of any planned public announcement. - **Tier 1 documented.** DoDI 5205.11 paragraph 1.2.b provides that only the Secretary or Deputy Secretary of Defense may alter the tier-1 SAP architecture, while "The Director, DoD Special Access Program Central Office (SAPCO) may approve establishment and disestablishment of DoD SAPs at tiers 2 and below." - **Tier 1 documented.** DoDI 5205.11 provides that Access Approval Authority for non-waived SAPs may be delegated to component SAPCO staff, while "AAA for waived SAPs may be delegated to the SAPCO Deputy Director or alternate designee," and that "DoD SAPCO will coordinate on PARs for judicial and other federal agency personnel for waived programs." ## Operação Prato (Operation Saucer)

Operação Prato (Operation Saucer) is a program on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1977-10-20 to 1978; axes C2 A2 D2; 9 connections; in the custody band.

In one line: Brazilian Air Force I COMAR (Belem/PA) intelligence-section operation, Colares, Para, 1977-1978; documents held in the Arquivo Nacional Fundo OVNI, BR DFANBSB ARX.

### What it is

Operacao Prato, literally Operation Plate and usually rendered Operation Saucer, was a field operation mounted by the Forca Aerea Brasileira in the state of Para in 1977 and 1978, centred on Colares and neighbouring riverine communities on the Atlantic coast of the Amazon delta. It was commanded by Capitao Uyrange Bolivar Soares Nogueira de Hollanda Lima, who named it, and ran under I COMAR with involvement from the Servico Nacional de Informacoes. Its stated object was to investigate a wave of reports of luminous objects that residents said projected beams of light at them, a phenomenon locals called the chupa-chupa.

### Why it is implicated

Operacao Prato is the specific thing the United States has never produced: a military UAP field operation with a name, a commanding officer identified by rank and full name, a defined area of operations, dated phases, a case count, a written final report, and a paper trail that a member of the public can now request from a civilian archive. The US analogue would be if Project Blue Book had deployed officers into a county for two months with cameras, and the resulting negatives, logs and the captain's signed report were downloadable from NARA. Everything about the operation that would in the American context be cited as a reason for permanent classification, that it was run under a military dictatorship, that it involved a domestic intelligence service, that it concerned civilians who claimed physical injury, and that it produced imagery, turned out not to prevent release. That is the contrast. The concealment argument in the US rests on the proposition that this class of record cannot be released; Brazil released this class of record, and the state did not fall.

### The longer account

In the second half of 1977 the coastal and riverine municipalities of Para began generating a volume of reports that local authorities could not absorb. Residents described luminous objects hovering over houses at night and directing narrow beams of light at people, and a hospital in Colares filled with patients presenting with burns, puncture-like marks and profound weakness. The mayor of Colares appealed for help. What the Brazilian state did next is what puts this node on the board: it sent the air force.

Operacao Prato was organised out of I COMAR, the First Regional Air Command in Belem, and placed under Capitao Uyrange Hollanda. It ran in phases across the last months of 1977 and into 1978, with a team on the ground carrying cameras and observation logs, and it operated in coordination with the Servico Nacional de Informacoes, the dictatorship's civilian intelligence service. Case counts in the surviving documentation are substantial. Material now in the Arquivo Nacional includes a compilation described as Registros de Observacoes de OVNI, drawn up from data gathered by I COMAR on the recommendation of the Minister of Aeronautics, forwarding 130 OVNI records catalogued between 2 September 1977 and 28 November 1978. Alongside the paper there are photographs and drawings, and elsewhere in the same fund the archive holds audio recordings of exchanges between pilots and control tower operators.

The written product of the operation was a report signed by Hollanda that recorded no evidence of extraterrestrials. That report is the operation's official position and, on the board's own method, it is the document that must be handled as an artefact rather than as an answer. It is dated, it is signed, it is attributable, and it was produced by an officer inside a chain of command under a military government. Twenty years later Hollanda repudiated it. In August 1997, at Cabo Frio in Rio de Janeiro state, he gave a long interview to the researchers A. J. Gevaerd and Marco Petit for Revista UFO in which he described what he said the team had actually encountered in the field, including close-quarters encounters, and described unexplained phenomena in his own home afterwards. On 2 October 1997 he was found dead in his apartment at Cabo Frio. The coroner recorded death by asphyxiation due to hanging and the death was ruled suicide.

The temptation with a witness who recants a signed military report and dies weeks later is obvious, and the record does not support taking it. Gevaerd, who had spent the hours of the interview with him and who has every professional incentive to say otherwise, has stated flatly that no one 'suicided' him for talking, that Hollanda acted for his own reasons, and that Hollanda had disclosed a previous attempt on his own life, a fall from the fourth floor of a building. The honest formulation is that the board has a commanding officer who signed a negative report, publicly contradicted it two decades later, and died by his own hand within weeks, and that the person best placed to allege foul play declines to. That is a knot, not a proof, and it belongs in the open questions.

The archival afterlife is where the contrast with the US becomes concrete. In April 2009 the Air Force's documentation centre, CENDOC, sent the first tranche of Operacao Prato material to the Arquivo Nacional. Later in 2009 the Gabinete de Seguranca Institucional of the Presidency released dozens of pages of former SNI documents on the same operation. The two streams converged into the collection coded BR DFANBSB ARX, the Fundo OVNI, where they sit digitised and searchable through SIAN, the Arquivo Nacional's online information system. The mechanism that made this routine was administrative rather than political: Portaria no 551/GC3 of August 2010 established that the Aeronautica registers and forwards, and the archive publishes.

What Operacao Prato does not do is establish recovery or reverse-engineering. There is no crash, no hardware, no custody chain in the released material. Its evidentiary function on this board is different and arguably stronger: it is a fully documented instance of a state deciding that objects it could not identify were interfering with its citizens, tasking a named officer to go and look, and then, three decades on, handing the whole file to the reading public. Every element of that sequence has a missing US counterpart, and the absence of the counterpart is the finding.

### What it has denied

The operation's own final report, signed by its commanding officer, is a negative finding: no signs of extraterrestrials at Colares. That report is the denial, and it is exactly the kind of denial this board catalogues rather than accepts, because it is dated, signed and attributable, and because the signatory publicly repudiated it twenty years later before dying by suicide within weeks. The FAB has never withdrawn or reaffirmed the report; it has simply released the file.

Roles the board assigns it: research, custody, disclosure, concealment.

### Open questions on this node

- The precise phase dates of the operation vary by source. One commonly cited framing gives two phases, 20 October to 11 November 1977 and 25 November to 5 December 1977, while other accounts extend the operation into 1978 and the archive compilation covers 2 September 1977 to 28 November 1978. No primary FAB order fixing the phases has been retrieved.
- The identity and archival citation of Hollanda's signed final report inside BR DFANBSB ARX have not been pinned to a specific SIAN item number in this pass.
- The attribution of the ARX 184 compilation to a report signed by Brigadeiro Jose Pessoa Cavalcanti de Albuquerque rests on a secondary blog account and has not been verified against the archive item.
- Whether the Colares hospital admissions were medically documented in the military file, and whether those medical records were transferred with the rest, is unresolved.
- The circumstances of Hollanda's death are recorded as suicide and the principal interviewer rejects foul play. The board should not treat the sequence of recantation followed by death as evidentiary without a document that has not been produced.
- No released Operacao Prato material speaks to recovery of material, custody of hardware, or reverse-engineering.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 75 of 1125, score 70). 10 connections, 5 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C2/A2/D2.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** A federal suit to compel declassification of Operação Prato films (Rafael Zatarian Pederneiras v. União) was dismissed and the dismissal upheld on 27 February 2023. The court held that the claim of surviving FAB UFO films 'tem lastro especialmente em matérias jornalísticas e relatos de testemunhas, de modo que não constituem indícios suficientes quanto à existência de tais arquivos', and applied the presumption of veracity to the Administration's statement that the surviving records are no longer classified and are held by the Arquivo Nacional. Source: Justiça Federal SC, Recurso Cível n. 5002417-48.2022.4.04.7200/SC, RELVOTO, 27/2/2023
- **Tier 1 documented.** Archival unit designation attested in a parliamentary document: the summary of SINDACTA I UFO cases sent to COMDABRA is recorded 'no Envelope nº. 10 da década de 1970'. The related petition is Processo nº 60.000.000026/2008-45 ('Dossiê UFO BRASIL'), filed at the Ministério da Defesa on 2 January 2008; the Operação Prato film material is its item 'j'. Source: Câmara dos Deputados, RIC 679/2011, items 1.1 and 1.2, 22 June 2011
- **Tier 1 documented.** Arquivo Nacional's own response to a litigant, quoted verbatim in the federal court record: 'Em pesquisa no SIAN, usando o termo "operação prato", retornam 6 (seis) documentos. Em nenhum deles há documentos de caráter audiovisual.' The catalogue therefore yields six items under that search term and no audiovisual material. NO ITEM-LEVEL SIAN REFERENCE CODES FOR THESE SIX WERE OBTAINABLE - see 'unresolved'. Source: Justiça Federal, Seção Judiciária de Santa Catarina, Recurso Cível n. 5002417-48.2022.4.04.7200/SC, Relator Juiz Federal João Batista Lazzari, RELVOTO, signed 27/2/2023, quoting Comando de Operações Aéreas/COMAER contestation, evento 13, ANEXO2 (https://www.migalhas.com.br/arquivos/2026/1/7F5E4CC12D7AE7_terceiraacao.pdf)
- **Tier 1 documented.** Brigadeiro Protázio Lopes de Oliveira is identified in the same parliamentary document as 'Chefe do I COMAR à época da Operação', placing the operation under the 1º Comando Aéreo Regional in Belém/PA, 1977-1978. Source: Câmara dos Deputados, RIC 679/2011, item 1.2, 22 June 2011
- **Tier 1 documented.** Documents and photographs from Operacao Prato are held in the Arquivo Nacional's Fundo Objeto Voador Nao Identificado, reference code BR DFANBSB ARX, and are accessible digitally through the Sistema de Informacoes do Arquivo Nacional (SIAN). Source: Arquivo Nacional, 'Conheca o fundo sobre OVNIs do Arquivo Nacional', https://www.gov.br/arquivonacional/pt-br/canais_atendimento/imprensa/noticias/conheca-o-fundo-sobre-ovnis-do-arquivo-nacional (SIAN access at http://sian.an.gov.br/sianex/consulta/login.asp)
- **Tier 1 documented.** RIC 679/2011 records that material was withheld from the National Archives transfer: 'Estão faltando centenas de fotografias de um total de mais de 500 - só foram fornecidas aproximadamente 230 -, várias horas de filmes de OVNIs em películas super 8 e super 16 mm', and that the original I COMAR reports - 'bem mais detalhados do que o "resumo de todos os casos de OVNIs de que o SINDACTA I participou"' - were not sent to the Arquivo Nacional's Coordenação Regional no Distrito Federal (COREG). Source: Câmara dos Deputados, RIC 679/2011, item 1.2, 22 June 2011
- **Tier 1 documented.** The Arquivo Nacional's OVNI fund includes photographs, drawings, videos and audio recordings alongside written reports and occurrence questionnaires. Source: Arquivo Nacional, 'Conheca o fundo sobre OVNIs do Arquivo Nacional', https://www.gov.br/arquivonacional/pt-br/canais_atendimento/imprensa/noticias/conheca-o-fundo-sobre-ovnis-do-arquivo-nacional
- **Tier 1 documented.** The Brazilian Senate's official record names Operacao Prato, dated to 1977, as one of several official UFO research programmes created by the Brazilian government through the Aeronautica since 1954. Source: Senado Federal, Requerimento no 193, de 2022, justificacao, presented 16 March 2022, https://legis.senado.leg.br/sdleg-getter/documento?dm=9089831&disposition=inline
- **Tier 1 documented.** The Senado Federal, in a requerimento approved by the Plenary on 24 June 2022, lists Operação Prato among official Brazilian government UFO research programmes: 'Desde 1954, o Governo Brasileiro, por meio de sua Aeronáutica, já criou vários programas oficiais de pesquisas dos OVNIs, como em 1977, quando ocorreu a chamada Operação Prato'. This is a primary legislative characterisation, not an independent finding of fact. Source: Senado Federal, Requerimento nº 193, de 2022 (RQS 193/2022), Sen. - **Tier 1 documented.** The operation's commander is named in a Chamber of Deputies information requirement as 'coronel Uyrangê Bolivar Soares Nogueira de Hollanda Lima', described as having narrated the missing film and photographic material to ufologists 'antes de sua morte em 1997'. - **Tier 2 named person on the record, allegation shaped wording as stored.** Jacques Vallée reported that individuals were said to have been killed by the lights and that injuries were consistent with radiation effects from microwaves. Source: Jacques Vallée, Confrontations: A Scientist's Search for Alien Contact (Ballantine, 1990), p.134
- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** Operacao Prato was a Brazilian Air Force operation conducted in the state of Para in 1977 and 1978, commanded by Capitao Uyrange Bolivar Soares Nogueira de Hollanda Lima, who gave the mission its name. Source: Wikipedia, 'Operacao Prato', https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opera%C3%A7%C3%A3o_Prato ; Terra, 'Ovnis no Brasil: o que foi a Operacao Prato, investigacao da ditadura sobre fenomenos ufologicos', https://www.terra.com.br/noticias/brasil/ovnis-no-brasil-o-que-foi-a-operacao-prato-investigacao-da-ditadura-sobre-fenomenos-ufologicos,a6c33e9a9fdad2837d1e86fc2913dddewptx0faq.html

- **Tier 2 named person on the record.** The FAB's standing official position, restated in writing in August 2026, is that all of its holdings on unidentified aerial phenomena from 1952 to 2025, which encompasses the Operacao Prato period, have already been transferred to the Arquivo Nacional and are in the public domain. ### Its strongest connections

- Operação Prato (Operation Saucer) comes in from **Força Aérea Brasileira / Ministério da Aeronáutica** (institution, board tier A), a programmatic link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: The Brazilian Air Force mounted Operação Prato as its own field operation. Operação Prato was run by the Força Aérea Brasileira out of the Belém air base in Pará, with the SNI, after mayors in the Colares area formally asked for help. The Brazilian Senate's own Requerimento 193/2022 lists it among the official Aeronáutica UAP programmes: 'Desde 1954, o Governo Brasileiro, por meio de sua Aeronáutica, já criou vários programas oficiais de pesquisas dos OVNIs, como em 1977, quando ocorreu a chamada Operação Prato'. Bob Pratt's file records that the operation was staffed by 'Captain Uyrangê Hollanda and six sergeants' and was shut down after four months by Brigadier Protázio Lopes de Oliveira, the base commander in Belém. This is the base institutional fact the whole Brazilian cluster hangs from and it was previously unwired. It opens: 'Operação Prato, a Brazilian Air Force investigation of a prolonged UFO flap in 1977-78'. It names Captain Uyrangê Hollanda and his six sergeants, names Brigadier Protázio Lopes de Oliveira as the Belém base commander who shut the operation down after four months, and describes the interviews of hundreds of witnesses in Colares and about thirty other villages. ## The Black Vault

The Black Vault is a institution on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 1996; axes C0 A0 D3; 9 connections; in the custody band.

In one line: FOIA document archive founded by John Greenewald Jr., 1996.

### What it is

The private Freedom of Information Act archive run by John Greenewald Jr, through which the large majority of the primary documents in this domain reached the public.

### Why it is implicated

It is an adversarial requester rather than an interested party, which is what gives its releases their evidentiary force, and several of this board's decisive negative findings exist only because it filed and published.

### The longer account

The archive's place on this board is structural: it is the mechanism, not the argument. Two of the records opened in this corpus show why it earns a node rather than a footnote. The first is the 341st Strategic Missile Wing release of 14 June 2001, eighty-seven pages of excerpts from five quarterly wing histories, sent to Greenewald by HQ 341st Space Wing in answer to a request of 9 May 2001. That release is the document the board's Malmstrom node has always named as decisive, and it is the one that states the Air Force's own conclusion and withholds the engineering report behind it. The second is the Defense Intelligence Agency release of 22 August 1996, IR 6 846 0139 76 on the Tehran F-4 intercepts, with DIA's covering letter stating that a search of its systems of records located one document responsive to the subject of the request. Both are cases where what the government holds, and how little of it, is knowable only because somebody outside asked in writing and published the answer with its covering letter attached.

Roles the board assigns it: disclosure, custody.

### Open questions on this node

- Whether the 181-page NARA Operation Northwoods file the archive indexes carries material beyond the fifteen-page JCS memorandum.
- Which of the archive's outstanding requests in this domain are still pending.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 202 of 1125, score 45). 9 connections, 4 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C0/A0/D3.

## The Disclosure Project

The Disclosure Project is a program on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 1993; axes C0 A0 D3; 7 connections; in the custody band.

In one line: Private witness-aggregation and congressional-hearing campaign, 1993-.

### What it is

A private witness-aggregation and congressional-hearing campaign founded by Steven M. Greer, running from 1993, whose method is to interview former military and government personnel on video and publish summarised transcripts. Its April 2001 Briefing Document prints sixty-eight dated testimonies; its press conference of 9 May 2001 presented about twenty witnesses and called for congressional hearings with subpoena power and amnesty.

### Why it is implicated

It is the largest single upstream source of witness claims on this board, and it is also an interested party with an explicit political objective. Both facts have to be visible at once. The board carries its testimony because the testimony is dated and attributed, and grades that testimony carefully because the collection method is one man with a stated goal.

### The longer account

The Disclosure Project's importance to this board is structural rather than evidential. Its Briefing Document is the anchor under a large share of the witness claims the board carries, which is why the document has its own L0 node, and its 2001 press conference is the event most often invoked as the moment those claims entered public life.

Two documented facts complicate it, and the node carries both. It controlled access to its own press events: Klass records being refused admission to a comparable 1997 conference, and reportedly the Fund for UFO Research was refused too. An organisation that selects its audience is entitled to, and a board recording its output should note it. And the Clinton White House's principal UFO FOIA collection, 2006-0474-F, consists in the majority of documents sent to the White House by Greer of CSETI, which means a federal records collection frequently cited as government material is substantially this organisation's own correspondence arriving back through the front door.

Its reach is not only rhetorical. The Wilson-Davis notes record the Disclosure Project's April 1997 Washington briefing week as the proximate trigger for Admiral Thomas Wilson's attempt to locate a special access programme. Whatever one concludes about the testimony, the organisation demonstrably moved a senior officer to go looking, and the board carries that at Tier 3 because the notes themselves are contested.

The 19 September pass found the organisation's cross-referencing habit worth flagging: the compiler links his own witnesses to each other in editorial notes, so convergence inside the Briefing Document is not independent convergence.

Roles the board assigns it: disclosure, research, custody.

### Open questions on this node

- The video recordings behind the sixty-eight printed testimonies. None has been opened.
- Whether any witness has retracted or amended testimony given to the project.
- The organisation's funding across its history, which nothing opened here addresses.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 400 of 1125, score 34). 8 connections, 0 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 1 official denial recorded against it. Axes C0/A0/D3.

## 1975 SAC Nuclear-Base Overflights (Loring, Wurtsmith, Malmstrom, Minot)

1975 SAC Nuclear-Base Overflights (Loring, Wurtsmith, Malmstrom, Minot) is a event on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 1975; axes C1 A2 D3; 7 connections; in the custody band.

In one line: Documented unidentified incursions over nuclear weapons and missile installations, Oct-Nov 1975.

### What it is

A sustained series of unidentified aerial incursions over Strategic Air Command nuclear weapons storage areas, bomber alert facilities and ICBM fields between 27 October and roughly 19 November 1975, at Loring AFB in Maine, Wurtsmith AFB in Michigan, Malmstrom AFB in Montana, Minot AFB in North Dakota, and Canadian Forces Station Falconbridge in Ontario. The events generated OPREP messages to the National Military Command Center, a SAC-wide security directive, NORAD Senior Director's Log entries, repeated failed interceptor scrambles, and a five-part CINCNORAD message dated 11 November 1975. Most of the documentary record entered the public domain through FOIA requests filed by civilian researchers between 1976 and 1978.

### Why it is implicated

This is the best-documented case in the entire record of sustained, deliberate interest by unidentified objects in American nuclear weapons infrastructure, and the government's own contemporaneous words are the evidence. An Air Force operational report stated that the intruder 'has demonstrated a clear intent in the weapons storage area.' SAC issued a directive to ten northern-tier bases under the subject line 'Defense Against Helicopter Assault,' placing them at Security Option 3 during hours of darkness. The commander of NORAD told his own command that Falconbridge personnel observed through binoculars a '100 ft. diameter sphere' that 'appeared to have craters around the outside' at altitudes between 26,000 and 72,000 feet, and in the same message wrote that he had 'expressed my concern to SAFOI that we come up soonest with a proposed answer to queries from the press to prevent overreaction by the public.' That is a dated, attributable instruction to build a press posture before the identification problem was solved, the concealment effort visible in real time in the commander's own sentence. Three years later, when a subcommittee chairman finally asked for the records, the Air Force replied in writing that it could find no official information and that some of the incidents 'may not have happened at all,' then reversed and produced material while asserting that UFO reports are 'of transitory interest to the Air Force and permanent files are not maintained.' The 1975 wave therefore supplies four elements at once: intrusion on nuclear custody, total failure of interception, an explicit press-management decision, and a documented policy of non-retention that forecloses future audit.

### The longer account

Loring Air Force Base in northern Maine was Strategic Air Command's easternmost continental installation, home to a B-52 squadron on nuclear alert and two KC-135 tanker squadrons, with a separately fenced nuclear weapons storage area. On the evening of 27 October 1975 security personnel observed what was officially recorded as 'an unidentified helicopter' near the northern perimeter at roughly 150 feet, showing a red navigation light and a white strobe, apparently interested in the weapons storage area. Army National Guard helicopters were dispatched and could not identify it. Control tower radar picked up a second unknown circling ten to thirteen miles northeast. The first turned north into Canada near Grand Falls, New Brunswick; the second vanished from the scope.

The following night it returned without lights. The operational report issued afterward contained the sentence that gave Fawcett and Greenwood's book its title: 'It is our opinion that the unknown helicopter has demonstrated a clear intent in the weapons storage area, is smart and a most capable aviator.' On 28 October, Sgt. Clifton W. Blakeslee, Staff Sgt. William J. Long and Sgt. Danny Lewis of the 42nd Security Police Squadron tracked an object for thirty-five minutes visually and on tower radar; the commander of the 42nd Bomb Wing observed it himself. Separately, B-52 crew chief Sgt. Steven Eichner and Sgt. R. Jones came within about 300 feet of a reddish-orange object roughly four car lengths long, hovering five feet off the ground without sound, with no visible doors, windows, propellers or engines. Priority messages went to the National Military Command Center, the Chief of Staff of the Air Force, Fort Ritchie, SAC Headquarters and the 9th Air Force, advising that an unknown object had penetrated the base and had been in the nuclear storage area.

Security Police Shift Commander Arthur Beers later described a call from the National Emergency Command Post, with a colonel ordering him to shoot the helicopter down if it buzzed the area again; minutes later a general officer said the President had been briefed and the orders were changed to shoot only if it attempted to land. Chief Warrant Officer Bernard Poulin of the Maine Army National Guard flew UH-1 search missions vectored to where the object was being seen and heard, and never acquired it. Poulin recalled the base commander saying, 'We've got to keep the lid on the fact that someone has been able to penetrate in and around the bomb dump, and we don't know what's going on.'

SAC escalated laterally. A SAC Security Police message subject-lined 'Defense Against Helicopter Assault' went to ten northern-tier bases from Pease to Barksdale, directing them to 'assume Security Option 3 during hours of darkness until further notice.'

On 30 October it was Wurtsmith's turn. Security police at the back gate reported at 10:14, 10:20 and 10:25 p.m. an unidentified craft with no lights hovering low over the weapons storage area. RAPCON tracked it roughly thirty-five miles southeast over Lake Huron and the wing commander ordered a returning KC-135 to identify. Instructor navigator Capt. Myron Taylor's 'Memo for the Record' of 18 January 1979, written for 379th Bomb Wing historian SSgt Paul J. Cahill, records the pursuit: 'each time we attempted to close on the object it would speed away from us... I would estimate that the UFO sped away from us doing approximately 1,000 knots.' Taylor also recorded that about a week later the aircraft commander, Capt. Higginbotham, was questioned by OSI and 'cautioned not to discuss the incident.' On 31 October the Air Force Intelligence Service Alert Officer's Log recorded: 'Per Ltc. Redican's direction. Contacted DIA OPS center and informed them of unidentified flight activity over two SAC bases near Canadian border.'

Malmstrom's turn came 7 and 8 November, in the missile fields. On 10 November Minot Air Force Station logged 'a bright star-like object in the west, moving east, about the size of a car,' which 'passed over the radar station, 1000-2000 feet high, no noise heard.' On the morning of 11 November, CFS Falconbridge held radar paints thirty nautical miles south of the site between 26,000 and 72,000 feet; two F-106s from Selfridge ANGB were scrambled and made no contact.

That same day the Commander-in-Chief of NORAD sent a five-part message to NORAD units across North America. Part I named the five installations. Part II recorded that Malmstrom radars carried an object between 9,500 and 15,600 feet at seven knots, that F-106s could not make contact, that site personnel reported the object as low as 200 feet, and that 'as the interceptors approached the lights went out. After the interceptors had passed the lights came on again.' Part IV recorded the Falconbridge '100 ft. diameter sphere' with 'craters around the outside.' Part V is the concealment sentence: 'I have also expressed my concern to SAFOI that we come up soonest with a proposed answer to queries from the press to prevent overreaction by the public to reports by the media that may be blown out of proportion. To date efforts by Air Guard helicopters, SAC helicopters and Norad F-106s have failed to produce positive ID.'

Congress learned about it three years late and from a magazine. Parade published 'UFO's vs. USAF' by Michael Satchell on 10 December 1978. Representative Samuel S. Stratton (D-NY), chairman of the Armed Services Investigations Subcommittee, wrote to Maj. Gen. Charles C. Blanton on 20 December 1978 demanding all Air Force reports. The Air Force Directorate of Operations and Readiness responded internally on 8 January 1979: 'We have been unable to find any official information regarding the incidents described in the Parade magazine article... some of these incidents may not have happened at all.' On 9 February 1979 Joseph J. F. Clark, Associate Director of Legislative Liaison, produced a partial compilation and explained its thinness: 'unidentified flying object reports are of transitory interest to the Air Force and permanent files are not maintained.' Stratton did not pursue the matter further. The oversight window opened and closed in seven weeks.

### What it has denied

The official characterization was 'unidentified helicopter,' preserved even inside NORAD's own message despite radar tracks to 72,000 feet, a silent football-shaped object hovering five feet off the ground at Loring, and a KC-135 crew's estimate of 1,000 knots at Wurtsmith. When Congress asked in 1979, the Air Force first stated it could find no official information and that some incidents 'may not have happened at all,' then supplied partial records while asserting that UFO reports are 'of transitory interest to the Air Force and permanent files are not maintained' - a written declaration of non-retention that forecloses future audit.

Roles the board assigns it: concealment, custody, oversight.

### Open questions on this node

- Where are the underlying OPREP-3 messages, base histories and 42nd Bomb Wing records now, and were they destroyed under the 'transitory interest' policy or merely never searched?
- What did the Joint Chiefs of Staff briefings on the incursions actually contain, and do JCS files retain them?
- Who authorized the shoot-down order relayed to Loring security police through the National Emergency Command Post, and was the President in fact briefed as the general officer told Shift Commander Beers?
- What became of Alaskan Air Command message 012224, subject 'Unidentified Foreign Object,' referenced in the 3 November 1975 AFIS log and declared unavailable by the Air Force in February 1978?
- Was Plattsburgh AFB in fact a sighting location, as the 379th Bomb Wing historian's memo implies, and if so why does no supporting record exist?
- Did SAFOI produce the press posture CINCNORAD requested on 11 November 1975, and does that guidance document survive?
- Why did Rep. Stratton drop the inquiry, and is there subcommittee correspondence after 9 February 1979?
- Were the Grand Forks AFB shooting incidents of 3-4 November 1975 genuinely unrelated, or were they released concurrently to give the wave a mundane frame?
- What did the Air Force Office of Special Investigations say to Capt. Higginbotham, and does an OSI file on the Wurtsmith pursuit exist?

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 163 of 1125, score 50). 7 connections, 0 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 1 official denial recorded against it. Axes C1/A2/D3.
