# 2017-2026: Post-disclosure

## All-domain Anomaly Resolution Office (AARO)

All-domain Anomaly Resolution Office (AARO) is a institution on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2022; axes C2 A3 D2; 61 connections; in the custody band.

In one line: DoD office established 2022; author of the official AAWSAP history.

### What it is

The All-domain Anomaly Resolution Office is the Pentagon body designated in July 2022 as the Department's authoritative office for unidentified anomalous phenomena. It was created under section 1683 of the FY2022 National Defense Authorization Act, sits under the Under Secretary of Defense for Intelligence and Security, and absorbed the Navy's Unidentified Aerial Phenomena Task Force. It publishes the statutory annual UAP report, runs a secure reporting mechanism for current and former government personnel, and produced the Historical Record Report.

### Why it is implicated

AARO is the single point through which every UAP record, contract and data holding in the Department of Defense was ordered to flow, which makes it either the instrument of disclosure or the instrument of consolidation, and the record supports the second reading. The office was given, by its own account, unrestricted statutory access to compartmented and special access material, then used that access to produce a report clearing the Department of every allegation made against it. Its own numbers show what the reporting channel actually does: of 262 people who came forward in a single year, 255 were declared out of scope and 7 were judged worth interviewing. Witnesses under oath before the House Oversight Committee have described being targeted after providing classified testimony, and one described a session he understood to be with AARO leadership as an effort to attack the credibility of UAP witnesses. AARO is on this map not as an arbiter but as a party: it holds the custody, it wrote the exoneration, and it decides who counts as a witness.

### The longer account

AARO exists because Congress forced it into existence. Section 1683 of the FY2022 NDAA required the Secretary of Defense, in coordination with the Director of National Intelligence, to stand up an office with a scope broader than the Airborne Object Identification and Management Synchronization Group that the Deputy Secretary had created in November 2021. On 15 July 2022 Deputy Secretary Kathleen Hicks signed the memorandum that renamed and expanded AOIMSG into AARO. That memorandum is the founding document and it is worth reading for what it actually does. It makes AARO the authoritative office for UAP and UAP-related activities for the whole Department. It orders the Secretary of the Navy to disestablish the UAP Task Force and transfer its data, analysis and other relevant material. And it contains the sentence that matters most for this board: any DoD Component acting on behalf of the UAPTF, or who has data, analysis, contracts, or other material related to UAP, will immediately synchronize their efforts with the AARO.

That is a consolidation order. In a Department where the argument is that recovered material and its associated paperwork have been dispersed across contractors and unacknowledged compartments, an instruction that all UAP data, analysis and contracts route to a single office under USD(I&S) is the sort of instruction that can be read two ways. It is the necessary precondition for an honest accounting. It is also the necessary precondition for a controlled one.

AARO's first director was Sean Kirkpatrick, previously chief scientist at the Defense Intelligence Agency's Missile and Space Intelligence Center. He retired on 1 December 2023 having largely completed the Historical Record Report that his office published the following March. He publicly characterised David Grusch's allegations while, by his own later account, never having interviewed him. Grusch's position is that AARO never contacted him. Whichever account is correct, the resulting document is an investigation of a whistleblower's claims conducted without the whistleblower. Jon Kosloski, from the National Security Agency, took over in late 2024.

The office's own annual reporting is the best evidence about how the reporting channel functions. Under section 3373b of title 50, AARO hosts a secure mechanism for people claiming first-hand knowledge of a US Government UAP-related programme dating back to 1945. In the FY2025 report AARO discloses that between 2 June 2024 and 30 May 2025, 262 individuals used it. AARO determined 255 were out of scope. Seven warranted a request for a follow-on interview. That is a 97 percent rejection rate applied by the office whose own conduct is the subject of many of the allegations. The same report states, in language AARO chose, that the office is authorised by law to receive all UAP-related information at any level of classification regardless of any restrictive access controls, special access controls, or compartmented access programs, and that generally no restrictions, including non-disclosure agreements, prevent AARO from receiving it. AARO therefore claims the access and simultaneously reports that almost nobody who comes to it has anything worth hearing.

The FY2025 report contains a second internal tension that a hostile reader should sit with. It states flatly that to date no evidence suggests that a USG or private entity has ever captured or exploited UAP-derived materials. Two sentences later it states that AARO is currently developing a formalized process for handling UAP-derived materials by an appropriate line organization, should such materials ever come into the possession of the USG, drawing on established procedures governing the recovery of foreign materiel. An office that has found nothing is building a custody chain for the nothing it has found.

The performance record is poor by the Department's own inspector general's measure. DODIG-2023-109, whose unclassified summary was released on 24 January 2024, found the DoD had not used a coordinated approach to detect, report, collect, analyze, and identify UAP, had issued no comprehensive UAP response plan, and had largely excluded the geographic combatant commands. The Secretary of the Navy, the Secretary of the Air Force and the Chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff each declined to provide official comments for the final report.

The delivery record is worse. Volume II of the Historical Record Report, covering interviews and research from 1 November 2023 to 15 April 2024, was promised in Volume I under a deadline set in section 6802 of the FY2023 NDAA. By late February 2026 it had not appeared, the FY2025 annual report had not appeared, the deputy director post was vacant, and Pentagon spokesperson Sue Gough, asked why AARO had not published or briefed in over a year, told DefenseScoop the office had nothing to announce at this time. Gough did confirm that AARO had been examining over 2,000 UAP cases, roughly 1,000 of which lacked sufficient data and sat in an active archive. When the FY2025 report finally issued, it carried document number 26-P-0880 and a Department of War letterhead, with an information cut-off of 30 May 2025.

The strongest single item against AARO is in its own Volume I. The report concedes that in 2021, without sufficient justification, the scope of an Intelligence Community Controlled Access Program was expanded to protect UAP reverse-engineering, and that the programme was later disestablished for lack of merit. AARO's framing is that the compartment was unwarranted and empty. The fact remains that a compartment was stood up to protect UAP reverse-engineering, and the office that told us so is the office that closed it.

### What it has denied

AARO has denied, in Volume I of its Historical Record Report and in every annual report since, that any US Government or private entity has recovered, possessed or reverse-engineered non-human technology, that any authentic UAP-related non-disclosure agreement exists, and that any UAP-derived material has ever been captured or exploited. Its FY2025 report states there is no evidence any entity has ever captured or exploited UAP-derived materials while simultaneously reporting that AARO is building a handling process for such materials. Its FY2024 and FY2025 reports assert that every resolved case is attributable to prosaic objects.

Roles the board assigns it: concealment, oversight, custody, disclosure.

### Open questions on this node

- Volume II of the Historical Record Report, statutorily promised under section 6802 of the FY2023 NDAA and covering 1 November 2023 to 15 April 2024, had not been published as of the last confirmed reporting in February 2026. Its current status is unresolved on this board.
- The identity of the IC Controlled Access Program whose scope AARO says was expanded in 2021 to protect UAP reverse-engineering is not disclosed in Volume I, nor is the authority that expanded it or the authority that disestablished it.
- AARO reports 255 of 262 secure-mechanism contacts as out of scope but publishes no breakdown of the scope test applied, no appeal route, and no record of which claims were referred elsewhere.
- Whether AARO's claimed unrestricted access extended to unacknowledged SAPs and to contractor-held Independent Research and Development compartments, or only to programmes DoD and IC security officials volunteered, is not established by any released document.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 3 of 1125, score 164). 64 connections, 6 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 11 official denials recorded against it. Axes C2/A3/D2.

## PURSUE - Presidential Unsealing and Reporting System for UAP Encounters

PURSUE - Presidential Unsealing and Reporting System for UAP Encounters is a program on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2026; axes C1 A3 D3; 40 connections; in the custody band.

In one line: Executive disclosure channel announced Feb 2026; first drop 8 May 2026.

### What it is

The PURSUE releases are the five dated tranches of UAP material published by the Department of War at war.gov/UFO between 8 May 2026 and 7 August 2026. Each tranche is posted as a paired download - a document bundle served from www.war.gov/medialink/ufo/ and a video bundle served from a CloudFront distribution - alongside a searchable database of individual records. The files carry originator-prefixed identifiers such as DOW-UAP-D###, DOW-UAP-PR## and FBI-UAP-D###, the first time the United States Government has issued UAP material under a single public numbering scheme.

### Why it is implicated

This is the physical evidence of what the government chose to hand over when a President ordered it to hand something over. The ledger is therefore diagnostic in both directions. It confirms the archive: material spanning 1944 to 2026, FBI holdings, NASA crew audio, INDOPACOM reporting, all of it previously unavailable and now released without clearance. It also confirms the shape of the withholding: at least a hundred of the roughly 160 files in the first tranche carry redactions, and every serious analyst who examined them - Mellon, Graves, Gallaudet, Rojas - reported the same absence of coordinates, sensor parameters, altitude, timing and chain of custody, which are the precise fields that would permit independent verification. No tranche carries a manifest, an exemption citation, a FOIA case number or a provenance statement. The releases are the strongest available proof that the holdings exist and the strongest available proof that the release process was designed to be unauditable.

### The longer account

Five tranches, over three months, from a Department web page. Release 01 on 8 May 2026, Release 02 on 22 May, Release 03 on 12 June, Release 04 on 10 July, Release 05 on 7 August. The portal states the sequence plainly: 'The Department of War just released the fifth tranche of records under PURSUE. The first tranche was released on May 8, 2026.'

The delivery mechanism is worth describing exactly, because it is not a records system. Each release offers two ZIP archives. The documents come from war.gov itself: Release 01 documents at 1.2gb, Release 02 at 70.1mb, Release 03 at 826mb, Release 04 at 227mb, Release 05 at 130mb. The videos come from a commercial content delivery network at d34w7g4gy10iej.cloudfront.net: 1.3gb, 5.6gb, 4.6gb, 1.4gb and 513mb respectively. There is no manifest file, no checksum, no metadata schema, no index of what was withheld. The portal offers a filterable database by agency, release, incident date, incident location and type - which is more structure than any prior US UAP release has had, and still less than any archivist would call a finding aid. At the time this dossier was compiled the portal's 'LATEST RELEASE' link still pointed to Release 04 while the banner above it announced Release 05, which tells you something about how the page is maintained.

The file identifiers are the most analytically useful artefact in the whole programme. Filenames visible on the portal include DOW-UAP-PR46-Unresolved-UAP-Report-INDOPACOM-2024, DOW-UAP-D098_Film-Analysis-of-Unidentified-Objects_1953, FBI-UAP-D025_Digital-Rendering_Airborne-Triangle_2002, FBI-UAP-D027_Digital-Rendering_Dark-Translucent-Triangle_2023, FBI-UAP-D031_Digital-Rendering_Large-Triangle-with-Red-Lights_2023 and FBI-UAP-D038 through D042, a run of digital renderings of multiple red lights dated 2026. Two things follow. First, the numbering is originator-prefixed, which means the tranches were assembled by pulling from multiple agencies' holdings under a single tasking rather than compiled from one department's files - a single-department dump would not need agency prefixes. Second, the Bureau is a substantial contributor, and its contributions run to the present: FBI-UAP-D038 through D042 are dated 2026, the year of release.

The imagery convention is itself a disclosure decision. A large share of the FBI material is published as 'Digital Rendering' - artists' reconstructions of witness accounts rather than sensor products. DefenseScoop's illustration caption for the first tranche describes an 'Actual site photo with FBI rendered graphic depicting corroborating eye witness reports from September 2023 of an apparent ellipsoid bronze metallic object materializing out of a bright light in the sky, 130-195 feet in length, and disappearing instantaneously.' A rendering cannot be independently analysed. It can only be believed or not.

On volume and redaction, the contemporaneous count is DefenseScoop's: 'The initial PURSUE tranche contained a little more than 160 files, with at least 100 of those including redactions, according to researchers and experts who are currently studying them.' Roughly five files in eight were released with material removed, in a programme whose stated purpose was to stop hiding material, and with no exemption citation attached to any redaction.

The substantive verdict from the people best placed to give one was delivered within a week and was unanimous on the technical point. Alejandro Rojas, whose Enigma Labs team was cross-referencing the drop against a database of over 200,000 public sightings: 'Although there is a lot of information here, there is little context. Case summaries without coordinates, sensor parameters, or altitude data make independent analysis very difficult.' Tim Gallaudet, who thought some of the videos showed genuinely anomalous behaviour including right-angle turns and formation flying: 'the ambiguity of the video content in particular is concerning as no metadata was included. Therefore, it is impossible to conclude that any of the objects were truly anomalous.' Ryan Graves, on what a useful release looks like: 'The real value is in records that include clear provenance, sensor context, metadata, chain of custody, date and location, and enough operational context to allow serious independent analysis. Ambiguous imagery without that surrounding data is hard for pilots, researchers, or the public to evaluate responsibly.'

Several of the same people noted that much of the first tranche was not new. The Australian researcher Grant Lavac: 'For those of us in the UAP research community, the newness of the data in this first tranche is underwhelming. Much of it is a compilation of documents that have been in the public domain for decades.' His countervailing point is the one this board accepts: 'the story here isn't necessarily the content, but the process of official acknowledgment.'

And there is a provenance problem that the releases themselves create. Jordan Flowers of the Disclosure Foundation raised it in the first week: the US Government's own documented mid-century practice of manufacturing false UAP documents as cover for real weapons programmes means some of what is being published as authentic government UAP records may be disinformation artefacts. 'If some of what is now being released to the public as authentic government UAP records are in fact disinformation artifacts from that era, the public deserves to know. Transparency requires not just releasing files, but accounting for their provenance and integrity.' PURSUE publishes without provenance statements, which means it cannot distinguish a genuine 1953 film analysis from a 1953 cover story, and neither can anyone downstream.

What the ledger establishes is therefore narrower and harder than the headlines suggested. The archive exists and is large. The Department was able to produce material from at least six agencies within eleven weeks of a presidential post. The material released is the unresolved residue, stripped of the fields that would let anyone check it, with a third to two-thirds of it redacted and none of it assessed. That is not the record of a government that has nothing. It is the record of a government deciding, file by file, how much of what it has to show.

### What it has denied

The Department of War declines to characterise its own releases: a War Department official told DefenseScoop 'We are not providing any comment or assessment on the files overall or on any specific file, so that the American people can make up their own minds regarding the files.' No exemption authority has been cited for any redaction, and no official has responded to the uniform expert finding that the released material lacks the metadata required for independent analysis.

Roles the board assigns it: disclosure, custody, concealment.

### Open questions on this node

- Per-tranche file counts and composition are reported by third-party inventories rather than by any official manifest; the Department has published no authoritative count for any release.
- Under what authority the redactions in at least 100 first-tranche files were applied, and whether any withholding log exists.
- Whether an ODNI-prefixed identifier series (ODNI-UAP-D###) exists in the released material; DOW- and FBI-prefixed identifiers are directly verifiable on the portal, ODNI-prefixed ones were not verified for this dossier.
- Whether any released video retains embedded sensor metadata in the source files as distinct from the portal case summaries.
- How the Department distinguishes authentic historical records from mid-century disinformation artefacts before publication, if it does so at all.
- Whether any material relating to recovery, custody or reverse engineering was within the scope of the PURSUE search, and whether any such material was found and withheld.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 4 of 1125, score 161). 47 connections, 11 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 6 official denials recorded against it. Axes C1/A3/D3.

## UAP Disclosure Act of 2023 (S.Amdt. 797 to S. 2226; Division G of the Senate-passed FY2024 NDAA)

UAP Disclosure Act of 2023 (S.Amdt. 797 to S. 2226; Division G of the Senate-passed FY2024 NDAA) is a legislation on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2023; axes C3 A3 D3; 26 connections; in the custody band.

In one line: disclosure-legislation.

### What it is

The Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena Disclosure Act of 2023 was submitted by Senator Charles Schumer on 13 July 2023 as Senate Amendment 797 to S. 2226, the FY2024 National Defense Authorization Act, and printed in full in the Congressional Record at pages S2953-S2959. It travelled into the Senate-engrossed NDAA as a freestanding division of fifteen sections. Its enforcement machinery - an independent nine-member Records Review Board, a federal exercise of eminent domain over privately held recovered materials, and a $20,000,000 authorisation - was removed before enactment, leaving only an archiving and postponement regime at sections 1841-1843 of Public Law 118-31.

### Why it is implicated

This is the only text ever adopted by a chamber of the United States Congress that legislates on the express premise that recovered technologies of unknown origin and biological evidence of non-human intelligence exist and are held in part by private entities. Its definitions are not rhetorical: 'controlling authority' is defined to include any commercial company, academic institution, or private sector entity in physical possession of such material, and 'legacy program' is defined to reach commercial, academic and private-sector reverse-engineering endeavours predating enactment. Six senators including the Majority Leader and the Vice Chairman of the Senate Intelligence Committee put their names to a finding that records exist which have been shielded from mandatory declassification review by Atomic Energy Act exemptions and an over-broad reading of transclassified foreign nuclear information. The bill then directs the Federal Government to seize that material by eminent domain and hand it to a board explicitly barred from containing anyone who has ever worked on a legacy program. The provisions that would have moved custody, compelled testimony and paid for the effort are precisely the provisions that did not survive conference - which is the single most informative fact on this node.

### The longer account

On 13 July 2023 the Senate Majority Leader rose and submitted an amendment to the defence bill. The Congressional Record entry is flat and procedural - 'SA 797. Mr. SCHUMER (for himself, Mr. Rounds, Mr. Rubio, Mrs. Gillibrand, Mr. Young, and Mr. Heinrich) submitted an amendment intended to be proposed by him to the bill S. 2226 ... which was ordered to lie on the table' - and what follows across seven pages of the Record is the most consequential UAP text in American law.

The structure is deliberately modelled on the President John F. Kennedy Assassination Records Collection Act of 1992: a records collection at the National Archives, a presumption of immediate disclosure, an independent review board with the power to override agency postponement, and a sunset. The differences are where the argument lives. The JFK Act dealt only with paper. This bill deals with objects.

The findings section states the premise. Congress finds that 'Legislation is necessary because credible evidence and testimony indicates that Federal Government unidentified anomalous phenomena records exist that have not been declassified or subject to mandatory declassification review as set forth in Executive Order 13526 ... due in part to exemptions under the Atomic Energy Act of 1954 ..., as well as an over-broad interpretation of "transclassified foreign nuclear information", which is also exempt from mandatory declassification, thereby preventing public disclosure under existing provisions of law.' That is a named legal mechanism of concealment, identified by the Senate, and it points at the Department of Energy and the Restricted Data regime rather than at the Department of Defense alone. The next finding says the Freedom of Information Act 'has proven inadequate.'

The definitions do the load-bearing work. 'Controlling authority' means 'any Federal, State, or local government department, office, agency, committee, commission, commercial company, academic institution, or private sector entity in physical possession of technologies of unknown origin or biological evidence of non-human intelligence.' A commercial company cannot be in physical possession of a thing that does not exist. 'Legacy program' is defined to include 'commercial industry, academic, and private sector endeavors to collect, exploit, or reverse engineer technologies of unknown origin or examine biological evidence of living or deceased non-human intelligence that pre-dates the date of the enactment of this Act.' 'Non-human intelligence' means 'any sentient intelligent non-human lifeform regardless of nature or ultimate origin that may be presumed responsible for unidentified anomalous phenomena or of which the Federal Government has become aware.'

The Review Board provisions establish 'as an independent agency a board to be known as the "Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena Records Review Board"', appointed by the President with the advice and consent of the Senate, nine citizens, without regard to political affiliation. Board staff must be citizens 'of integrity and impartiality who ha[ve] had no previous or current involvement with any legacy program or controlling authority relating to the collection, exploitation, or reverse engineering of technologies of unknown origin or the examination of biological evidence of living or deceased non-human intelligence.' A statute does not write a conflict-of-interest bar against a category of person that Congress believes to be empty.

Then the section that made the bill unpassable. 'The Federal Government shall exercise eminent domain over any and all recovered technologies of unknown origin and biological evidence of non-human intelligence that may be controlled by private persons or entities in the interests of the public good.' Such material 'shall be made available to the Review Board for personal examination and subsequent disclosure determination at a location suitable to the controlling authority of said material.' The Board would then decide whether the material constitutes technologies of unknown origin 'beyond a reasonable doubt', whether it qualifies for postponement, and 'what changes, if any, to the current disposition of said material should the Federal Government make to facilitate full disclosure.' The Board would also 'have access to all testimony from unidentified anomalous phenomena witnesses, close observers and legacy program personnel and whistleblowers within the Federal Government's possession.'

The money section authorised $20,000,000 for fiscal year 2024 and, pending appropriation, permitted the President to use 'such sums as are available for discretionary use.'

What was enacted on 22 December 2023 in Public Law 118-31 is three sections - 1841, 1842 and 1843 - creating the Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena Records Collection at the National Archives, requiring agencies to review, identify, transmit and disclose UAP records, and setting out grounds for postponement. There is no Review Board. There is no eminent domain. There is no authorisation of appropriations. There is no witness-solicitation mandate. The surviving regime asks the original classification authorities that hold the records to identify their own records and to invoke their own postponement grounds, and gives no independent body the power to say no.

That is the argument. The Senate wrote a bill whose operative theory was that the material is held by controlling authorities including private companies, that those companies will not surrender it voluntarily, and that only compulsory acquisition plus an outside board with special access clearances could break the hold. Conference removed compulsion and removed the outside board. What remains is a filing cabinet with the same locks and the same keyholders.

### What it has denied

No executive branch office has ever publicly explained why the Review Board and eminent domain sections were removed in conference, and no conference report language identifying an objecting party has been made public. The Department of Defense has never responded on the record to the bill's finding that Atomic Energy Act exemptions and transclassified foreign nuclear information are being used to keep UAP records out of mandatory declassification review.

Roles the board assigns it: oversight, disclosure, custody, concealment.

### Open questions on this node

- Which members or agencies asked for the Review Board and eminent domain sections to be struck in the FY2024 NDAA conference, and in what written form was the request made?
- Did any executive branch agency submit a Statement of Administration Policy or legislative-referral objection to Division G, and is it retrievable?
- Has any agency ever identified a specific record withheld under the 'transclassified foreign nuclear information' exemption that would otherwise be a UAP record?
- The Senate text presupposes controlling authorities in physical possession; no controlling authority has ever been named in any public congressional document. Which entities did the drafters have in mind?
- Section numbering differs between the Congressional Record print of SA 797 (SEC. __01 through __15) and the engrossed division; the engrossed section numbers used elsewhere on this board should be re-verified against BILLS-118s2226es directly.
- The negative claim that no Review Board or eminent domain text survives in Public Law 118-31 was established here by comparison against NARA's description of the enacted sections rather than by a direct full-text search of the enrolled bill, which should be run to close the point.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 9 of 1125, score 134). 26 connections, 8 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 3 official denials recorded against it. Axes C3/A3/D3.

## National Archives Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena Records Collection

National Archives Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena Records Collection is a institution on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2024; axes C0 A1 D3; 13 connections; in the custody band.

In one line: Statutory collection under 2024 NDAA secs. 1841-1843.

### What it is

The Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena Records Collection is a statutory records collection at the National Archives and Records Administration, created by sections 1841 to 1843 of the FY2024 National Defense Authorization Act and codified at 44 U.S.C. 2107 note. NARA established it as Record Group 615. Every federal agency was required to review, identify and organise its UAP records for disclosure and transmission, and to transfer publicly releasable digital copies in advance of 30 September 2025.

### Why it is implicated

The collection is the concealment argument reduced to an inventory problem, and the inventory is nearly empty. Congress wrote a statutory definition that expressly reaches records concerning technologies of unknown origin and non-human intelligence, which is a legislature drafting for the existence of the thing rather than the possibility of it. It then stripped out the two provisions that would have given the statute teeth, the eminent domain power over privately held recovered material and the independent Review Board modelled on the JFK Act, leaving agencies to grade their own homework. What arrived is the measure of what compliance is worth: as of May 2026 the collection listed transmittals from seven organisations, none of them a military department, and on 28 August 2026 the Department of the Air Force became the eighth and the first service to transfer in its own right. The Central Intelligence Agency, the Department of Energy, the Defense Intelligence Agency, NASA, the Army and the Navy are still not among them. A parallel executive-branch release channel opened in 2026 that routes disclosures around the statutory collection entirely.

### The longer account

The Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena Disclosure Act, sponsored by Senators Schumer and Rounds, passed the Senate in July 2023 as an amendment to the FY2024 NDAA. In its Senate form it would have created an independent Review Board with authority over agency withholding decisions, and it would have directed the federal government to exercise eminent domain over any recovered technologies of unknown origin and biological evidence of non-human intelligence held by private persons or entities. Neither survived conference. What was enacted in Public Law 118-31 on 22 December 2023 was the records collection framework alone.

What survived is still remarkable as legislative drafting. The statute directs NARA to build a collection consisting of copies of all Government, Government-provided, or Government-funded records relating to unidentified anomalous phenomena, technologies of unknown origin, and non-human intelligence, or equivalent subjects by any other name, with the specific and sole exclusion of temporarily non-attributed objects. Congress wrote three categories into federal records law. Unidentified anomalous phenomena is the neutral one. Technologies of unknown origin and non-human intelligence are not neutral at all; they are the categories a legacy recovery programme would generate. The clause about equivalent subjects by any other name is anti-evasion drafting aimed at codeword renaming. The exclusion of temporarily non-attributed objects is there to keep routine drone and balloon traffic out. Legislatures do not build statutory taxonomies for things they believe do not exist.

NARA implemented it. It established Record Group 615, issued guidance to agencies through memoranda AC 26.2024 and AC 04.2025, and set the mechanics: agencies identify all UAP records in any format, make digital copies, attach prescribed metadata, and transfer. NARA accepts only digital versions. Agencies were to complete identification and organisation by 20 October 2024 and to initiate transfer in advance of 30 September 2025. One provision deserves particular attention. Where a publicly releasable record includes redactions, the agency must simultaneously transfer redacted and unredacted copies to NARA. The National Archives therefore holds unredacted originals of redacted UAP records that it does not publish, which makes NARA itself a custody node with material it is sitting on.

The results are the story. As of the Record Group 615 page's stated review date of 22 May 2026, the collection lists transmittals from seven organisations: the Federal Aviation Administration, the Nuclear Regulatory Commission, the Office of the Director of National Intelligence, the Office of the Secretary of Defense, the National Security Agency, the Department of State and the Federal Bureau of Investigation. That list is as notable for its absences as its contents. The Central Intelligence Agency, whose own historian documented decades of UFO cover story management, is absent. The Department of Energy, which holds the nuclear complex and the Restricted Data authority, is absent. The Defense Intelligence Agency, which AARO has confirmed holds the AAWSAP archive, is absent. NASA is absent. The Departments of the Army and Navy are absent. The Department of the Air Force was absent too until 28 August 2026, when it transferred thirty records as catalog series 656595571 -- the first military department to appear in the collection in its own right, and the service that ran Project SIGN, Project GRUDGE and Project Blue Book. AARO itself, whose Volume I lists partnering with the National Archives on locating UAP data as one of its four lines of effort, appears only through OSD.

The statutory architecture explains why. The Act imposes an obligation on agencies to identify their own records and to decide what is publicly releasable. There is no Review Board, no independent adjudicator of withholding, and no penalty for a null return. Independent tracking of the transfer deadline found that most agencies approached with records requests either produced nothing or did not respond at all. A statute that asks the holders of a secret to declare it, and provides no mechanism when they do not, produces exactly the collection that exists.

The 2026 development compounds rather than fixes this. A presidential directive in February 2026 launched a multiagency effort to identify, review, declassify and publicly release UAP records, with a centralised repository opened in May 2026 on the Department of War's own website and material issued in periodic tranches. The releases have included previously unseen material, including a Department of Energy report on a 2015 incident over the Pantex nuclear weapons plant. But the channel is executive, discretionary, and controlled by the departments whose compliance with the statutory channel was the problem. Jordan Flowers of the Disclosure Foundation made the point directly in February 2026, noting that Congress had already passed laws establishing a public UAP records collection at the National Archives and that the existing statutory framework gave the administration a path it was not using, while warning that classification nuances could produce misleadingly partial or incomplete disclosures.

The collection is therefore best understood on this board as an instrument that works and a compliance record that does not. The statute is the strongest UAP disclosure law ever enacted and it names the categories precisely. Record Group 615 is real, it is growing, and NARA is holding unredacted copies the public cannot see. Eight agencies have delivered, the eighth on 28 August 2026. The agencies at the centre of the argument still have not, and the Air Force transfer costs them their best remaining excuse: a military department has now done it.

### What it has denied

NARA has made no substantive claim about the phenomenon and issues no denials; it is a records custodian executing a statute. Its published position is procedural, that it receives records from agencies on a rolling basis and will add to Record Group 615 as they are transferred. The absence of denial is itself significant: NARA has never stated that any agency has certified it holds no UAP records, and it publishes no compliance register.

Roles the board assigns it: disclosure, custody, oversight.

### Open questions on this node

- NARA publishes no register of which agencies have certified compliance, which have made null returns, and which have not responded at all. Without it, the eight listed transmittals cannot be distinguished from a near total failure of compliance.
- The statutory transfer deadline of 30 September 2025 has passed. No enforcement action, referral or public accounting of non-compliance has been identified.
- NARA holds unredacted copies of redacted UAP records under the simultaneous transfer requirement. The volume of that unpublished holding is not disclosed anywhere.
- Whether records classified as Restricted Data under the Atomic Energy Act are treated as within or outside the transfer obligation has never been addressed by NARA guidance, which matters directly for DOE and the nuclear weapons complex.
- The relationship between the statutory Record Group 615 collection and the 2026 executive-branch release channel is undefined. It is not established whether material released through the executive channel is also being accessioned into RG 615, or whether it substitutes for it.
- The Senate-passed Review Board and eminent domain provisions have not been reintroduced and enacted. Whether any subsequent NDAA restores them is a live tracking item.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 37 of 1125, score 90). 13 connections, 2 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 7 official denials recorded against it. Axes C0/A1/D3.

## National Archives Record Group 615 - UAP Records Collection

National Archives Record Group 615 - UAP Records Collection is a institution on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2023-12-22; axes C0 A0 D3; 9 connections; in the custody band.

In one line: Statutory collection under FY2024 NDAA sections 1841-1843; transfer deadline 30 September 2025.

### What it is

Record Group 615, the Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena Records Collection at the U.S. National Archives, created by Sections 1841-1843 of the FY2024 National Defense Authorization Act (Public Law 118-31, div. A, title XVIII, subtitle C, December 22, 2023, 137 Stat. 699). It is the statutory destination for record copies of all Government, Government-provided or Government-funded records relating to unidentified anomalous phenomena, technologies of unknown origin, and non-human intelligence.

### Why it is implicated

RG 615 is the first time Congress has created a records collection whose statutory scope explicitly includes 'technologies of unknown origin, and non-human intelligence.' A legislature does not write a records-collection statute around a category it believes to be empty; the statutory text is itself a congressional finding that such records are believed to exist and to be held by federal agencies. But the enforcement architecture was hollowed. The Senate-origin design contemplated an independent review board to push postponed records toward disclosure; the enacted subtitle contains none. Postponement is decided by 'the original classification authority' -- the agency holding the record -- and postponed records physically remain with the originating body until NARA stands up a security programme. So the collection that was supposed to break the compartment is, for the withheld portion, still inside the compartment. What has actually arrived at RG 615 by 2026 is the readily releasable material from FAA, NRC, ODNI, OSD, NSA, State and FBI, joined on 28 August 2026 by the first military department to transfer in its own right: thirty unrestricted Air Force PDFs from 1945 to 1969.

### The longer account

Read the statute rather than the coverage.

Section 1841(a)(1)(A) required the Archivist to commence establishing the collection within 60 days of enactment. Section 1841(a)(1)(C) defines its contents with unusual reach: 'record copies of all Government, Government-provided, or Government-funded records relating to unidentified anomalous phenomena, technologies of unknown origin, and non-human intelligence (or equivalent subjects by any other name with the specific and sole exclusion of temporarily non-attributed objects).' Three features of that sentence are load-bearing. First, 'Government-funded' reaches contractor holdings. Second, 'or equivalent subjects by any other name' is an anti-euphemism clause -- a direct legislative anticipation that the material will be filed under cover terminology. Third, the sole carve-out is for temporarily non-attributed objects, i.e. drones and balloons, which is Congress saying the collection is not about the prosaic.

Section 1841(a)(1)(D) requires the Archivist to 'prepare and publish a subject guidebook and index to the Collection.' Section 1841(b) requires that transmitted records be available for inspection and copying at the National Archives within 30 days of transmission and digitally within not more than 180 days thereafter. Section 1841(e) assigns continuing oversight jurisdiction to Senate Homeland Security and Governmental Affairs, Armed Services and Intelligence, and to House Oversight and Accountability, Armed Services and Intelligence.

Section 1842 governs the agencies. Subsection (c)(1) gave each head of a Government office 300 days from December 22, 2023 to review, identify and organise every UAP record in its custody for disclosure and transmission. Subsection (a)(2)(A) is absolute: 'No unidentified anomalous phenomena record shall be destroyed, altered, or mutilated in any way.' Subsection (a)(2)(B) forbids withholding, redacting, postponing or reclassifying any UAP record already made available or disclosed to the public before enactment -- a retroactive lock on the existing public record, including documents like the Halt memo. Subsection (a)(2)(C) extends the same protection to records created by persons or entities outside the federal government.

Subsection (c)(2)(G) sets priorities, and the priority order is revealing. Agencies must give precedence first to records not already public; second to 'all records that most unambiguously and definitively pertain to unidentified anomalous phenomena, technologies of unknown origin, and non-human intelligence'; third to records already in FOIA litigation; fourth to earliest-provenance records. Congress wrote a triage rule that puts the most explicitly non-human material at the front of the queue.

Then the gate. Section 1843(a) permits postponement 'if the original classification authority makes a determination that there is clear and convincing evidence' that the threat to military defence, intelligence operations or foreign relations 'is of such gravity that it outweighs the public interest in disclosure' and that disclosure would reveal a currently protected agent, a currently used source or method, or 'any other matter currently relating to the military defense, intelligence operations, or conduct of foreign relations of the United States, the disclosure of which would demonstrably and substantially impair the national security.' Additional grounds cover the Privacy Act, unwarranted invasion of personal privacy, and confidentiality understandings with cooperating individuals.

'Clear and convincing evidence' is a demanding standard on paper. The problem is who applies it. The original classification authority is an official of the agency that owns the record and, on the allegations at issue, may be the same institutional actor accused of the concealment. There is no independent adjudicator in the enacted text. This is the JFK Records Act model with the Assassination Records Review Board removed.

Section 1842(g) supplies the only ongoing pressure. Postponed records must be reviewed periodically by the originating agency and the Archivist. Every continued postponement 'shall require an unclassified written description of the reason for such continued postponement relevant to these specific records,' provided to the Archivist and published in the Federal Register. And section 1842(g)(2)(D) sets the backstop: each record must be publicly disclosed in full not later than 25 years after its first creation, unless the President personally certifies that continued postponement is necessitated by an identifiable harm of such gravity that it outweighs the public interest. On a 25-year rule, anything created before 2001 is already past due absent a presidential certification.

Section 1841(d)(2) and section 1842(f) together create the physical bottleneck. NARA's National Security Program Office, with the Information Security Oversight Office, must establish a programme to secure the postponed and classified portion of the collection -- and until that programme exists, postponed records are 'held for reasons of security and preservation by the originating body.' The withheld material does not move.

What has actually landed is documented on NARA's RG 615 page: series from the Federal Aviation Administration (National Archives Identifier 493468575), the Nuclear Regulatory Commission (488808322), the Office of the Director of National Intelligence (493468579), the Office of the Secretary of Defense (493468580), the National Security Agency (580103959), the Department of State (608806625), and the Federal Bureau of Investigation (580705010) -- seven as of the page's May 22, 2026 review date -- and, added on August 28, 2026, the Department of the Air Force (656595571).

The Air Force series is the first transfer by a military department in its own right, and it is worth reading closely because it is the only evidence on the board of what compliance looks like when an agency with custody proximity actually does it. It is accession TR-0615-2026-0003: thirty PDF files, inclusive dates 25 October 1945 to 31 December 1969, access and use both Unrestricted, disposition authority DAA-GRS-2024-0002-0001, described from NARA file NAC_2026-08-27_23.116_DESC_0001.xml and indexed at 08:30 UTC on 28 August 2026. The thirty items carry contiguous UAP Record Identifiers 342UAP00001 to 342UAP00030 and each has one attached PDF; all thirty were fetched and returned PDF content.

Two things about the contents matter more than the count. The first is the declassification block printed on the documents themselves. Item 342UAP00005 carries 'Air Force Declassification Office / Authority: FY24 NDAA, now codified at 44 U.S.C. 2107 / Date: 5/05/2025 / Declassified in Full: X / Case Number: 342UAP00005'. The Air Force ran this as a declassification action under the UAP records statute, in May 2025, and used the UAP Record Identifier as its own case number -- so the identifier series is a declassification docket, and the gap between the May 2025 decision and the August 2026 accession is over fifteen months of something other than review.

The second is that the titles cannot be taken at face value. Item 342UAP00005 is catalogued as 'Estimate of the Situation, September 8, 1948', which reads as the Air Materiel Command estimate that Ruppelt described and that Vandenberg is said to have ordered destroyed. It is not. Opened, it is Headquarters Fifth Air Force, Office of the AC of S, A-2, APO 710, a 21-page periodic Far East theatre intelligence estimate covering 7 August to 4 September 1948, published for Fifth Air Force Division and Wing-Base Commanders, whose table of contents runs Japan, Korea, China, Philippines, Malaya. Eighteen of the thirty items are unit or installation histories of the same kind, in which any UAP content is a passage inside a monthly wing or division history rather than the subject of the document. Anyone citing this series from its catalog titles will misdescribe it. NARA issued AC Memo 04.2025 to agencies on the transfer of publicly releasable UAP records, and describes transfers as arriving on a rolling basis.

By 2026 a parallel executive channel has opened alongside the statutory one. Following a presidential order to disclose collected UAP records, the Presidential Unsealing and Reporting System for UAP Encounters -- the PURSUE Task Force -- released a fourth tranche of records on July 10, 2026, published at war.gov/ufo, and ODNI issued memorandum ES 2026-00818 on July 31, 2026 waiving nondisclosure agreements for anyone speaking to PURSUE or AARO. The analytically important question is whether PURSUE releases are being routed into RG 615 as section 1842(e) requires, or are accumulating in a separate executive-branch publication stream outside the Archivist's custody and outside the Federal Register accountability of section 1842(g)(2)(B).

### What it has denied

No agency has publicly asserted that it holds no UAP records; the posture is instead procedural. Agencies transfer 'publicly releasable' material under AC Memo 04.2025 while postponement determinations remain with each originating agency's own original classification authority under section 1843(a), and postponed records stay physically in agency custody under section 1842(f) pending a NARA security programme. The result is that the withholding is never articulated as a denial -- it is expressed as a classification determination made by the withholder.

Roles the board assigns it: custody, disclosure, concealment, oversight.

### Open questions on this node

- Whether the subject guidebook and index required by section 1841(a)(1)(D) has been prepared or published.
- How many records have been postponed under section 1843, by which agencies, and whether any unclassified written descriptions of continued postponement have been published in the Federal Register as section 1842(g)(2)(B) requires.
- Whether NARA's National Security Program Office has stood up the security programme required by section 1841(d)(2) -- until it does, no postponed record can leave agency custody.
- Whether any President has issued a certification under section 1842(g)(2)(D) to continue postponing records older than 25 years, and how many records are past that deadline without one.
- Whether any agency has certified completion of the section 1842(c)(1) 300-day review, and whether the Department of Energy, CIA, DIA, NRO, NGA, the Army and the Navy have transferred anything into RG 615. Half-closed on 28 August 2026: the Department of the Air Force transferred thirty records, so a military department has now complied and the rest cannot claim the obligation is unworkable.
- Whether contractor-held records are being captured under the 'Government-funded' clause of section 1841(a)(1)(C), and whether any contractor has been served a records demand.
- Whether PURSUE tranche releases at war.gov are also being transmitted to the Archivist for inclusion in RG 615, or are a parallel channel outside the statutory accountability structure.
- Why the Air Force records were declassified in May 2025 under the UAP records statute but not accessioned into RG 615 until August 2026, and whether the same interval applies to other agencies' completed reviews.
- Whether the thirty Air Force records are the whole of that department's return or a first tranche, and whether AFOSI, AFHRA Maxwell and the Air Force Declassification Office hold further UAP records already adjudicated releasable.
- Four of the 30 Air Force items carry another record's page identifier, and the series has no OCR layer. Whether NARA or the Air Force Declassification Office knows, and whether the same processing produced the stamps on other RG 615 transfers, is unasked.
- The Fourth Air Force 1947 file indexes at least 51 serials including FBI liaison and the Crisman/Dahl/crashed-B-25 report. None of that record is on this board, and the file is now unrestricted with a PDF attached.
- How many of the thirty Air Force items were declassified under the FY24 NDAA and how many were already declassified years earlier and merely moved. Item 342UAP00005 carries a May 2025 action; item 342UAP00014 carries a 1979/1982 one. Without that split, the size of the transfer says nothing about how much was actually being withheld.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 80 of 1125, score 69). 9 connections, 3 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C0/A0/D3.

## S.Amdt. 797 to S. 2226 (Schumer-Rounds UAP Disclosure Act amendment)

S.Amdt. 797 to S. 2226 (Schumer-Rounds UAP Disclosure Act amendment) is a document on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates 2023; axes C2 A2 D3; 9 connections; in the custody band.

In one line: disclosure-legislation.

### What it is

S.Amdt. 797 to S. 2226 was the Schumer-Rounds "Unidentified Anomalous Phenomena Disclosure Act of 2023," submitted in July 2023 and folded into the Senate-passed FY2024 National Defense Authorization Act on July 27, 2023 as Division G, sections 9001 through 9015. Modeled on the President John F. Kennedy Assassination Records Collection Act of 1992, it would have created a nine-member, Senate-confirmed UAP Records Review Board with subpoena-adjacent powers, a government-wide records collection at the National Archives, a controlled disclosure process, and a mandate that the federal government exercise eminent domain over recovered technologies of unknown origin and biological evidence of non-human intelligence held by private parties.

### Why it is implicated

This is the only time the United States Senate has passed statutory text asserting, as a matter of legislative fact, that private entities may be holding recovered non-human technology and biological material and that the government should take it. The bill defines "legacy program," "non-human intelligence," "technologies of unknown origin" and "controlling authority" in statutory language, and its Review Board qualification clause bars anyone with "any previous or current involvement with any legacy program or controlling authority relating to the collection, exploitation, or reverse engineering of technologies of unknown origin", a conflict-of-interest screen that presupposes the existence of the thing being screened against. Senators Schumer, Rounds, Rubio, Gillibrand, Young and Heinrich, several of them sitting members of the Intelligence and Armed Services Committees, wrote that clause. The Office of the Under Secretary of Defense for Intelligence and Security then submitted a thirty-three page line-by-line rewrite to conferees in November 2023 stating that the Department "strongly urges consideration" of its revisions, and the Department's core objectives prevailed. The gap between what the Senate passed and what the President signed is the single cleanest measurement of institutional resistance available on this board.

### The longer account

The UAP Disclosure Act was unveiled on 14 July 2023 with Senate Majority Leader Chuck Schumer as prime sponsor and Senator Mike Rounds as lead co-sponsor, with Rubio, Gillibrand, Young and Heinrich joining. It was added to the FY2024 NDAA in an agreed bloc of amendments without floor debate or a separate vote, and the Senate passed the NDAA including it on 27 July 2023.

The findings section is short and remarkably direct. Congress would have declared that all federal records concerning UAP "should carry a presumption of immediate disclosure"; that "Legislation is necessary to create an enforceable, independent, and accountable process for the public disclosure of such records"; and that "Legislation is necessary because credible evidence and testimony indicates that Federal Government unidentified anomalous phenomena records exist that have not been declassified or subject to mandatory declassification review as set forth in Executive Order 13526."

The definitions are where the bill does its most consequential work, because definitions survive in the mind even when the operative provisions die. "Legacy program" is defined as "all Federal, State, and local government, commercial industry, academic, and private sector endeavors to collect, exploit, or reverse engineer technologies of unknown origin or examine biological evidence of living or deceased non-human intelligence that pre-dates the date of the enactment of this Act." "Non-human intelligence" is "any sentient intelligent non-human lifeform regardless of nature or ultimate origin that may be presumed responsible for unidentified anomalous phenomena or of which the Federal Government has become aware." "Technologies of unknown origin" is a lawyer's inventory: "any materials or meta-materials, ejecta, crash debris, mechanisms, machinery, equipment, assemblies or sub-assemblies, engineering models or processes, damaged or intact aerospace vehicles, and damaged or intact ocean-surface and undersea craft associated with unidentified anomalous phenomena or incorporating science and technology that lacks prosaic attribution or known means of human manufacture." And "controlling authority" reaches "any Federal, State, or local government department, office, agency, committee, commission, commercial company, academic institution, or private sector entity in physical possession of technologies of unknown origin or biological evidence of non-human intelligence." Whoever drafted that list was not speculating about the abstract; they were describing categories of custody.

The Review Board would have been nine citizens appointed by the President with the advice and consent of the Senate, nominated within ninety days after considering persons recommended by the four congressional leaders, the Secretary of Defense, the National Academy of Sciences, the American Historical Association, and, a genuinely unusual inclusion in a statute, "the UAP Disclosure Foundation." Nominees had to include at least one current or former national security official, one current or former foreign service official, one scientist or engineer, one economist, one professional historian and one sociologist. They would have been "granted the necessary security clearances and accesses, including any and all relevant Presidential, departmental, and agency special access programs, in an accelerated manner." Confirmation ran through Senate Homeland Security and Governmental Affairs on compressed clocks: hearings within thirty days, committee vote within fourteen, floor vote within fourteen.

The eminent domain provision, section 9010(a), read: "The Federal Government shall exercise eminent domain over any and all recovered technologies of unknown origin and biological evidence of non-human intelligence that may be controlled by private persons or entities in the interests of the public good." Subsection (b) required that any such material "should it exist, shall be made available to the Review Board for personal examination and subsequent disclosure determination." Subsection (c) tasked the Board with deciding "whether the material examined constitutes technologies of unknown origin or biological evidence of non-human intelligence beyond a reasonable doubt." Subsection (d) gave the Board access to all testimony from UAP witnesses, close observers, "and legacy program personnel and whistleblowers" in the government's possession, and subsection (e) directed it to solicit more, extending the whistleblower protections of section 1673(b) of the FY2023 NDAA, 50 U.S.C. 3373b(b).

Other operative provisions: government offices had 300 days to review, identify and organize their UAP records; the Board was to be treated as an agency of the United States for purposes of 18 U.S.C. 6001, the witness immunity statute; the Board would report to congressional leadership, Senate Homeland Security and Governmental Affairs, and House Oversight; and $20,000,000 was authorized for fiscal year 2024, with the President permitted to use available discretionary sums in the interim.

Then the Pentagon wrote back. In November 2023, during end-stage conference negotiations, the Office of the Under Secretary of Defense for Intelligence and Security transmitted to congressional negotiators a thirty-three page line-by-line rewrite of the Senate-passed text, presented as the Department's "informal views" but stating that the Department "strongly urges consideration" of the changes and that the position was arrived at "after conferring with NARA/ISOO." The document was first published by Douglas Dean Johnson in July 2024. Its treatment of section 9010 is the tell: OUSD(I&S) proposed replacing the mandatory "The Federal Government shall exercise eminent domain over" with "It is the sense of Congress that the Federal Government should exercise eminent domain appropriately over", while leaving the object of the sentence, "any and all recovered technologies of unknown origin and biological evidence of non-human intelligence that may be controlled by private persons or entities," untouched. Asked why, former AARO director Sean Kirkpatrick replied that "a compromise was proposed to allow for the exercising of eminent domain under AARO's authority to underscore that AARO could compel disclosure of anything, should anything exist. Since we know nothing exists, we didn't feel it made our job harder."

Kirkpatrick was blunter elsewhere. On 17 July 2024 he told interviewer Marik von Rennenkampff, "We convinced Congress last year not to go down that road." The Review Board, its staff, the eminent domain authority and the controlled disclosure process were all deleted in conference. Schumer, on the Senate floor on 13 December 2023, called it "really an outrage."

### What it has denied

The Department of Defense never denied the bill's premises in public. It filed a redline. The 'informal views' rewrite objected to structure, duplication with AARO's existing historical-report mandate, and burden on industry, Kirkpatrick's stated reasoning was that the Review Board would duplicate AARO and that 'we found no evidence of any of these allegations. None.' The Department nonetheless declined to strike the eminent domain object, which is the more interesting fact.

Roles the board assigns it: disclosure, oversight, custody.

### Open questions on this node

- Whether the text as filed in S.Amdt. 797 differs in any material respect from the text released by the Majority Leader's office in July 2023; congress.gov is the authoritative version and section numbering in the released draft is left blank.
- Who drafted the definitions of 'legacy program,' 'controlling authority' and 'technologies of unknown origin,' and on what factual briefing those categories were built.
- What 'the UAP Disclosure Foundation' is, why a private entity was written into a statutory nomination process, and who proposed it.
- Which conferees specifically insisted on removing the Review Board and eminent domain provisions; Douglas Johnson attributes the outcome to a small group of senior Republican members but names none in the public record.
- Whether the Senate ever received a classified justification from DoD for opposing the bill, distinct from the unclassified 'informal views' redline.
- Whether NARA and ISOO, whom OUSD(I&S) said it consulted, supported the rewrite or merely commented on records-management feasibility.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 259 of 1125, score 41). 9 connections, 0 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C2/A2/D3.

## Rep. Burlison records demand to MITRE, 22 May 2026

Rep. Burlison records demand to MITRE, 22 May 2026 is a document on the board; board tier A, named in sworn testimony or primary documents as directly inside the programme; dates 2026; axes C2 A2 D3; 7 connections; in the custody band.

In one line: 10-page letter; 40+ production requests; records back to 1930.

### What it is

A ten-page package of legislative interrogatories and production requests dated 22 May 2026, sent by Rep. Eric Burlison (R-MO-07) to Dr. Mark Peters, President and Chief Executive Officer, and Christopher Land, Senior Vice President and General Counsel, of The MITRE Corporation at 7525 Colshire Drive, McLean, Virginia. Copied to Task Force Chairwoman Anna Paulina Luna, Oversight Chairman James Comer, and Michael D. Thomas, Director of the Information Security Oversight Office at NARA. The enclosure runs eleven numbered sections and more than forty specific requests, with a relevant time period beginning 1 January 1930.

### Why it is implicated

This is the first congressional instrument that treats a federally funded research and development center as a potential custodian of the legacy program's paper. It does not ask MITRE whether UAP are real. It asks MITRE which Special Access Programs, Controlled Access Programs, Alternative Compensatory Control Measures, Managed-Need-To-Know codewords, DOE Restricted Data compartments and SIGMA categories it participated in touching UAP subject matter, and for each one, 'whether the program was reported to the appropriate congressional committees.' It asks for any classification guide, nondisclosure agreement or sponsor communication that instructed MITRE personnel to avoid the words UAP, UFO, USO, crash retrieval, reverse engineering, technology of unknown origin or non-human intelligence in their records. It asks for funding routed through 'a cover program' whose 'public description differed materially from the responsive work performed,' and for funding MITRE understood to be 'hidden from, not briefed to, or not readily visible to ordinary sponsor budget channels.' And it names twelve companies by name. The letter is a map of the concealment hypothesis rendered as a document request.

### The longer account

Burlison's MITRE package is the moment the congressional inquiry stopped asking the Pentagon and started asking the people who hold the Pentagon's files.

The premise is stated in the cover letter's second paragraph. MITRE operates multiple FFRDCs, and 'By design, FFRDCs often receive special access to government facilities, data, sponsor requirements, and sensitive technical information that ordinary private contractors may not receive. This special public-trust role makes records stewardship, sponsor accountability, and congressional visibility especially important.' The statutory hook is sections 1841-1843 of the FY2024 NDAA and NARA's Record Group 615: if government-funded UAP records sit in a contractor's archive, the statute reaches them, and MITRE's status as a nonprofit operator does not create a safe harbor. DefenseScoop reported that FFRDCs are largely shielded from public disclosure by FOIA exemptions, and that some officials have warned this lets them hold government information the public has a right to see.

The scope is deliberately enormous. 'Unless otherwise specified, the relevant time period is January 1, 1930, to the present.' MITRE was incorporated in 1958; the 1930 start date is a trap for inherited archives, telling MITRE that if it contends the request is overbroad for a legacy period it must 'identify the earliest date for which MITRE can search reliably, the reason older records cannot be searched, and the custodian or sponsor most likely to hold the older records.' The definition of 'UAP-related' extends to any record 'described, coded, compartmented, or euphemized as involving' UAP, UFO, USO, transmedium craft, advanced aerospace threat, technology of unknown origin, non-human intelligence, legacy crash retrieval, crash retrieval, reverse engineering, recovered vehicle, recovered material, biological material, 'or equivalent terms.' Euphemism is named as a category of evidence.

The deadlines are tight and sequenced. Within five business days, designate a vice-president-level officer to coordinate. Within ten business days, issue and confirm a written preservation hold covering 'restricted systems, air-gapped environments, classified repositories, personal workspaces used for official business, archived systems, email, collaboration platforms, backup media, contractor/subcontractor systems, and offsite storage.' Within fourteen days, contact the office to coordinate a classified briefing for Task Force Members and cleared Committee staff at 'the appropriate TS/SCI/SAP level.' Within thirty days, written answers plus a records-location index naming repositories, custodians, contract vehicles, sponsors, classification authorities and record schedules. Within forty-five days, production of all unclassified responsive records in native electronic format with complete metadata, plus a segregated classified annex and a privilege and classification log. The letter warns that 'False statements, concealment, alteration, destruction, or obstruction in connection with this inquiry may implicate federal law, including but not limited to 18 U.S.C. §§ 1001, 1505, 1512, 1519, and 2071.'

The eleven enclosure sections read as a taxonomy of how a program stays hidden. Section I demands an accounting of records destroyed, degaussed, sanitized, reclassified, moved to a restricted system or 'placed under sponsor control,' with the authority and the responsible personnel named. Section II demands identification of every contract, IDIQ, GWAC, BPA, OTA and CRADA under which MITRE touched UAP information, and every individual involved with 'their specific functions and duties', and names AARO, the UAP Task Force, AATIP, AAWSAP, DIA, CIA, NSA, NRO, NGA, ONI, NRL, DARPA, IARPA and DOE laboratories, 'or any successor, predecessor, cover, compartment, or renamed activity.' Section II.6 asks whether MITRE 'provided cover' for another entity's UAP work.

Section III is the compartmentation audit already described. Section IV asks what MITRE has and has not sent to NARA under the UAP Records Collection. Section V asks for sensor work across radar, optical, infrared, acoustic and undersea domains, expressly including 'SOSUS/IUSS-related' systems and 'unidentified submerged objects.' Section VI asks about recovered anomalous materials, isotope ratios, chain of custody, metamaterials, propulsion and 'cognitive interface systems', and, in VI.4, about 'biological material, biological samples, human physiological effects, medical monitoring, occupational exposure, anomalous injury, cognitive or neurological effects, or environmental hazards associated with UAP-related materials, objects, fields, occupants, or events,' with the careful caveat that the request 'seeks records and safety documentation only and does not presume the validity of any particular biological claim.' The word 'occupants' is doing a great deal of work in a congressional document.

Section VII asks for SCIFs, SAPFs, vaults and 'material-containment work.' Section VIII asks for internal disclosures, retaliation, and nondisclosure instruments 'beyond standard government-secrecy obligations' that restricted personnel from reporting to Congress, IGs, AARO, NARA or law enforcement. Section IX names Lockheed Martin, Northrop Grumman, Raytheon/RRTX, Boeing, General Dynamics, SAIC, Leidos, Battelle, The Aerospace Corporation, Booz Allen Hamilton, BAE Systems and Bigelow Aerospace or its successors. Section X goes at the money. Section XI demands a certified response signed by a responsible MITRE officer and counsel.

MITRE's answer, given to DefenseScoop on 27 May 2026, was custodial rather than responsive: 'If any relevant material is found, we will coordinate with the federal agencies responsible for the work to determine how to best provide any assets.' That is a statement that anything found goes back to the sponsor, not to Congress. A member of Burlison's staff told DefenseScoop the letter was informed by 'advice from many whistleblowers, disclosure advocacy organizations, and those working at FFRDCs.'

The MITRE package was not the first shot. On 7 May 2026 Burlison had written to MIT Lincoln Laboratory Director Melissa G. Choi and counsel David A. Suski about a single object: a reel-to-reel recording catalogued as 'AF-ATIC-FILM, 03/52' from the Beacon Hill Study, labeled 'flying saucer talk,' with Edward J. Ruppelt, then head of Project Blue Book, listed as the briefer. That letter demanded the laboratory determine whether the recording is extant, preserve it, digitize it to preservation grade, and transmit it with metadata to NARA for Record Group 615. It also instructed the laboratory to coordinate with the cognizant sponsor, the original classification authority, ISOO and the Public Interest Declassification Board if an active Security Classification Guide still applies to a 1952 tape.

### What it has denied

MITRE has not denied holding responsive records. Its only public statement, that if relevant material is found it will 'coordinate with the federal agencies responsible for the work to determine how to best provide any assets', asserts a custodial rather than a disclosing posture, positioning the sponsor agency, not Congress, as the recipient. That is precisely the routing Burlison's letter anticipated and attempted to foreclose by demanding that MITRE identify the sponsor, the classification authority and 'the shortest practical path to lawful disclosure to Congress and transfer to NARA.'

Roles the board assigns it: disclosure, oversight, custody, concealment.

### Open questions on this node

- Did MITRE meet the 5-, 10-, 14-, 30- and 45-day deadlines, and has any responsive production, records-location index or certification been delivered? Nothing is public as of August 2026.
- Did the classified briefing at TS/SCI/SAP level for Task Force Members and cleared Committee staff ever occur?
- The letter's paragraph 6 of the MIT Lincoln Laboratory response request renders the item as 'AF-ATIC-FLIM, 03/52' while the opening paragraph reads 'AF-ATIC-FILM, 03/52', which string appears in the actual finding aid, and does the transposition affect the search?
- Which federal sponsor commissioned any MITRE UAP-related work, and would that sponsor be the entity MITRE says it will 'coordinate with' before providing assets?
- Has any of the twelve named companies in Section IX.2 received a parallel congressional demand?
- Did MITRE issue the written preservation hold, and does it cover air-gapped and sponsor-controlled repositories as demanded?
- The Burlison letter's staff contact block is redacted in the published version while the MIT Lincoln Laboratory letter names Aaron Jenks; is the same staffer handling both?
- Has Burlison or the Task Force moved toward a subpoena, which the cover letter expressly reserves by reference to the Committee's authority?

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded A on a global pass (rank 67 of 1125, score 73). 10 connections, 1 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 1 official denial recorded against it. Axes C2/A2/D3.
