# NO DATE: no start date recorded

## United States Navy

United States Navy is a institution on the board; board tier B, documented channel and capability, programme role indicated but not yet nailed to a primary source; dates no dates recorded; axes C0 A0 D0; 13 connections; in the custody band.

In one line: Military service.

### What it is

The maritime service of the Department of Defense, organised under a civilian Secretary of the Navy with the Chief of Naval Operations as senior uniformed officer, and stating its own purpose as deploying combat-ready sailors, ships, aircraft and submarines worldwide without dependence on foreign land bases. The board holds it through its component parts rather than as a monolith: the Office of Naval Research, placed inside the Office of the Secretary of the Navy by Public Law 79-588 on 1 August 1946; the Deep Submergence Systems Project, assigned by the Secretary to the Special Projects Office after the review group reported in February 1964; the Naval Criminal Investigative Service, a Department of the Navy organisation whose head's title is fixed in the Navy's own codified regulations at 32 CFR part 701; and James V. Forrestal, Secretary of the Navy until September 1947.

### Why it is implicated

The Navy generated the modern evidentiary core of the problem this board maps, and then became the office that handled it. The encounters that anchor the contemporary record are Navy carrier aviation events: off southern California in November 2004 and off the eastern seaboard in 2014-2015, recorded through targeting pods and radar and filed by aircrew through operational channels. On 27 April 2020 the Department authorised release of three unclassified Navy videos and said why, and on 4 August 2020 the Deputy Secretary made the Department of the Navy the lead for the UAP Task Force. It is also an assignee of record: the Pais inertial mass reduction device patent is assigned to the United States of America as represented by the Secretary of the Navy. Acknowledgement, custody of the reporting channel and ownership of the exotic-propulsion paperwork are all in the same service.

### The longer account

The Navy's position on this board is unusual because it is the one institution here that has conceded the evidentiary question in public and declined the interpretive one in the same sentence. The April 2020 release states that the Department was acting 'in order to clear up any misconceptions by the public on whether or not the footage that has been circulating was real, or whether or not there is more to the videos', and in the same document holds that 'The aerial phenomena observed in the videos remain characterized as unidentified.' That is a hinge: material that had circulated as leak became acknowledged official product, and the acknowledgement carried no characterisation with it. The board's own record notes the reading that this was damage control rather than disclosure, and grades that reading Tier 3, because it is an inference from the wording of the release and nothing more.

What gives the service structural weight rather than merely evidentiary weight is what sits under it. The Office of Naval Research was created inside the Office of the Secretary of the Navy with the provision that its orders be considered as emanating from the Secretary and have full force and effect as such. The Deep Submergence Systems Project was assigned by the Secretary to the Special Projects Office and by 1967 was a separate project inside Naval Material Command with its own project designator. The Pais patent names the Secretary of the Navy as the assignee. These are the ordinary mechanics of a service that can commission research, place it under a project office and hold title to what comes out, and they are the mechanics that any custody account of anomalous material would have to run through.

The honest statement of this node is that the board has the outputs and not the interior. Its degree here is nine, its Tier 1 claims number two, and its own research record is a known stub filled in from public description. The Navy is on the map because everything downstream of the 2004 and 2015 encounters passes through it, not because any opened record shows what it did with them.

### What it has denied

The board records no denial link on this node in either direction. The posture the record does establish is confirmation without characterisation: the Department confirmed the videos were genuine Navy product and stated in the same release that what they show remains unidentified. The board's own reading of that as damage control rather than disclosure is carried as a Tier 3 inference from the release's wording, and is not treated as a denial by anyone.

Roles the board assigns it: disclosure, custody, research.

### Open questions on this node

- This node's own research record is a filled stub. It carries four claims, two of them Tier 1, and the board's node letters for consequence, access and disclosure are all zero. A service of this size with this position in the record should not be thinner than its own subordinate offices.
- The Navy's aviation safety and hazard reporting systems are the channel every aircrew report entered, and no record of those filings has been opened here. The board asserts the channel exists and holds none of its contents.
- Shore facilities under Navy control are repeatedly named in recovery and material-storage allegations elsewhere on this board. Nothing opened for this node names a facility, a programme or a date, and no claim here rests on those allegations.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded B on a global pass (rank 517 of 1125, score 30). 13 connections, 0 of them custody/funding/programmatic, 1 official denial recorded against it. Axes C0/A0/D0.

### The claims on the record

- **Tier 1 documented.** On 27 April 2020 the Department of Defense authorised the release of three unclassified Navy videos - one taken in November 2004 and two in January 2015 - stating it was releasing them 'in order to clear up any misconceptions by the public on whether or not the footage that has been circulating was real, or whether or not there is more to the videos'. Source: DoD release, 27 April 2020
- **Tier 1 documented.** On 27 April 2020 the Department of Defense authorised the release of three unclassified Navy videos, one taken in November 2004 and two in January 2015, stating it was releasing them 'in order to clear up any misconceptions by the public on whether or not the footage that has been circulating was real, or whether or not there is more to the videos', and that 'The aerial phenomena observed in the videos remain characterized as unidentified.' Source: Statement by the Department of Defense on the Release of Historical Navy Videos, 27 April 2020, https://www.war.gov/News/Releases/Release/Article/2165713/statement-by-the-department-of-defense-on-the-release-of-historical-navy-videos/

## Wayne State University

Wayne State University is a institution on the board; board tier C, contextual, real and relevant, no direct programme linkage on the record yet; dates no dates recorded; axes C0 A0 D0; 7 connections; in the custody band.

In one line: Academic.

### What it is

Wayne State University is a public research university in midtown Detroit whose School of Medicine operates jointly with the Detroit Medical Center, a large urban academic hospital system. Inside that medical school sat a small, defense- and intelligence-facing neuroscience unit called the Emergent Technology Research Division, built around functional neuroimaging and cognitive-workload measurement. That division, not the university's public research portfolio, is the reason Wayne State appears on this board.

### Why it is implicated

Four of the thirty-eight Defense Intelligence Reference Documents commissioned under the DIA's Advanced Aerospace Weapon System Applications program are attributed on the government-derived DIRD list to two Wayne State men: Christopher C. "Kit" Green and Richard J. Genik II. Green is a career CIA analyst (1969-1985) who came to Wayne State as executive director for emergent technology research for the School of Medicine and Detroit Medical Center, and then as assistant dean; Genik directed the Emergent Technology Research Division that Green's role had seeded, and the two had already co-authored on "cognitive avionics" for spaceflight crews in 2005. Green's DIRD is the human-effects paper, the single AAWSAP deliverable about injured bodies rather than propulsion, and it states outright that clinical diagnosis of injured witnesses can be used to reverse-engineer the craft that hurt them. That makes a named academic medical centre in Detroit a node in the custody chain for injury data on people harmed by anomalous craft, and it makes the university's silence about that work the relevant fact. Wayne State has never issued any public acknowledgement of the DIA contract deliverables written by its faculty, and Green himself has since recast the entire enterprise as private consulting done outside any institutional review structure.

### The longer account

The program reached into universities, and Wayne State is where it reached into a hospital.

The entry point is Christopher C. Green. His career is the point: senior division analyst and assistant national intelligence officer for science and technology at CIA from 1969 to 1985, then General Motors, then Wayne State. The National Research Council's own 2008 biography of him, published while he chaired a committee on military and intelligence methodology for emergent neurophysiological research, records that immediately prior to becoming assistant dean he was "executive director for emergent technology research for the SOM/DMC" and that he held clinical appointments in the Departments of Diagnostic Radiology and of Psychiatry and Behavioral Neurosciences of the School of Medicine and the Detroit Medical Center. Note what that means. A former CIA science-and-technology officer was running the emergent-technology research function of a large American medical school, with hospital-side appointments in the two departments that give access to brain imaging and to psychiatric case material.

By 2009 the unit had a name and a successor. Wayne State's own School of Medicine news office described Richard J. Genik II as "director of the Emergent Technology Research Division and assistant professor of the Department of Psychiatry and Behavioral Neurosciences," announcing that the U.S. Army had picked him as one of fifteen experts to direct two decades of Army neuroscience investment, and that he briefed the Pentagon, the Board of Army Science and Technology, the Army Research Lab and Training and Doctrine Command. The university publicised that. It has never publicised what came next.

What came next was AAWSAP. The DIRD list attributes to Genik three papers and to Green one. Genik's three are all verifiable in the released record: "Technological Approaches to Controlling External Devices in the Absence of Limb-Operated Interfaces" (DIA-08-1003-012, 23 March 2010), "Cognitive Limits on Simultaneous Control of Multiple Unmanned Spacecraft" (DIA-08-1101-001, 15 December 2010, ICOD 8 September 2010, issued in the "Defense Futures" series), and a quantum-computing paper dated 10 December 2010. Read together they are not miscellaneous: brain-machine control of external devices, cognitive ceilings on piloting multiple craft at once, and molecular computing. That is a specification sheet for operating something you do not fly with your hands.

Green's contribution is the one that matters most. "Anomalous Acute and Subacute Field Effects on Human Biological Tissues," dated 11 March 2010 with an information cut-off of 1 December 2009, produced under the FY 2009 AAWSA program in the "Acquisition Threat Support" series, opens with three antenna engineers who suffered erythema, fever, hair loss, blood dyscrasia and, in one case, "signs of malignant transformations" after a near-field exposure centred at about 785 MHz. Its summary is the load-bearing sentence in the whole DIRD corpus: the paper "relates, summarizes, and analyzes evidence of unintended injury to human observers by anomalous advanced aerospace systems," and argues "that the subsequent work can inform (e.g., reverse engineer), through clinical diagnoses, certain physical characteristics of possible future advanced aerospace systems from unknown provenance." Later it states: "It is our contention that characteristics of the fields or mechanisms associated with close encounters with anomalous craft can even sometimes carefully be gleaned from archival records where effects on human physiology have been carefully reported." Its appendices draw on the Schuessler catalog of UFO-related human physiological effects and on Vallee's close-encounter typology. The internal references cite "Green C. 2009 & 1983 (Unpublished Personal Archives)" and "Green, CC 2001, 2009 Unpublished HIPPA files", a defence intelligence reference document resting, in part, on one physician's private patient archive.

The custody problem is exactly there. The released PDFs redact every author name under FOIA exemption (b)(6); the attribution to Wayne State comes from the DIRD list, not from the documents. Green, interviewed in April 2022, said he was a consultant to AAWSAP facilitated by BAASS, that there were no control groups and no institutional review board because he was doing "case studies" and not research, that a client gave him a grant he would not name, and that DIA reviewers objected to his use of the word UFO and had him change the wording. So the human-effects paper commissioned by a defence intelligence agency was written by a Wayne State assistant dean, drew on patient material he holds personally, passed through no university IRB, and was scrubbed of the word that described what it was about. The institution's name is on the government's list; the institution's oversight machinery never touched the work; and the institution has said nothing since. That is not an accident of paperwork. It is how the program used a university: for the credential and the clinical access, not for the accountability.

### What it has denied

Wayne State University has issued no public acknowledgement of the DIA/AAWSAP deliverables written by its School of Medicine personnel; no press release, statement or research disclosure naming the contract has been located, in contrast to the university's 2009 publicity for Genik's Army neuroscience work. Green's own posture is displacement rather than denial: he characterises the human-effects paper as private consulting and personal case studies conducted outside any university review structure, and declines to name the funder. The DIA's standing line that DIRDs are contractor-produced theoretical papers that do not represent agency positions performs the same function for the government side.

Roles the board assigns it: research, custody, concealment.

### Open questions on this node

- No evidence was found that Green ever held a named or endowed chair at Wayne State. The verifiable titles are assistant dean, adjunct/clinical professorial appointments in Diagnostic Radiology and Psychiatry and Behavioral Neurosciences, and earlier executive director for emergent technology research. Any claim of a 'chair' for anomalous-exposure research should be treated as unsupported until a Wayne State appointment letter or catalogue entry surfaces.
- The exact start and end dates of Green's Wayne State appointments are not established in the sourced record; the 2008 NRC biography is the firm anchor, and his status in 2009-2010 when the DIRD was written is inferred rather than documented.
- Whether any part of the AAWSAP work flowed through Wayne State accounts, contracts, facilities or the Emergent Technology Research Division's budget is unresolved. Green describes personal consulting; no subcontract, subaward or effort-reporting document has surfaced either way.
- Whether Genik's three DIRDs were written under university affiliation or, like Green's, as private consulting is not established. He is attributed to Wayne State on the DIRD list, but no contract instrument has been located.
- The document number on the human-effects DIRD is illegible in the released scan, so the paper is cited here by title and date only. The quantum-computing DIRD's number OCRs ambiguously and is likewise cited by date.
- Whether Wayne State's institutional review board ever reviewed, or was ever asked to review, any of the clinical or imaging work Green describes conducting on 'experiencers' in later years.
- The identity of 'Morris' in the DIRD's Appendix B 'Green/Morris' ICD-coding exercise, and whether that collaborator was a Wayne State colleague.
- Whether the Emergent Technology Research Division still exists at Wayne State, when it was wound down or renamed, and where its records went.
- Whether Wayne State has ever received a FOIA, records request or press inquiry about the DIA work, and what it said in response.

### Why it holds its tier

Re-graded C on a global pass (rank 965 of 1125, score 15). 7 connections, 0 of them custody/funding/programmatic. Axes C0/A0/D0.

- Wayne State University comes in from **Technological Approaches to Controlling External Devices in the Absence of Limb-Operated Interfaces** (document, board tier B), a institutional link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: author's affiliation of record. Wayne State is the DIA-listed affiliation for four DIRDs: Genik's three human-factors papers and Kit Green's "Field Effects on Biological Tissues." That makes it the single most productive institution in the DIRD set after EarthTech International, and the locus of the programme's human-effects line of inquiry. Sources: Released DIRD, 'Technological Approaches to Controlling External Devices in the Absenc', FOIA copy, cover page read 22 August 2026, https://documents2.theblackvault.com/documents/dia/AAWSAP-DIRDs/DIRD_12-DIRD_Technological_Approaches_to_Controlling_External_Devices.pdf, dated 2026-08-22; DIA FOIA Electronic Reading Room entry for this reference document, https://www.dia.mil/FOIA/FOIA-Electronic-Reading-Room/FileId/237621/, dated 2026-08-22; DIA letter of 9 January 2018 to the Senate Armed Services Committee, listing the 38 AAWSAP/AATIP reference products and their authors and affiliations, https://fas.org/publication/aatip-list/, dated 2026-08-23; https://ufos-scientificresearch.blogspot.com/2019/01/back-to-those-38-defense-intelligence.html, https://ufos-scientificresearch.blogspot.com/2019/01/back-to-those-38-defense-intelligence.html; https://today.wayne.edu/medicine/news/2009/07/13/richard-genik-phd-25514, https://today.wayne.edu/medicine/news/2009/07/13/richard-genik-phd-25514
- Wayne State University comes in from **Quantum Computing and Utilizing Organic Molecules in Automation Technology** (document, board tier C), a institutional link at Tier 1 documented. The link reads: author's institutional affiliation at time of writing. Wayne State University is the affiliation recorded against this title on the DIA congressional list. Wayne State appears more than once on the list: it is also the affiliation given for Kit Green's human-tissue field-effects deliverable, so both the human-effects and human-interface strands of the AAWSAP deliverable set run through the same medical school. Sources: Released DIRD, 'Quantum Computing and Utilizing Organic Molecules in Automation Techno', FOIA copy, cover page read 22 August 2026, https://documents2.theblackvault.com/documents/dia/AAWSAP-DIRDs/DIRD_35-DIRD_Quantum_Computing_and_Utilizing_Organic_Molecules_in_Automation_Technology.pdf, dated 2026-08-22; DIA FOIA Electronic Reading Room entry for this reference document, https://www.dia.mil/FOIA/FOIA-Electronic-Reading-Room/FileId/237618/, dated 2026-08-22; DIA letter of 9 January 2018 to the Senate Armed Services Committee, listing the 38 AAWSAP/AATIP reference products and their authors and affiliations, https://fas.org/publication/aatip-list/, dated 2026-08-23; https://irp.fas.org/dia/aatip-list.pdf, https://irp.fas.org/dia/aatip-list.pdf; https://ufos-scientificresearch.blogspot.com/2018/08/an-analysis-of-that-list-of-aawsapaatip.html, https://ufos-scientificresearch.blogspot.com/2018/08/an-analysis-of-that-list-of-aawsapaatip.html; https://today.wayne.edu/medicine/news/2009/07/13/richard-genik-phd-25514, https://today.wayne.edu/medicine/news/2009/07/13/richard-genik-phd-25514
- Wayne State University runs out to **The 38 Defense Intelligence Reference Documents (DIRDs)** (document, board tier A), a research link at Tier 2 named person on the record. The link reads: Wayne State clinician authored the AAWSAP human-effects reference document. Green, while a Wayne State professor, wrote the Defense Intelligence Reference Document 'Anomalous Acute and Subacute Field Effects on Human and Biological Tissues' for the DIA-funded AAWSAP contract; DIA released it with the other DIRDs in 2022. Tier 2 because the work was Green's, commissioned through BAASS, not a Wayne State institutional contract.
